1 EXECUTIVE SUMMARY

This report presents the findings of a year-long pre-FEED study into the feasibility of a CO2 intermodal hub
in Port Tampa Bay which would collect, store, and liquify captured CO2 from major emitters across the
State of Florida for marine transport out of the state. The proposed hub is called the Tampa Regional
Intermodal Carbon Hub (T-RICH). The study was funded by the National Energy Technology Laboratory
(NETL) of the U.S. Department of Energy under Award No. DE-FE0032464. The study was completed on
time and under budget.
The main objective of the project was to determine if it would be technically feasible to build an
intermodal CO2 hub on an available parcel of land in Port Tampa Bay for export to Class VI sequestration
wells or for enhanced oil recovery in Louisiana or Texas on specialized liquified CO2 carrying ships. The
secondary objective was to determine the cost of building such a hub to determine if it is economically
and commercially viable. The goal for hub throughput was initially set at 2 million tons per annum (mtpa).
For reference, the average individual CO2 emissions of electricity generating plants within 30 miles of the
hub are between 2 and 3 mtpa, and the combined emissions from the Greater Tampa region exceed 30
mtpa.
The principal findings of the study are:
 1. A 2mtpa CO2 hub will require approximately 8.5 acres of land area. This is a little more than half
 of the available parcel of land chosen for the study in Port Tampa Bay. There are several other
 available parcels in the port that may also be suitable.
 2. With a total area of almost 16 acres, the chosen parcel could be expanded upon to achieve a total
 throughput of 8 mtpa, or four times the initial throughput goal.
 3. Due to Tampa’s relatively warm climate and local cooling water restrictions, the T-RICH hub
 requires air cooling and is therefore expected to consume more power than a hub with equivalent
 throughput in cooler climates or where water cooling is feasible.
 4. Based on the AACE Class IV cost estimate performed, the hub is expected to have a capital cost of
 approximately $774 million (margin of error 30%).
 5. There are no known technical challenges that pose a significant threat to the hub being successful
 in helping the United States meet its emissions-reductions goals. Technically, the hub is fully
 feasible, being similar to operating terminal hubs in Europe. Entr, who performed most of the
 preliminary engineering for this study, is heavily involved in successful European hubs. And given
 its location in an operating industrial port, with nearby terminals already handling potentially
 dangerous liquefied gases and chemicals, opposition from regulatory bodies or the surrounding
 community is not anticipated.

                                               Page 8 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

2 INTRODUCTION

The purpose of this project was to perform a pre-FEED analysis for the design and engineering of the
Tampa Regional Intermodal Carbon Hub (“T-RICH”), a comprehensive intermodal transportation hub for
captured CO2 on the west coast of Florida sited at Port Tampa Bay. T-RICH is intended to be the principal
gateway for intermediate storage of CO2 captured from emitters across the State of Florida. The hub will
receive, store, process, and load for maritime transport no fewer than 2,000,000 metric tons (mt) of CO2
per year for permanent sequestration on- and offshore in the Gulf of Mexico region as such underground
storage sites receive final Class VI permits. Some of the transported CO2 could be used for enhanced oil
recovery (EOR) in this same region. As a result of the recently passed H.R. 1 bill, EOR now qualifies for the
same amount of federal tax credit as for permanent underground sequestration.
This project is essential to provide Florida’s largest emitters with a solution for the transport and storage
of captured CO2. Florida is the third highest emitting state in the U.S. due to the large number of electricity
generating power plants that line its Gulf and Atlantic coasts. 1 The Tampa Bay region is home to a high
concentration of power generating facilities and large industrial CO2 emitters. Port Tampa Bay has
identified a suitable available parcel within the port with access to existing deep-water Berth 30 for the
site of the hub. The deep-water berth is critical for the efficient transport of outbound CO2 on a ship,
which will minimize the cost of transport of the CO2 compared to competing technologies, thereby
maximizing project viability. Development of this hub involves the installation of storage tanks and
liquefaction equipment at Port Tampa Bay. As proposed, T-RICH will provide an intermediate storage hub
for captured CO2 accessible to emitters anywhere in the State of Florida via all transport modes including
existing rail, truck, pipeline, and ship infrastructure systems into the hub site at Port Tampa Bay.
This project’s main objective was to determine that the development of T-RICH is technologically feasible
and commercially viable. Existing transport infrastructure at Port Tampa Bay and the vicinity includes
interstate highways, major state roads, existing pipeline rights of way upon which new CO2 pipelines could
be built, the CSX rail network, and shipping channels with deep water access. The study determined the
technical, physical, and regulatory requirements for the installation of CO2 storage tanks, gas conditioning
and transfer equipment, and extensions of nearby existing roads, rail lines and pipelines into the site. The
study evaluated the siting, design, and technical requirements for a minimum storage capacity of 30,000
metric tons (mt) which would support processing up to 2,000,000 mt of CO2 captured in Florida annually
for permanent sequestration or EOR use. The plan includes the ability to expand the capacity of the T-
RICH site as more CO2 is captured by emitters in the region and the State, and more marine vessels would
be built in the U.S. and added to transport the additional quantity of captured CO2 delivered to and stored
at the hub site for subsequent shipping to the northern Gulf Coast.
Though not part of the funded study scope, the study envisions that CO2 liquified and stored at the hub
site will be loaded into dedicated CO2 marine ships for regular delivery to a deep-water berth at a CO2

tank terminal on the Mississippi River near Baton Rouge, Louisiana. The ships, which may be articulated
tugs and barges (ATBs), will be designed by Aptamus, built in America, and qualified as Jones Act U.S. flag
carriers. The CO2 will subsequently be delivered via an existing CO2 pipeline network to permanent,
approved, underground Class VI sequestration wells or EOR sites in the Gulf Coast region.2

2.1.1 ABOUT APTAMUS CARBON SOLUTIONS
As of this writing, Aptamus Carbon Solutions is a subsidiary of Overseas Shipholding Group, Inc. (OSG),
this grant’s prime recipient, and a member of the Saltchuk family of companies. Aptamus was formed to
leverage OSG’s 80 years of shipping experience with liquid bulk commodities in tanker vessels into the
marine transportation of carbon dioxide, a product never before carried on the waters of the United
States. Aptamus’ principals have more than a combined 100 years managing engineering and construction
projects, much of it in the marine field. Aptamus intends to focus on development of CO2-carrying ships
and the marine terminals at which they will load and discharge their CO2. The T-RICH intermodal CO2 hub
and ship loading terminal is a critical piece of that planned value chain.
Aptamus’ principals, including this grant’s principal investigator, are also heavily involved in providing
technical support to OSG’s fleet of 21 crude, product and chemical tankers. Project work includes building
new vessels or heavily modifying existing ones, incorporating environmental technology like ballast water
treatment systems and emissions reduction technology, and studying future methods of reducing
emissions even further such as retrofitting onboard carbon capture and storage systems which could
offload their captured CO2 at the T-RICH hub.
Aptamus and OSG are both owned by Saltchuk, a privately owned family of diversified freight
transportation (including marine shipping companies and airlines), marine service, and energy distribution
companies headquartered in Seattle, WA. With consolidated annual revenue of approximately $5.5 billion
and 8,500 employees, Saltchuk has the resources necessary to commit to large CO2-related infrastructure
projects once they are ready to be built.

2.1.2 ABOUT ENTR, PART OF AKER SOLUTIONS
Entr draws upon Aker Solutions’ rich heritage of innovation and project development to deliver insights,
advice, and solutions to customers, enabling robust decision making and maximum value from inception
to implementation. As Aker Solutions dedicated consultancy arm, Entr builds upon a deep technical
expertise and broad understanding of the commercial, economic, ESG, regulatory and supply chain
challenges, to support customers in navigating the new energy landscape.
Aker’s CCS journey began in the 1990s, and since then, it has continued to deliver first-of-a-kind CCS
projects to clients across the globe. It has successfully delivered the Sleipner CO2-storage platform and
the engineering, procurement and construction (EPC) of the amine plant at the CO2 Technology Centre in
Mongstad, Norway – the world’s largest and most advanced facility for testing carbon capture
technologies.
Aker Solutions leverages its strong project expertise, engineering competence, and proven delivery
models to deliver viable and scalable solutions for large and complex developments, facilities, and sites
across the entire CCS value chain. From capture to injection, it provides innovative solutions working with
leading technology partners to help pave the way for commercial CCS.

2.1.3 ABOUT PORT TAMPA BAY
Port Tampa Bay is the largest port in the State of Florida and is supervised by the Tampa Port Authority.
The port is primarily located in the city of Tampa, Florida, though some of the port property resides
outside city limits and is administered by Hillsborough County. The port directly accesses Tampa Bay on
the west coast of Florida and is approximately 30 miles from the Gulf of Mexico. The port serves
containerships, tankers, and cruise ships among its primary maritime customers. As of 2023 year-end data,
Port Tampa Bay ranks 24th in the U.S. in total trade in cargo tonnage. It is the largest and most diverse
port in Florida with an economic impact of more than $15.1 billion and supports more than 80,000 jobs3.
As a result, Port Tampa Bay is well qualified to support the T-RICH project. It has an in-house engineering
staff with extensive experience assisting terminal development involving liquid cargoes including
petroleum, natural gas, and ammonia products. Its capabilities include providing engineering, business
development, and regulatory expertise related to new terminal tenants. Moreover, it has adopted a
forward-looking approach to sustainability and fully supports the emerging cargo of liquified CO2. Most

3
 U.S. Port Ranking, American Association of Ports Authority, 2024

importantly, its location on the west coast of Florida in a region of more than 30 million tons of CO2
emitted annually, within a short three-day transit route to the northern Gulf Coast makes Port Tampa Bay
an ideal location for the development of the T-RICH loading and processing hub site.

3 FACILITY SITING STUDY

The primary requirement which Aptamus identified for the selection of a suitable site for T-RICH was that
it be on, or near, a deep-water navigable shipping channel capable of accommodating vessels with a draft
of at least 30 feet (9.1 meters). Being on such a channel will allow for the export of CO2 from the T-RICH
site via ocean going marine vessels. This supports Aptamus’ original vision of a marine transportation
system to move CO2 captured at industrial sites in Florida to Louisiana and Texas for use in enhanced oil
recovery or for permanent sequestration since those states contain the nation’s largest confirmed safe
underground CO2 storage sites. Since Aptamus and its affiliate company OSG are based in Tampa, Florida,
the Port of Tampa Bay (PTB) was contacted to identify vacant marine terminal sites that would facilitate
the export of CO2 via marine vessels. Ideally the site would be next to an existing deepwater navigable
waterway or reasonably close enough to one that a short pipeline from the hub site to a marine berth
could be installed.
PTB has several such terminal sites available, but one stands out to both parties as being particularly
appealing for the T-RICH site. The site is on the Pendola Point Peninsula, near existing Berth 30. Additional
advantages include the fact that the site is surrounded by existing, active industrial terminal sites and is
more than one mile from the nearest residential or public building and road. Several existing industrial
facilities nearby already handle and process liquefied gases with similarities to liquified CO2, so Pendola
Point contains existing hazardous materials facilities and has excellent rail and truck access.
The proposed site is owned by Port Tampa Bay, located in the City of Tampa, Hillsborough County, FL. The
red dot in Figure 3.1 depicts the site within the city and county. Hillsborough County includes the City of
Tampa and large suburban municipalities such as Brandon, Riverview, and Town ‘n’ Country. The total
county population is 1,451,358 people as of the last reported census.

 Figure 3.1 – Site Location Within Hillsborough County, FL (red circle)

The specific site envisioned for the hub within Port Tampa Bay boundaries is shown in Figure 3.2 below.
The site is currently unoccupied and undeveloped, and totals approximately 16± acres in land area. The
first objective of this study was to determine if the parcel was of sufficient size for a hub capable of
handling at least 2 million tons of CO2 throughput per year. As detailed later in this report, Entr’s
equipment layout requires approximately 8.5 acres and therefore this objective was met. Further study
revealed that the overall 16-acre parcel is of sufficient size to eventually expand to a throughput of 8
million tons per year. The proposed hub site is adjacent to deep water accessible Berth 30 which already
has infrastructure installed to serve the adjacent marine terminal tenants, a liquefied petroleum gas (LPG)
terminal to the east, and a cement and bulk aggregate terminal to the west. PTB advises that Berth 30 is
seldom used by those two adjacent tenants and would normally be readily available for Aptamus’
proposed two CO2 ships regularly calling on the T-RICH terminal.

by other existing industrial sites of a similar nature, the site itself does not introduce an increased hazard
to the immediate surrounding environment that wasn’t already there due to existing port operations.
Risks unique to CO2 are discussed later in this report.

3.1 GEOLOGY

3.1.1 THE STATE OF FLORIDA
The land in Florida lies on the eastern side of the “Florida Platform,” a flat, sedimentary geological
structure that is more than twice as wide as the state itself. It was formed millions of years ago from
calcium carbonate and similar materials that were deposited from the warm, shallow ocean that fully
covered it at the time. From that, limestone formed which now extends hundreds of feet below the
surface. There is a karst surface above the limestone with many sinkholes, folds, sags and warps, which
itself is covered by a thin layer of quartz sand which was transported over time from the Appalachian and
Piedmont regions to the north. Since the end of the last ice age 20,000 years ago, global sea levels have
risen approximately 122 meters covering the majority of the Florida Platform and resulting in the Florida
shorelines we see today, including Tampa Bay. Furthermore, the complexity of local processes such as
sedimentation and estuarian evolution (semi-enclosed coastal zones where river freshwater mixes with
seawater) plays a critical role in shaping the region’s contemporary appearance.
Although the latest preliminary studies suggest that CO2 sequestration might be feasible in some very
limited parts of Florida, the high-water tables, abundant karst formations, and wide regions of unstable
and porous limestone present real challenges for permanent geologic sequestration of CO2 in Florida. CO₂
mineralization does not happen instantly. It also does not stay in just one place while mineralizing. CO₂
reacts with minerals at variable rates over time and how a CO2 plume migrates horizontally depends on
the site-specific geology and raises a serious risk of water table contamination.
Florida sites believed by some to have potential for sequestration are often situated near or beneath the
groundwater surface. Injecting CO₂ into zones with these characteristics will require long-term monitoring
to address cross-contamination potential and related concerns. The risk of leakage is heightened due to
elevated water tables, which limit the available pore space for CO₂ storage and may also promote
buoyancy-driven upward migration. Regulatory approval will necessitate long-term evidence before
proceeding. Sea level rise, which is a present and continuing problem impacting Florida, also increases the
risk of contamination and seepage of any injected CO2 into this already problematic geology.4
Karst systems are characterized by networks of diverse sinkholes, defined by fractures and subterranean
conduits. These features result in highly permeable and unpredictable flow paths, posing significant
challenges for the secure containment of injected CO₂. Karstic formations heighten the risk of rapid

4
  There has been some early computer modeling and simulation testing of sequestration involving a site in Polk
County, Florida. That project appears to have been halted. As a result, the T-RICH hub site is the only reasonable
solution for the safe, efficient processing, temporary storage, and transport of captured CO2 from Florida’s major
emitters, and which could be operational in five years to start moving carbon to locations where existing EOR and
sequestration options exist. With new gas fired power plants now being considered by most Florida generation
companies, adopting carbon capture systems should be seriously pursued to give Florida ratepayers the benefit of
clean power from these new electricity generation facilities.

                                                Page 17 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

vertical and lateral CO₂ migration, thereby undermining the safety and effectiveness of geological storage
in such environments.
When exposed to slightly acidic fluids like carbonic acid, which forms when CO₂ interacts with water,
limestone dissolves in an unstable manner. This process creates additional voids with features that may
collapse, such as new sinkholes, compromising both the integrity of the caprock meant to seal CO₂
underground and the migration of fluids. Due to this inherent instability, modeling, monitoring, and
ensuring the permanent storage of injected CO₂ present significant challenges.
Only a limited number of locations are likely to qualify for long-term CO2 sequestration if they adhere to
rigorous hydrological and geological assessments. It may take over a decade to obtain the necessary
geological details of Florida's geology. Consequently, stakeholders are actively considering marine
transportation for exporting captured CO2, as it presents an attractive option in terms of efficiency, risk
reduction, cost, and time to commercial operation. Marine transport is likely to be the only viable
alternative for many emitters, particularly for those located near the coast.
It is these unusual geological features of Florida that are currently thought to make it generally unsuitable
for safe, permanent sequestration of CO2 which makes the export of captured CO2 out of Florida on a
marine transportation system even more critical as the best solution for Florida emitters.

3.1.2 THE PROPOSED T-RICH SITE
In 2005 soil borings were taken on a site adjacent to the proposed T-RICH site, approximately 2,000 feet
east of where the T-RICH CO2 storage tanks are proposed to be placed on the Pendola Point peninsula.
Those borings found various layers of silty clayey sand, sandy clay to clay, and slightly silty to silty sand
generally down to a depth of 45 to 50 feet. Below 50 feet lies a layer of weathered to completely
weathered limestone, and at a depth below approximately 70 feet slightly weathered limestone is
encountered. Groundwater was found at a depth of 8 to 10 feet with a note that the seasonal high level
of groundwater should be expected to be 6 to 8 feet below the surface. 5 The borings were taken in
preparation for the construction of cement storage domes with a noted design weight of 45,000 tons. The
weight of CO2 tanks taking up an equivalent amount of ground surface area is only approximately 6,500
tons. The report recommended the use of precast concrete piles or drilled shaft foundations with a 5 to
10 foot penetration into the limestone layer, for a total pile depth of approximately 60 feet. Figure 3.4
depicts an approximate graphical representation of the strata described in the report.

3.2 WEATHER

The proposed site in Port Tampa Bay has a humid subtropical climate. There is a significant average
amount of rainfall, particularly from June through September, making proper drainage of the site of
particular importance. Average temperatures are among the highest in the nation which proves a
challenge to cooling of the CO2 liquefaction process and the resultant energy costs as discussed elsewhere
in this report. And there is an increased risk of hurricanes throughout the region which necessitates
additional preparation for potential storm surges and high winds. However, the site is expected to be
approximately 14 feet above sea level once prepared for equipment installation, so it will naturally be well
protected against forecasted sea level rise and most storm surges from hurricanes.
Average rainfall in Tampa peaks in August, with June, July, and September also being very wet months.
Total annual rainfall is 49.48 inches, placing it in 48th place out of 253 U.S. cities.6

 Figure 3.5: Monthly Average Rainfall, Tampa, FL (source: NOAA NCEI)
Tampa’s average annual temperature is among the highest in the nation at 74.5°F, placing it 10th among
253 U.S. cities.7

 Figure 3.6: Monthly Average Temperature, Tampa, FL (source: NOAA NCEI)

6
 National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, National Centers for Environmental Information
7
 National Oceanic and Atmospheric Administration, National Centers for Environmental Information

                                                 Page 20 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 3.7: Tampa International Airport Wind Rose, 2020-2024 (source Midwestern Regional
 Climate Center)

The proposed hub site is in a port contiguous to the Gulf of Mexico, where much of the land is generally
just a few feet above sea level. So while much of the area surrounding the site may suffer from a projected
sea level rise of 7.2 feet by the year 2100, the T-RICH site will not since it is significantly higher than most
of the surrounding port and region which will insulate it from such negative effects. A topographical survey
based on the North American Vertical Datum of 1988 shows that the site’s native elevation ranges from
8.1 feet at the southwest corner to 17.6 feet in the eastern half. After it is filled and graded for use as the
T-RICH facility, it is projected to be 13.5 feet throughout the center drainage basin to 14.5 feet near the
perimeter. Therefore, all CO2 handling equipment, buildings, and infrastructure will be located well above
the projected 7.2 foot level by 2100. Note that in the Tampa Bay region, an elevation of 0 feet relative to
the NAVD88 system is 1.48 feet higher than mean lower-low water (MLLW). Therefore, the site’s NAVD88
elevation of 14 feet is actually 15.48 feet above MLLW, providing an even greater margin of safety.
Storm surge due to hurricanes and other severe weather events is a greater threat to the site in the
foreseeable future than long-term sea level rise. Scientists generally agree that climate change is
producing more frequent and more severe destructive storms including tornados and hurricanes, both of
which have appeared in Florida more regularly in recent years. However, a storm surge in this region is
unlikely to reach 15.48 feet above MLLW which would be necessary to flood the site. Hurricane Helene in
2024 created a record storm surge reported to be 7.2 feet above MLLW in nearby East Bay. That broke
the previous record of 4.56 feet set the previous year by Hurricane Idalia, a Category 4 hurricane off
Tampa’s coast. The site is still approximately 8 feet higher than this record storm surge event.
Hurricane-strength winds are also a risk throughout this region of the United States. However, the most
stringent building codes for wind velocity have been adopted in Florida and will be followed to minimize

                                                Page 21 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

the risk of damage or uncontrolled release of CO2 due to a direct hit of a major hurricane or other severe
wind weather events. Ensuring that all equipment and materials are suitable for saltwater environments
to the extent practical will also be part of future detailed design phases.

3.3 PROXIMITY TO EXISTING CO 2 SOURCES & DESTI NATIONS

There are numerous types of CO2 emission sources in the West Central region of Florida, all of which could
be served by the T-RICH hub. This section summarizes those source types in order of decreasing priority
for this project. We have identified electricity generating power plants as the highest priority, due to them
having the greatest point source quantities of emissions compared to all other emitter types. Power plants
in the region tend to have annual CO2 emissions greater than 500,000 tons, and even up to 4.2 million
tons, compared to other industrial types of emitters whose annual CO2 emissions generally tend to remain
under 125,000 tons. Therefore, it would likely be the most cost-effective option, and the greatest positive
benefit to the environment and human health, to install carbon capture systems at power plants and
transport that CO2 to T-RICH. At the other end of the spectrum, a small but increasingly important emitting
category are marine cargo vessels that capture CO2 from their exhaust gas and store it onboard until they
reach port. Once they reach a port, they will need a facility like T-RICH to discharge it to. The International
Maritime Organization has issued regulations for shipping companies to reduce their vessels’ carbon
emissions. Onboard carbon capture systems are being developed and tested to meet these emission
regulations. In the future, as more ships install these onboard systems, the demand for port terminals like
T-RICH to offload the captured gas has the potential to become a significant terminal activity. Such vessels
are expected to transfer less than 1,000 tons of CO2 per ship visit. However, there would be no need to
invest in permanent transportation infrastructure to service these emitter types; they would pull up to
the berth at T-RICH to discharge their CO2 directly into the storage tanks using the existing piping systems
at T-RICH.

3.3.1 POWER PLANTS
The largest target anthropogenic CO2 emission sources in the region are electricity generating power
plants. These CO2 sources are generally expected to adopt amine-based carbon capture systems due to
that technology’s high Technology Readiness Level (TRL). Prevalent exhaust emissions from industrial
emitters in the region come from natural gas sources. According to the U.S. Energy Information
Administration (EIA), natural gas power plants account for about 75.2% of the total electricity generation
in the Tampa Bay area. This is significantly higher than the national average of 42.3%. The high percentage
of natural gas power plants in the Tampa Bay area is due to several factors, including the availability of
natural gas resources, the environmental regulations in the area, the cost of electricity generation,
increasingly prolonged heat and humidity, and the growing population in the Greater Tampa region and
the state.

                                               Page 22 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The percentage of electricity generation by fuel type in the Tampa Bay area in 20228 was as follows:
       Natural gas: 75.2%
       Nuclear: 13.3%
       Coal: 5.2%
  Non-hydroelectric renewables: 6.3%
The Tampa Bay area is home to several natural gas power plants, including the Bayside and Big Bend
Power Stations operated by Tampa Electric (TECO), and the Bartow, Anclote and Crystal River plants
operated by Duke Energy. See Table 3.1 for a summary of these and the other plants shown in Figure 3.8
and their respective CO2 emissions. The high percentage of natural gas power plants in the Tampa Bay
area has a few benefits. Natural gas is a cleaner-burning fuel than coal, and it produces fewer emissions
of greenhouse gases and pollutants. Additionally, natural gas is a relatively inexpensive fuel, which helps
to keep electricity prices low. The high percentage of natural gas power plants in the Tampa Bay area
reflects the region's commitment to produce electricity from non-coal fuel sources. Development of the
proposed hub would further the region’s sustainable energy and carbon reduction goals. However, it is
important to be aware that natural gas power plants produce significant CO2 emissions. Thus, adopting
carbon capture systems on gas power plants will result in the production of clean power which will provide
greater health benefits to state residents, assist in reducing CO2 emission levels in the state, and allow
power companies to meet future projected increases in power demand in Florida.
The exhaust gas composition from a natural-gas power plant can vary depending on the efficiency of the
power plant and the specific type of natural gas used. However, the following are some of the most
common components of exhaust gas from natural gas power plants9:
       Carbon dioxide (CO2): 15-50%
       Water vapor (H2O): 15-20%
       Nitrogen (N2): 70-75%
       Oxygen (O2): 1-2%
  Other gases: trace amounts of methane, ethane, propane, butane, and sulfur dioxide.
The robust concentration of CO2 in a natural gas burning power plant exhaust makes the cost of carbon
capture cost effective and within or near the current tax credit incentives10. In addition, amine-based point
source carbon capture solutions at natural gas burning power plants have been demonstrated for many

3.3.2 GENERAL INDUSTRIAL EMISSIONS
T-RICH would not be limited to serving natural gas power plants. There are many other significant emitters
in this region that could be served by T-RICH. For example, the Mosaic Company operates a number of
phosphate mining and fertilizer production facilities in the region with a combined 627,707 tons of CO2
emissions according to EPA data. And the City of Tampa operates a waste-to-energy plant just 3.3 miles
from the proposed T-RICH site, emitting 120,522 tons of CO2 in 2023. These and many other emitters in
the region can benefit from the T-RICH hub, with options of transporting their captured CO2 to T-RICH via
new pipelines, rail cars or tanker trucks.

We analyzed the spatial distribution of significant stationary CO₂ emitters within the Tampa Bay region
(central coordinates: 27.9517° N, –82.4588° W) and throughout the state of Florida which involves
identifying clusters of high-emission sources such as power plants, industrial facilities, and large
manufacturing operations. These sources are typically concentrated near urban centers and along major
transportation corridors due to infrastructure availability and proximity to markets. In the Tampa Bay
area, emitters are likely to be found near the Port of Tampa, industrial zones, and energy production sites.
Statewide, regions like the Miami metropolitan area, Jacksonville, and the I-4 corridor between Tampa
and Orlando also host substantial stationary emissions. Mapping these emitters with geospatial tools
allows for the estimation of total CO₂ that could be served by T-RICH utilizing multiple transportation
modalities.

The U.S. Environmental Protection Agency (EPA) has compiled national greenhouse-gas (GHG) emissions

(GHGRP). When rendered as geospatial visualizations, the inventories reveal distributional patterns that
facilitate regional comparisons, highlight emission “hotspots,” and may indicate associations with
industrial activity, population density, or recent policy changes. Public availability enables independent
verification and secondary analysis by researchers, practitioners, and the broader public, thereby
supporting evidence-informed prioritization and impact monitoring. While visualization alone does paint
the full picture, it helps prioritize targeted efforts.

3.3.3 MARINE VESSELS
Another potential future source of CO2 for the T-RICH facility is CO2 captured onboard ships and offloaded
in port. In this scenario, CO2 is gradually captured from the ship’s exhaust gas over the course of a voyage,
liquified, and stored in specialized tanks onboard. When the ship reaches port, it discharges this stored
LCO2 to a shore facility such as T-RICH. When T-RICH’s berth is not in use by the dedicated CO2 export ship,
another ocean-going ship visiting the port or transiting the Gulf coast region with Onboard Carbon Capture
and Storage (OCCS) can berth at T-RICH to discharge its LCO2 directly into T-RICH’s storage tanks. Provided,
of course, that the ship’s captured CO2 has been verified to meet T-RICH’s CO2 quality specification prior
to the transfer.

Table 3.4 below summarizes the projected 2026 vessel activity at the Port of Tampa with operational
parameters to inform potential expansion opportunities at the CO₂ offloading hub. Key metrics include
main engine specifications, fuel consumption expectations, voyage durations, and estimated annual visit
factors for calculating onboard carbon capture potential and transfer infrastructure requirements.

The data reflects industry-standard propulsion systems for vessel classes frequenting the port, paired with
fuel consumption quantities calibrated to typical ships sizes and voyage duration (e.g., medium-range
(MR) tankers, and Caribbean and Latin America trade loops for containerships). While voyage days are
generalized due to route variability, they provide a baseline for estimating cumulative CO₂ generation per
arrival - a prerequisite for sizing shore-based reception systems.

This analysis assumes vessels employ conventional marine fuels (VLSFO/HSFO) and do not yet account for
alternative propulsion systems, ensuring conservative estimates for liquid CO₂ offloading volumes. Engine
sizes and visit frequencies are derived from the port’s public statistics information and cargo priorities,
particularly phosphate exports and distillate imports dominating bulk and tanker traffic.

The projections hinge on several key assumptions requiring consideration: A 10% adoption rate of
onboard carbon capture systems (OCCS) is estimated, reflecting a conservative scenario where regulatory
incentives or global emission fees drive early adoption. In practice, actual participation could fall below
5% due to high retrofit costs, operational complexities, or slow regulatory mandates, or surge beyond 20%
if carbon pricing mechanisms or stringent emission regulations are implemented. Another key assumption
is the 25% capture rate which assumes amine scrubbers achieve baseline efficiency under ideal conditions,
though real-world performance may vary by ±10% due to OCCS performance metrics, renewable fuels
availability, or system downtime. Voyage days and fuel consumption are derived from industry averages,
but individual vessel routing (e.g., shorter Gulf Coast shuttles vs. transatlantic hauls) and speed
optimization practices could alter actual CO₂ generation by 15–30%. Finally, the 3.2 CO₂-to-fuel multiplier
accommodates multiple combustion efficiencies and marine fuel compositions, though sulfur content
variations or engine derating could marginally affect this ratio. These variables collectively introduce a

12
  https://www.porttb.com/statistics (Calculation Formula: CO₂ Offloaded per Ship = Avg. Fuel

10% of Annual Visits)

                                             Page 28 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

potential ±40% uncertainty range around the projected 18,280–53,320 MTCO2 annual offload capacity,
requiring an adaptive infrastructure design and phased implementation at T-RICH.

Ensuring the safe offloading of captured CO₂ at Aptamus’ T-RICH hub requires addressing not only the
technical infrastructure details but also the broader industry uncertainties, such as the lack of global
standards for CO₂ purity and best practices in pressure-temperature conditions. Here's how the system
responds within this evolving context:

1. Manifold Connection:

Ships connect to the T-RICH berth’s loading arm using ISO-standardized flanges. However, current
standards do not specify requirements for CO₂ impurities, such as sulfur compounds and residual amines,
which could corrode seals or compromise storage integrity. The design would incorporate quick-release
couplings with dual-ball valves and emergency shut-off triggers. Yet, the absence of global guidelines for
CO₂ composition suggests that emerging Carbon Capture technologies may introduce unregulated
contaminants unknown at this time. Accommodating CO2 from emerging Carbon Capture technologies
might introduce necessary modifications to the equipment.

2. Pressure & Temperature Control:

While the T-RICH site’s storage tanks and associated equipment will be designed for a pressure range of
approximately 15 to 18 bar and a corresponding temperature range of -25°C to -30°C, there is no
standardized pressure-temperature regime for maritime CO₂ carriage and offloading. This lack of
standardization poses future connectivity uncertainties. Ships employing various capture technologies,
such as low-pressure cryogenic or high-pressure amine systems, might deliver CO₂ outside the hub’s
compatibility range, requiring expensive retrofits.

3. Transfer Equipment:

Cryogenic pumps and insulated hoses are designed and constructed according to industry standards and
best practices. However, the absence of standardized carbon capture equipment requirements might
introduce mandatory equipment upgrades and changes to maintenance practices due to evolving
legislation.

4. Operational Workflow Recommendations:

Pre-connection screening of the offloading ship’s CO2 quality is a critical quality control measure that must
be implemented to ensure the integrity and safety of shipments. By rejecting shipments that exceed
specified contaminant thresholds, such as more than 100 parts per million (ppm) of water (H₂O) or over
50 ppm of amines, this process helps maintain the purity standards of the received CO2. This screening
step is particularly important in industries where even small amounts of impurities can significantly impact
product quality, process efficiency, or safety.

                                              Page 29 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

3.4 PROXIMITY TO EXISTING INFRASTRUCTURE

The proposed T-RICH hub site is perfect for use as a CO2 intermodal hub due to 1) being surrounded by
numerous existing industrial facilities and 2) the existence of several suitable modes of transportation
nearby with relatively little work necessary to tie the site directly into them. The primary infrastructure
required for T-RICH, with its planned export of CO2 on ocean-going marine vessels, is a ship berth. There
is an existing deep-water berth at the site ready to accept a large CO2 vessel with minor modifications to
receive the hub’s processed and liquified CO2 and load it for transport to the permanent sequestration or
EOR site in the northern Gulf region. See Section 4.5.12 for a detailed discussion on this berth.
The next type of infrastructure needed to make T-RICH a true intermodal hub is an incoming rail line.
There is a main rail line owned by U.S. rail operator CSX within one mile of the site, with an existing spur
owned by Port Tampa Bay running down Pendola Point peninsula which serves the terminal adjacent to
T-RICH. An extension of this rail line can be installed onto the proposed hub site, as confirmed by Port
Tampa Bay engineers. CSX operates a rail network of over 20,000 miles serving 23 states. Figure 3.10
shows this existing rail network in blue with the T-RICH site depicted as a red dot. Existing rail lines run
very close by, or even directly adjacent to, several of the large power plants shown in the central part of
the state in Figure 3.8. For example, a rail line currently runs to the entrance of Duke’s Hines Energy
Complex which is the single largest point source of CO2 emissions identified in this study (4.2 mtpa of CO2
emissions). Existing rail lines also run to within approximately 500 feet of the generators at both the CD
McIntosh Jr. Power Plant and the Osprey Energy Center, both in Lakeland, FL. This existing rail network
can be leveraged to transport large quantities of CO2 captured at plants across the state to T-RICH even
without significant investments in pipelines, and the associated long lead times involved with pipeline
permitting and construction.
An incoming pipeline to T-RICH is, however, considered ultimately necessary to make the overall CO2
transportation system, of which T-RICH is a critical part, cost effective. There are currently no CO2 pipelines
in the region which the hub can make use of, and new, relatively short pipelines to transmit CO2 captured
by the region’s major stranded emitters to T-RICH will incur high capital costs. But the path forward to
creating the necessary local network of CO2 pipelines to deliver CO2 to the T-RICH hub is clear and
practical. Peoples Gas, a Florida natural gas pipeline distribution company, operates natural gas delivery
pipelines in the immediate vicinity and throughout the mid-Florida region. They have the capacity and
expertise to build CO2 pipelines from those natural gas customers to the proposed T-RICH hub along some
of the same rights of way they already own for natural gas delivery to its customers. In their project
support letter written in July 2023, Peoples Gas wrote “Peoples Gas System’s infrastructure is located near
the proposed carbon capture and storage transportation hub facilities and is well positioned to support
the development and advancements of this technology for the state.” There is also the strong possibility
of running a new CO2 pipeline along ammonia pipelines and main power lines from the port out to
consumers in the central part of the state. See Figure 3.11 which shows existing natural gas and hazardous
liquid (ammonia) pipelines between T-RICH and several of the major power plant emitters in Central
Florida listed in Table 3.1.

                                               Page 30 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 3.10: Existing CSX Rail Network in West-Central Florida (T-RICH is the red dot)

 Figure 3.11: Existing Natural Gas and Hazardous Liquid Pipelines13
Being within a major existing port complex, T-RICH will have ready access to multiple interstate highways
via U.S. Highway 41, an industrial 4-lane highway which runs up the east side of the port. The entrance to
the Pendola Point area of Port Tampa Bay, on which T-RICH will be situated, is directly off U.S. 41, an
industrial road which numerous large commercial trucks traverse on a daily basis going into and out of

13
 https://pvnpms.phmsa.dot.gov/PublicViewer/

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

the port complex. Trucks carrying CO2 from the central part of the state can travel west into the Tampa
Bay region on I-4 which begins on the east coast of Florida near Daytona Beach and travels through
Orlando, Florida’s third most-populous metropolitan region after Tampa-St. Petersburg-Clearwater. From
there, they would travel south on U.S. 41 directly to T-RICH. CO2 trucks coming from the west, such as
Pinellas County, would travel east on I-275 and I-4, again heading south on U.S. 41 to reach T-RICH. And
CO2 trucks heading north on I-75 from south Florida (I-75 begins in Miami), would take the exit for
Gibsonton Drive, a 4-lane road through a sparsely populated area, in order to reach U.S. 41 northbound
and ultimately T-RICH. An abundance of commercial-grade 4-lane roads and interstate highways will allow
CO2 trucks to access T-RICH from any direction in Florida.

3.5 PROXIMITY TO EXISTING UTILITIES

Due to its location in a large, diverse, and well-developed industrial shipping port, the T-RICH site has
ready access to all of the utilities needed to perform its function. Primarily, a significant amount of

                                             Page 32 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

electricity is needed to power the liquefaction process where the compressors that compress the CO2 gas
in three stages are by far the largest consumer of electricity. Aptamus and its technical consultant Entr
have had several meetings with TECO personnel during the study period, and TECO has confirmed that it
is able and willing to provide new power transmission infrastructure as necessary to satisfy T-RICH’s power
demands. In those meetings, TECO and Entr agreed that the optimum supply voltage would be 69 kV.
Other details were discussed but not yet finalized because laying new power lines and associated
equipment from outside the fence line of the T-RICH site is not within the scope of this study. Some
outstanding issues for future study, however, include 1) whether power would come from an existing or
new substation, or from a tap on an existing transmission line, 2) whether Aptamus or TECO will provide
the switchgear, and 3) whose specifications would govern the switchgear design. Additionally, we intend
to evaluate whether a solar energy generation system could reduce the cost of the TECO-supplied
electricity, thereby reducing the overall OPEX of the T-RICH site.
The T-RICH hub will consume a relatively small amount of water, and generate only a small amount of
sewage from its administrative building and workshop. PTB’s subcontractor confirmed that existing
potable water and sewer lines run down Pendola Point Road, along the south edge of the T-RICH hub site.
The potable water line is 8 inches in diameter, and a new 2-inch branch line will be connected with a
tapping valve and sleeve and run to the administrative building with a 2-inch isolation valve, a water
meter, and a backflow preventer. In addition, a new 6-inch line will be tapped off the existing 8-inch city
line to be run to a new fire hydrant in the middle of the hub.
A 2-inch HDPE force main will be installed from the administration building back to the existing 8-inch
force main at the street. Sewage will be run through an E/One DH071 Grinder Pump Station to ensure
adequate pressure. See Figure 3.13 for a snip of the Utility Plan.

 Figure 3.13: Existing and New Utility Lines at the T-RICH Hub

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

3.6 NEARBY SENSITIVE AREAS

The proposed site is not located within any designated Environmentally Sensitive Area (ESA). Tampa Bay
includes several Aquatic Preserves managed by the Florida Department of Environmental Protection,
including the Pinellas, Boca Ciega Bay, Terra Ceia and Cockroach Bay Aquatic Preserves. But the proposed
T-RICH site is at least ten miles from any of them.
The site is also not believed to be near any High Consequence Area (HCA). A major release of CO2 from
the site is not expected to reach any dense population areas, schools, hospitals, or essential infrastructure.
One exception may be the Tampa Electric Bayside Power Plant not far to the northeast of the site. It is
theoretically possible that a release of CO2 could disrupt power generation there and cause power outages
in the surrounding region. However, a large release of CO2 from the site’s storage tanks or processing
equipment will likely travel south of the site, away from the power plant, and into an unpopulated area
of Tampa Bay due to the innovative use of walls to direct released CO2 south and away from existing
adjacent industrial sites. This was analyzed in the CO2 dispersion analysis performed as part of the study
and discussed in the next section.
The surrounding waterways comprising greater Tampa Bay may unofficially be considered
environmentally sensitive areas even if they are not officially designated as such. Though it requires
further study, it may be possible that a large release of CO2 into the bay south of the site may have a
detrimental effect on a very small number of birds and fish which might reside there or come to feed from
time to time since it is a very remote location far from both port traffic and human interaction. But one
of the major benefits of CO2 in this case is that, in the highly unlikely event of such a large spill, the escaped
CO2 will quickly warm up and evaporate before it reaches any designated ESAs or HCAs. And once it
evaporates soon after the spill, there is no longer any lingering potential for harm to the marine
environment whatsoever. Accordingly, it is highly unlikely that any leakage will result in harm of loss of
natural wildlife in the undeveloped aquatic area south of Pendola Point.
It should be reiterated that the selection of this proposed site was based in large part on the existence of
current adjacent industrial operations handling a range of products that are potentially harmful to the
surrounding population and environment. Those include the liquefied petroleum gas storage and handling
site directly to the east of this proposed site, and the ammonia storage, liquefaction and handling facility
just to the north of the site. Both operations were evaluated in the past for their potential impact on the
surrounding community and environment and found to be safe and appropriate for their location. The
proposed CO2 operation at T-RICH is not expected to introduce any greater hazards to the surrounding
area than these two operations currently do, and in fact T-RICH is likely to present less cumulative risk of
harm or loss due to the unique properties of CO2.

3.7 CONCEPTUAL EMERGENCY RESPONSE PLAN

The proposed T-RICH site is near existing port facilities and terminals which regularly handle hazardous
materials in their daily operations which could negatively affect the surrounding communities and
environment if not adequately addressed, and lessons can and should be utilized from these existing

                                                Page 34 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

terminal operators who have safely managed these risks when crafting the emergency response plans for
T-RICH.
Perhaps the most hazardous material handled in the port with the highest potential negative impact to
the area is anhydrous ammonia, which has been dubbed one of the region’s most dangerous chemicals.
Approximately two million tons per year are imported into Tampa via ship from an ammonia production
facility in Donaldsonville, Louisiana, for use in the production of ammoniated phosphate fertilizer. West-
central Florida produces roughly 75% of the nation’s phosphate. It is then piped in liquid form from the
port to inland facilities like Mosaic’s Bartow and New Wales plants via a 75-mile underground pipeline
where ammoniated phosphate fertilizer is made with the phosphates mined in the area. Anhydrous
ammonia is classified by the EPA as an Extremely Hazardous Substance (EHS).
The port houses two such ammonia handling facilities, one 1.3 miles northwest on Hooker’s Point, and
the other only 700 feet away directly across the shipping channel from the T-RICH site. A study of these
and a third nearby terminal was funded by the EPA in 1995 to identify existing hazards and response
capabilities and to make recommendations for local first responders and other affected groups. This study
was cited in the Tampa Bay Hazardous Materials Emergency Response Plan, dated June 2020 14 . The
following is a brief summary of some of their findings:
 1. Improved warnings to the public in the event of accidental release. In 1999 a siren warning and
 telephone ring-down system was installed to inform residents adjacent to Port Tampa Bay of an
 unintended release. The coverage area was expanded from an initial one-mile radius to a ten-mile
 radius with additional sirens installed. The ring-down system notifies all tenants of the Port as
 well as schools, churches, other public facilities, and certain private residences who chose to
 register their numbers. The sirens include pre-recorded or live voice capability. It is highly possible
 that the T-RICH site will incorporate a similar audible and telephone warning system, or perhaps
 even piggy-back off this existing system.
 2. Periodic safety meetings between the ammonia terminal operators and local stakeholders.
 Through these efforts the ammonia terminal operators invested in added security and safety
 measures over the years, including upgraded perimeter fencing and alarm systems, and a wall
 surrounding the storage tanks designed to completely contain a major storage tank leak. Entr has
 already identified a similar safety measure in this pre-FEED study, namely to install a wall around
 three sides of the CO2 storage tank area to direct any major leaks of CO2 south to the uninhabited
 open water. As of 2019, an “Ammonia Operations Group” meets on a bi-monthly basis to discuss
 safety measures, new regulations and initiatives. Meetings are attended by the terminal
 operators as well as staff from Port Tampa Bay, Tampa fire, emergency management and police,
 and the U.S. Coast Guard among others.
 3. Consideration of a terrorist event involving sabotage of the ammonia storage tanks. In 2001, as
 a result of these meetings and studies, the port agreed to completely fence the port facilities, and

14
 https://www.epa.gov/system/files/documents/2022-01/tampa-bay-hazardous-materials-emergency-reponse-
plan-2020.pdf

                                                Page 35 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 in particular the ammonia terminals, with guarded entry and exit points. It is envisioned that the
 T-RICH hub site will be equipped with its own perimeter security fence and separate guarded
 entry point, as well as utilize all of the latest technology in terms of CCTV cameras with motion
 sensing, infrared heat/cold detection, and other new intrusion detection and access control
 systems.
The ammonia terminals in the port have been recognized as being a model for safety and concern for
community welfare for other industries to follow, including the T-RICH CO2 terminal.

T-RICH Emergency Response Plan will likely include the following topics:

3.8 PROXIMITY TO THIRD-PARTY HAZARDS

3.8.1 LPG
The threat of fire and explosion due to a Liquid Petroleum Gas (LPG) spill and resultant fires or vapor
explosions currently exist on the adjacent terminal site due to the Plains LPG Services LPG Terminal and
Railcar Operations. The normal components of LPG are propane (C3H8) and butane (C4H10). Small
concentrations of other hydrocarbons may also be present. LPG at atmospheric pressure and temperature
becomes a gas which has a relative density to air of 1.5 to 2.0 and is readily liquefied under moderate
pressures. The relative density of LPG liquid to water is approximately 0.53 to 0.58 at 15°C. LPG has a flash
point at atmospheric pressure of -42°C and becomes a flammable mixture in air at a concentration of 1.5%
(LFL) to 9.6% (UFL).
The LPG Terminal primary storage area is approximately 1,000 feet away from the eastern extent of the
proposed T-RICH plant layout and is surrounded by extensive berms and fire water monitors. Refer to Fig.
3.14. It is not expected that the terminal property and the associated LPG storage dome will present a
significant enough risk to the T-RICH site that it would require additional risk mitigation. Additionally, the
LPG marine transfer pipeline to the north is not considered to be a significant threat to the T-RICH site
due to its location, infrequent usage (reported to be less than 3 times per year), and the potential to
generate a large persistent fire of sufficient magnitude to pose a significant hazard to T-RICH equipment
and personnel.
However, it is recommended that in FEED the project review any existing Facility Siting or Major Process
Hazard Analysis documentation that should exist for the Plains LPG terminal property and operations in
order to verify the above assumptions.
The highest risk associated with LPG handling is considered to be fires or explosions related to the LPG
railcar marshalling and loading facilities along the southern portion of the T-RICH Site (see Fig. 3.14). Entr
has performed a coarse review potential fire and explosion magnitudes and frequencies of occurrence
based on experience and generic industry data. The conclusion was that explosions from a railcar rupture
or BLEVE or a large railcar operations related leak generating an LPG vapor cloud explosion (VCE) requires
further evaluation in FEED if explosion risk analyses for Plains LPG assets are unavailable (i.e. a Facility
Siting Study).

                                               Page 38 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 3.14: LPG Handling Assets Adjacent to T-RICH Site

3.8.2      AMMONIA
The threat of toxic exposure exists from ammonia vapor leakage from the nearby Tampa Ammonia
Terminal (NH3). Based on the apparent tank configuration at the Ammonia Terminal, it is assumed that
the ammonia is being stored at or near atmospheric pressure just below its boiling point of approximately
-33 °C which is a typical terminal storage design. This makes a large breach of a tank "unlikely" and will be
a low-pressure release. Ammonia is a gas at atmospheric pressure and temperatures above its boiling
point (-33°C). Its flash point is 11°C, and it has a relative density in air of ~0.7 and a molar mass of 17.03
g/mol. Ammonia becomes a flammable mixture in air at concentrations of 15% (LFL) to 28% (UFL) and is
highly toxic to humans.
The Ammonia Terminal has a leak "area notification system" as part of the Port of Tampa Emergency
Response Program. This system is briefly summarized in Section 3.7. The Ammonia Terminal is located
approximately 1,000 feet north (see Fig. 3.15). While the risk of toxic exposure levels needs to be
understood, it is likely that the "area notification system," combined with appropriate training and
procedures, and the planned provision of CO2 leakage "shelter(s) in place" (See Section 8.3.5) at the T-
RICH site will likely be determined to be sufficient to address the risk of worker exposure to ammonia.
However, a detailed review of any existing Facility Siting Study or Major Process Hazard Analysis
documentation that should exist for the Ammonia Terminal property and operations should be
undertaken in order to verify the above assumptions.

                                               Page 39 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 3.15: Ammonia Terminal Relative to T-RICH Site

3.9 FUTURE EXPANSION

The initial equipment and infrastructure layout for T-RICH (See Section 4.5) takes up only 8.5 acres of the
total 16± acres within the available parcel. As can be seen, there is an additional 7.5± acres on the east
side of the site that can be used for future expansion. It was found that T-RICH can be expanded by utilizing
one of the following two options:
Option 1: Add 9 more storage tanks, for 20 total, and two more sets of 50% liquefaction trains for an
increased annual throughput of 6mtpa. See Figure 3.16.
Option 2: Add 9 more storage tanks as in Option 1, but change the arrangement of the liquefaction trains
to make them more compact. This change in liquefaction trains allows for an ultimate throughput increase
to 8 mpta. See Figure 3.17.
With Option 1, the plot plan depicted herein can be built and commissioned to support the initial
anticipated 2mtpa throughput. Then, in future years when additional carbon capture plants come online
in the region and their additional captured CO2 is sent to the T-RICH hub, identical storage tanks and
liquefaction equipment can be procured and installed to handle the additional CO2 without disturbing the

                                              Page 40 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

initial operation. In this arrangement, each 1mpta liquefaction train has its own dedicated set of cooling
fans.
With Option 2, the liquefaction train arrangement must first be optimized so that two 1mtpa liquefaction
trains must share one set of cooling fans, reducing overall plant redundancy. If the plant were built with
an initial throughput of 2 mtpa but using this arrangement for the maximum potential plant expansion
capacity, failure of one or more cooling fans could have a greater negative impact on the plant’s
operational throughput. However, this reduced redundancy does not appear to be a showstopper.
It must be noted that neither expansion option provides for the same storage tank capacity buffer as the
base case, due to land area limitations. In both Option 1 and Option 2, the number of storage tanks can
be increased in number from 11 to 20, resulting in a total storage capacity increase from 30,000 m3 to
54,500 m3. That equates to an 80% increase in on site storage capacity, but the aggregate storage capacity
of the number of ships that must visit the terminal to load CO2 to achieve the desired increased
throughput will increase by 200% with Option 1 and by 300% with Option 2. So the ability for the hub
operations to accommodate unplanned delays in the vessel schedules will be hindered substantially.

 Table 3.5: Expansion Options Vs Storage Margin

 Daily Throughput Storage Capacity Storage / Throughput
        Base Case                   5,500 m3              30,000 m3                 5.5
        Option 1 Expansion          16,400 m3             54,500 m3                 3.3
        Option 2 Expansion          21,900 m3             54,500 m3                 2.5

Utilizing vertical storage tanks instead of horizontal tanks could allow for improvement of these storage
buffers due to their smaller footprint and being able to fit more tanks within the same land area. However,
it was determined during this study that the construction cost of vertical tanks would be significantly
higher, thereby negatively affecting the plant’s business case.

                                             Page 41 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Figure 3.16: Option 1 - 6mtpa Future Expansion Plan

Figure 3.17: Option 2 – 8mpta Future Expansion Plan

                   Page 42 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Additional considerations to be made when thinking about future plant expansion capability include the
following:
1) Incoming Utility Supply: Additional main incoming supply lines from TECO may be required if the
 initial feed is not rated for the maximum site load post-expansion. Incoming power transformers will
 be insufficient for maximum load and an additional Power Transformer will be required for each
 train bringing the total to 8. Note if incoming power transformers include capacity for expansion, it
 may be possible to limit the number of additional units to 3. Gas Insulated Switchgear will increase
 in size / number / complexity.
2) 13.8 kV Power Distribution: Each 13.8kV bus will need to be expanded to accommodate additional
 feeders and VFDs.
3) 480V Power Distribution: An expansion of switchgear and distribution equipment is necessary to
 feed the new users
4) Incoming Pipeline: It is anticipated that an additional pipeline will be required to provide the
 additional CO2 feeding the plant, or the initial trunk pipeline within the “last mile” of the plant is to
 originally be sized for greater than 2 mtpa. If an additional pipeline is to be added in future
 expansion phases, space must be allocated in any right of way utilized or created for the initial
 pipeline.
5) Traffic: Additional shipping and unloading activities may increase risk of collision or necessitate
 additional safety systems.
6) Noise: Additional fan coolers will increase noise level around the facility.
7) Proximity to LPG Terminal: The plant will expand towards the LPG Terminal and may be more
 exposed to fire and blast events emanating from that location.
8) Additional Personnel: An increase in plant size or complexity will increase the required number of
 operations personnel, which might drive need for additional shelters etc.
9) Berths: The number of required loading berths is driven by the frequency of cargo operations and
 loading rate. At 2mtpa and the base vessel loading rate of 1,250 m3/h the time between a vessel
 departing from the berth and the next one starting mooring operations is 69 hours. Increasing the
 vessel loading rate to 2,000 m3/h increases the margin to 75 hours. At 8mtpa there will be less than
 an hour between one vessel departing and the next one mooring. Increasing the vessel loading rate
 will increase this interval to 7 hours. This still provides insufficient margin and therefore a second
 berth would be recommended.

                                               Page 43 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

There is enough room at the site to comfortably add a second berth in association with future
throughput expansion. There may even be enough room to add a third berth as shown in Figure 3.18.

 Figure 3.18: Potential future berths to accommodate throughput expansion.

Expansion outside the planned initial fence line is also possible with the potential to expand total CO2
throughput in the immediate area to far beyond 8 mpta. Figure 3.19 shows parcels in dark blue that are
currently vacant and available as of the writing of this report (from Port Tampa Bay’s website). It is
conceivable that, for example, an identical hub to T-RICH could be built on the same-sized parcel directly
to the north. Or, perhaps only additional storage tanks could be placed at one of these “satellite” parcels
which would store CO2 liquefied at T-RICH. Several of the available parcels are already on deep water
channels, and there is currently a low, undeveloped area to the southwest that could be reclaimed and
developed into an even larger hub site. No engineering work has yet been put into an estimate, but it
would seem feasible that by connecting two or more of these sites together via pipelines or having them

                                             Page 44 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

operate independently as nearly-identical hubs, the total CO2 throughput in this part of the port could
exceed 15 mtpa. Thus, as the volume of captured CO2 supplied by Florida industrial emitters increases, T-
RICH can be scaled up proportionately to accommodate this growth in the Florida CCS industry .

 Figure 3.19: Available Parcels (in dark blue) Within Short Radius of T-RICH Site.

3.10 LAND ACQUISITION PLAN

As is typical with the nation’s ports, the local Port Authority owns the land and operators enter into long-
term lease agreements in order to build on the land and operate a ship terminal, warehouse, and other
cargo or vessel-related facilities. That is also the case with Port Tampa Bay, which owns, under a charter
from the State of Florida, most of the land on which marine terminals are operating in the port.
Accordingly, Aptamus expects to enter into a long term lease agreement, customarily for at least a 20-
year term, in parallel with negotiations for CO2 supply and transportation commitments with customers
and partners. Port Tampa Bay’s website states that they will consider lease terms up to 40 years.
Since PTB is a partner in this study and has confirmed that the chosen parcel can be used for this intended
cargo operation, the primary remaining critical item is to ensure that the parcel remains available in the
years it may take to solidify commercial agreements with CO2 “suppliers”. Aptamus is unlikely to commit
to a long-term tenant lease until it is highly confident that it will have a customer in place to supply CO2
to the T-RICH hub for processing which would then be the cargo for its CO2 vessel for export out of Florida.
And this timeline depends on those potential future supply customers’ plans to study carbon capture,
engage in pre-FEED and FEED studies, and then proceed into the formal EPC process. During the several
years it may take for that process to play out, the risk is that the chosen parcel gets leased to another
future tenant. However this risk is manageable since there are several other suitable parcels currently
available in the Port, and that have been available for many years. There does not currently seem to be a
high demand for such parcels and therefore no significant risk that they will all be taken in the next several

                                               Page 45 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

years. Nonetheless, Aptamus is considering offering a nominal annual payment to reserve the land during
T-RICH project development over the next few years.
When it is time to enter into a long-term lease for the land, Aptamus expects to pay a base lease rate plus
a “Wharfage Rate” which is “applicable on commodities loaded to or unloaded from vessels in a bulk
state.” See the Tampa Port Authority’s Terminal Tariff No. 14 publication on their website.15 The base rate
will be negotiated based on the location of the parcel and market conditions at the time of negotiations.
Aptamus has some experience with this since its affiliate OSG holds a long-term lease on a smaller parcel
in the port on which its fleet-support warehouse is situated. Regarding the “Wharfage Rate”, Port Tampa
Bay currently has no published rate applicable to CO2 since it has never been shipped by marine vessel in
the U.S. before. So along with the base lease rate negotiations, this wharfage rate will also be negotiated.
But the port does have a published rate for anhydrous ammonia which is mentioned elsewhere in this
report as being a similar cargo to CO2 in some ways. This report assumes that rate will also apply to CO2
and assumes that the price charged for anhydrous ammonia, at $0.417 per short ton (2,000 lbs), will also
apply to CO2.
The following table summarizes the path to commissioning the hub in terms of land “acquisition”:

 Table 3.6: Summary of the Land Acquisition Process

 Item Description

 Timeline  Approximately five years until commissioning.
  Aptamus may consider paying a nominal payment to reserve the
 land for several of those years.
  Five years may be necessary to allow area emitters to commit to
 carbon capture and then take several years for the EPC process.

4 TECHNICAL DETAILS

The total liquid CO2 storage capacity of 30,000 m³ is provided by 11 insulated horizontal pressure vessels
(each with a diameter of 10 m and a length of 32.3 m). This accommodates one full 20,000 m³ tanker and
a buffer volume of 10,000 m³ equal to 50% of a tanker load or approximately 1.5 days production at the
design rate.
The project is targeting a tanker turnaround time of 24 hours (inclusive of a non-loading time of 6 hours
for mooring operations etc.) which sets the loading pump capacity of 1,250 m³/h. With this configuration
the system should be able to load a 20,000 m3 tanker in approximately 16 hours. To provide redundancy,
2×100% loading pumps are provided (one in operation and one as a spare). The export system also
includes a metering package for liquid CO2 pumped to the vessel, and CO2 gas returning from the vessel.
Three loading arms are provided for the following services:

  Offloading Liquefied CO2 (LCO2)
  CO2 vapor return
  Spare arm capable of either service

4.1 CO 2 CONDITIONS AT RECEIPT AND DELIVERY

There are no CCS projects known as of this writing in the Tampa Bay area that are far enough along to
have established a quality or condition specification. Aptamus is in contact with several emitters who are
considering carbon capture installations at their Tampa-area facilities. But none are prepared to establish
quality specifications, or even the general condition (pressure and temperature) yet. In the meantime, for
our planning purposes, we have assumed that the CO2 will be processed by the emitter to the same quality
standard as that required by the Northern Lights project in Norway, which is summarized in Table 4.1. As
such, there is no significant purification equipment intended to be installed at T-RICH. If measurement
devices at T-RICH detect incoming CO2 that is out of spec, the T-RICH operators will instruct the emitter
to stop sending captured CO2 down the pipeline, valves will be closed, the pipeline will be vented and
purged (potentially through the vent stack on site, or back to the emitter), and the equipment at T-RICH
will be idled until the emitter can improve the quality of its outgoing CO2.
The CO2 supply to the plant through the pipeline is assumed to come from amine-based post-combustion
carbon capture technology applied to natural gas-fired equipment. The removal of nitrogen oxides (NOx)
and sulfur oxides (SOx) is typically part of the operations at upstream carbon capture plants that use
amine-based processes. Additionally, the CO2 must be dehydrated before being introduced into the
pipeline, and the concentrations of O2 and H2S must be tightly controlled in the emitter’s capture process
in accordance with PHMSA regulations to prevent issues such as corrosion and stress cracking.
In the T-RICH plant, the stripper partially separates non-condensable gases such as O2, H₂, N2, CO, Argon,
and CH4 from the rich CO2 stream. The incoming gas from the pipeline to the T-RICH plant may require
additional treatment (e.g. removal of O2/Amines and its degradation products) to meet the CO2 product
specifications. As import and export specifications are not fully defined at this stage, allowances have

                                                 Page 47 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

been made in the MEL and layouts to accommodate typical treatment equipment in future phases,
depending on the negotiations and capabilities of the plant’s customers.
