Similarly, there are no set specifications for the temperature and pressure of CO2 coming into T-RICH.
Conservatively, Aker has based their equipment sizing on a relatively low incoming pressure of 15 bar at
ambient temperature, which results in a gaseous state. This is the worst-case scenario in terms of pressure
since, as discussed in the next section, the liquefaction process requires that the pressure be raised
through 3-stage compression to 85 bar before being reduced again back down to 15 bar for storage and
loading on the vessel. If the emitter’s carbon capture plant can produce CO2 at, for example, 35 bar, that
would significantly reduce the equipment and energy requirements at T-RICH. Aptamus is aware of one
capture project in the Midwest that is planning to produce CO2 at 35 bar for transportation to a
liquefaction hub. If the emitter in the Tampa Bay Region that eventually becomes a T-RICH customer can
send their CO2 out at 35 bar, that will be a benefit to T-RICH and improve the business case.
Transporting CO2 via pipeline in gas form at 15 bar is feasible for relatively short distances such as, for
example, a pipeline to transport captured CO2 to T-RICH from an emitter 20 miles away. For longer
transport pipelines, a CO2 condition in a supercritical phase is generally preferred.
Receiving System from Rail and Trucks
CO2 is sourced from local or regional emitters. A continual stream of gaseous CO2 is assumed to be
provided via pipeline from major emitters. Other emissions are assumed to be received in liquid CO2 form
via road or rail tankers. Liquid CO2 is unloaded from rail or road cars at three common unloading stations.
Each station is equipped with a loading arm package that includes an LCO2 unloading arm, a CO2 vapor
return arm, and one spare arm capable of either service and can service both rail and road cars. Incoming
streams are analyzed and metered.
Three unloading stations are provided - each station can service both rail and truck transport. Each station
is equipped with a loading arm package that includes a LCO2 unloading arm, a CO2 vapor return arm, and
one spare arm capable of either service. Additionally, there are four loading pumps, of which three are
operational and one serves as a spare. Each pump has a capacity of 150 m³/h. The loading stations also
feature two metering packages, and each package is equipped with an analyzer to ensure that the
incoming CO2 meets product specification and can be transferred directly to storage without any
processing.
The estimated unloading time for an LCO2 truck with a capacity of 30m3 is 1 hour, including hose
connection and disconnection and product transfer, but excluding truck movement time. Using all 3
unloading stations could allow 25 trucks or approximately 750m³/day to be processed in daylight hours.
This frequent truck movement could cause traffic issues in the region and should be investigated further
during later phases of the study.
The time required to unload the 12×100m³ rail cars that can be brought onto site with one rail movement
is estimated to be 4 hours (1 hour per car including connection / disconnection). If rail operations take
another 4 hours to remove the empty cars and bring in an additional load of full tanks, assuming 24-hour
rail operations, 3,600m³/day of LCO2 can be transferred into the plant each day.

                                              Page 49 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.1.1 PIPELINE ARRIVAL PRESSURE VS LIQUEFACTION ENERGY REQUIRED
As discussed above, an assumed incoming CO2 pressure of 15 bar in gas phase is a worst-case scenario for
liquefaction. Aker conducted a brief optimization study to estimate the amount of energy that could be
saved at T-RICH if its customer(s) is able to send CO2 via pipeline at pressures higher than 15 bar.
The design is currently based upon gaseous CO2 arriving from a local distribution system at 15 barg and
up to 38°C / 100°F. This concept requires three stages of compression with intercooling to liquefy the
product. The advantage of a gaseous pipeline is that for inlet pressures below approximately 30 bar there
is no risk of liquid drop out in the pipeline upstream of T-RICH. Gas pipeline networks operating at higher
pressures have a greater risk of liquid formation during low temperatures but may reduce compression
load in the liquefaction plant.
Long and medium distance CO2 pipelines typically operate in the Dense Phase to avoid liquid formation
within the network (above the critical pressure no separate liquid phase is produced at the range of
normal ambient temperatures), with pressures let down at the inlet to the plant. Pipeline diameters can
be reduced due to compressed fluid under transport. During expansion CO2 will drop below the critical
pressure and cool meaning that liquids may be formed and must be handled within the process.
Depending on the expansion pressure CO2 can be routed to different stages of the process to capture
liquid and compress gas efficiently.
High arrival pressure can reduce plant complexity and cost but requires a dense phase pipeline network
to be installed upstream of T-RICH. Such a pipeline network is required to transport CO2 over longer
distances. By operating a dense phase pipeline network with an arrival pressure at T-RICH of 85barg or
above, the following savings can be realized
  Replace LP, MP and HP Stages of Compression with smaller LP, MP and HP Recycle Compression
  Reduce peak power consumption by ~40%
  Estimated cost saving: $10M per year in electricity costs plus equipment cost savings
This optimization option is able to achieve significant savings because by significantly raising the incoming
pressure, the Low and Medium Pressure compression stages can be eliminated, and the CO2 is instead
directed to the High Pressure flash drum upstream of the stripper column to recover liquids generated
during the expansion process. As a result, two new recycle compressor stages are required to move gas
from LP & Flash Drums to the HP Compressor Suction. Electric loads on these stages would be reduced
versus the base case’s Low and Medium Pressure compression loads – particularly in winter.

4.2 PROCESS ENGINEERING & FLOW DIAGRAM

Process engineering has focused on developing a design basis, including assumptions around composition
and throughput, followed by development of a robust flow scheme. Simulations were conducted using
HYSYS V.14 and the Soave-Redlich-Kwong (SRK) equation of state (EOS). The SRK EOS was selected based
on the results of the experimental data collected in the study by H. Li and J. Yan, titled "Evaluating cubic
equations of state for calculation of vapor-liquid equilibrium of CO2 and CO2 mixtures for CO2 capture and

                                                Page 50 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

storage processes." The reliability of five cubic equations of state was assessed in predicting the vapor-
liquid equilibrium of CO2 and binary CO2 mixtures, which include CH4, O2, H2S, N2, Ar, and SO2. The results
indicate that the SRK equation is superior in calculating the saturated pressure for the vapor-liquid
equilibrium properties of CO2. The initial phase of work focused on developing a process flow scheme for
T-RICH using in-house analogues backed up by simulation to enable sizing and layout of key equipment.

4.2.1 OVERVIEW OF THE PROCESS
The T-RICH plant is configured to address the following functional requirements:

  Receipt of CO2 from regional emitters via pipeline, railcars and trucks.

  Compression, Liquefaction and Inert Removal - to produce a marine transportable liquid CO2
 product
  Storage and Tanker Loading
This is illustrated in the diagram below.

 Figure 4.1: Main Process Block Diagram

4.2.2 POTENTIAL CAPACITY IN COMPRESSION AND LIQUEFACTION PROCESS
The facility includes an allowance for 7 days of downtime per year. At this stage the preliminary equipment
sizing incorporates standard allowances such as:

  A 20% allowance for vessel sizing
  Conservative assumptions regarding efficiencies when sizing motors and other equipment
This approach has allowed the design to progress and support a more efficient design with a low risk of
growth in footprint, weight or power consumption. As equipment specifications are finalized, margins can
be reduced and replaced by vendor performance guarantees and actual performance data. It is currently
anticipated that there is at least a 5% potential increase in throughput, which could translate to an
additional 14 days of production capacity. In the next phase of design, the assumption of a 7-day

                                              Page 51 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

shutdown can be revisited to confirm this increased capacity in the design without requiring major
changes.
During periods of high ambient temperatures, the efficiency of air-cooled heat exchangers decreases as
the temperature rises. The compressors, which are the most energy-intensive equipment in the
liquefaction process, require additional power to maintain the necessary pressure and cooling levels
under these conditions.

4.2.3      CO 2 VENTING
The liquefaction plant will be designed to minimize CO2 venting and emissions into the atmosphere,
aiming to essentially eliminate the need for venting during normal steady-state operation. The only
continuous vent anticipated is from the inert removal unit, which contains approximately 0.27% CO2
emissions per mass weight of LCO2 produced. As the tankers are dedicated to this service, CO2 in heel will
meet export specification, therefore during the loading of LCO2, the returning CO2 vapor, with a maximum
flow rate of 1250 m³/h from the carrier, will be redirected back to the compression system. Loading
activities to the tanker shall be stopped when the liquefaction plant is not in operation.
Both the liquefaction plant and the storage units will be equipped with pressure control valves (PCVs) as
the primary protection against overpressure, and pressure safety valves (PSVs) as secondary protection.
CO2 vapor will be released to the atmosphere when the pressure in the system exceeds normal operating
levels. In an emergency situation, pressure relief will be managed by the PCVs and the PSVs. Based on
previous projects it is anticipated that the CO2 released from the PSVs can be vented directly to the
atmosphere through short tailpipes, maintaining high velocities (greater than 100 m/s) to ensure effective
dispersion. These protection assumptions would be confirmed during later design phases.
A comprehensive vent and relief study will be conducted in the next engineering phase. This will include
defining the key venting scenarios, designing the CO2 vent system, and conducting dispersion analysis to
determine the necessary height and location for the vent stack. Similar analyses on other projects have
not demonstrated a requirement for a dedicated knockout (KO) drum and vent stack. However, as this
has not been the case on all reference projects, a closed CO2 vent system comprising a vent KO drum and
an elevated vent stack, which will safely dispose of discharged CO2 at a designated safe location, has been
included for this phase.

4.2.4      EXPORT
The facility features an export system consisting of 2x100% LCO2 Ship Loading Pumps (one in operation
and one as a spare) with a pumping capacity of 1,250m³/h at an estimated head of 10 bar / 100m. These
loading pumps transfer liquid CO2 via the export metering package to the loading arm, while displaced
CO2 vapor from the ship will return to the plant via the vapor return loading arm and the vapor return
metering package. A common spare loading arm is also provided. See Figure 4.2.
CO2 vapor displaced during filling of the storage vessels, any evaporated CO2 from the storage units, and
excess CO2 vapor returned from the ship during offloading will be recycled for liquefaction. During periods

                                             Page 52 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

where the liquefaction plan is not in operation - evaporated CO2 from the storage units and excess vapor
returned from the ship during loading will be vented to atmosphere.
If necessary, pressurized CO2 vapor can be generated from a liquid CO2 take-off downstream of the ship
loading pumps using an electrically heated vaporizer. The header can be used for pressurized drainage of
equipment, vessels, and piping prior to maintenance and for pressurization following maintenance.

 Figure 4.2 – Simplified Process Flow Diagram – Storage & Export

The sections that follow outline activities / assessments undertaken during the pre-FEED design, and key
discipline findings. In the FEED phase the sizing, properties, etc. of some equipment can be optimized to
provide a better fit for the respective application. As far as practicable we have included American
equipment manufacturers and suppliers in our lists of potential equipment suppliers.
The following sections describe the main equipment types required as part of this development and some
representative suppliers. Final selection of equipment types including material selection will be carried
out in FEED.

                                             Page 53 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.3.1 CO₂ COMPRESSION
The plant consists of two liquefaction trains, each with three stages of compression. Given the plant duty,
centrifugal compressors have been considered as they offer the best all round solution, with two options
being:
 1. In-line Compressors:

  Generally more expensive option

  Highly reliable

  Good access and maintainability

  Reduced ability to optimize speeds due to each stage driven from the same shaft
 2. Integrally Geared Compressors:

  Typically cheaper than in-line
  Less reliable
  Each stage can be optimized due to running on its own pinion
  Sensitive to vibrational issues, especially with variable speed drives
  Prone to surge i.e. each stage could require an anti-surge valve
Typical Integrally Geared and In-Line Compressor skids are shown below.
As part of the next FEED phase, evaluation of the two options should be part of the equipment technical
solution review.
The compressors shall be designed, manufactured, and tested in accordance with API 617. Typical
compressor suppliers used by Entr are:
  Siemens
  Mitsubishi Heavy Industries

  MAN-Turbo

  Elliott Turbo
  Baker Hughes

                                              Page 54 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.3: Siemens In-Line Compressor Skid

 Figure 4.4: MAN Integrally Geared Compressor

Variable Frequency Drive (VFD) use on compressors provides significant benefits like reduced energy
consumption, improved system efficiency, minimized motor inrush current during startup, better
pressure control, extended component lifespan, and overall operational cost savings by matching
production to actual demand. VFD's are suitable for this project especially due to the fluctuating demand.
Key benefits of VFDs:

  Energy Efficiency: The main benefit is energy savings, as the compressor operates at variable
 speeds based on demand, cutting power usage when full capacity isn't required.

                                             Page 55 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  Reduced Startup Current: VFDs offer soft starts, reducing the high inrush current spikes that
 happen when a compressor motor starts at full voltage, which can extend motor life.

  Precise Pressure Control: A VFD adjusts motor speed to keep pressure steady, eliminating
 fluctuations.

  Lower Maintenance Costs: A VFD's smoother operation reduces component stress, leading to less
 wear and lower maintenance needs.

  Improved System Reliability: Maintaining consistent pressure and reducing motor stress improves
 the compressor system's reliability.

  Reduced Noise: VFD's are able to operate at a reduced noise level.

4.3.2 HEAT EXCHANGERS
Process data concerning each application will be used to assist in determining the type of heat exchanger
to be utilized. Types include shell and tube exchanger, air-cooled exchanger, plate & frame exchanger and
brazed/compact exchanger, considering the following inputs: fouling, pressure drop, footprint, and
materials of construction.
Pressure parts of shell and tube heat exchangers made of carbon, ferritic alloy, and austenitic steels shall
be designed, manufactured, inspected, and tested in accordance with ASME VIII. The design of shell and
tube heat exchangers shall also comply with ASME, TEMA, and API 660. Plate and frame heat exchangers
shall be designed, manufactured, inspected, and tested in accordance with API 662 Pt1. Brazed aluminum
plate and frame heat exchangers shall be designed, manufactured, inspected and tested in accordance
with API 662 Pt2.
Typical suppliers of heat transfer equipment include:
  Alfa Laval
  NOV

4.3.3 INSULATED STORAGE UNITS
Liquid CO2 is typically stored in two ways, bullet shaped pressure vessels or spherical units. Bullet shaped
pressure vessels were the focus of this study due to the high costs associated with spherical tank
fabrication and construction, as well as site layout improvements. During the initial phases the focus was
on vertical vessels, but due to the availability of larger horizontal pressure vessels, these were the focus
of the second phase of the work. The storage units will be insulated and fabricated from low temperature
carbon steel, which is being driven by the liquid CO2 storage pressure and temperature. The pressure
vessels shall be manufactured, inspected, and tested in accordance with ASME VIII. See Section 4.9.1 for
additional information on the alternative storage arrangements considered.
As part of this concept study, several potential manufacturers were engaged for high level / budgetary
supply quotations.
 1. Idesa: Located in Asturias, Spain, Idesa has provided a proposal for 11 off horizontal pressure
 vessels. This option is the basis of our design, providing for the preferred layout and improved
 constructability. This manufacturer is familiar to Entr / Aker Solutions having delivered Liquid CO2
 storage units previously.
 2. Chart Industries: Located in Theodore, Alabama, USA, has provided a proposal for 17 horizontal
 pressure vessels. 17 tanks were necessary to reach the total desired hub storage capacity of
 30,000 m3 due to size limitations at Chart’s manufacturing facility, whereas Idesa can provide a

                                              Page 57 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 smaller number of larger tanks. This option has been screened out of this phase of the study as
 the increased number of units was not ideal from construction, layout, and operation aspects.
 This option will be reevaluated during future design phases.
 3. American Tank and Vessel: This company is very interested in the project and has ample
 experience building and delivering large, specialized tanks throughout two dozen countries. They
 have several manufacturing facilities in the southern U.S. and proposed building spherical tanks
 onsite rather than transporting spheres or bullet tanks from their manufacturing base. However,
 they have no experience with liquified CO2 and their engineering and materials selection needs
 further study.
 4. Trans Tech Energy: Unable to manufacture vessels to project requirements.
 5. OEG Cryogenics: Unable to manufacture vessels to project requirements
 6. RFCC: Located in Heibei Province, China. Maximum size vessel RFCC can manufacture is 500 m3,
 resulting in over 60 pressure vessels being required to satisfy the T-RICH storage volume
 requirement. This would increase the potential for leak paths and increase the complexity of
 installation.

4.3.5 LOADING ARMS
Loading arms are required for rail car and truck unloading and loading, as well as on the berth for loading
CO2 to the tankers. As the technology and marine transportation industry is relatively new, special
attention will be paid to qualifications and references pertaining to the hoses, sealing systems, and safe
design. Rail and road transport of CO2 is more established and there are existing technologies that can
load / unload cargos onto standard rail / road transport systems.
Rail car transportation of pressurized liquid CO2 is well established in the USA, and standards exist for rail
cars and associated fittings. As a result, this assessment has focused on overhead unloading systems in

                                               Page 59 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

line with the Class-105 prohibition on bottom outlets and washouts in these pressurized insulated rail
cars.

could be challenged during FEED based upon the anticipated ratio of rail and truck unloading operations,
as a savings may be obtained by having dedicated rail and road unloading arms with reduced reach and
numbers tailored to the frequency of the various operations.

which will be required for T-RICH's location.

4.4 ELECTRICAL DESIGN

The primary focus for the Electrical discipline in this study phase has been the development of Electrical
Load Lists (ELL) and a Single Line Diagram (SLD), input to the MEL, input to the Site layout, and participation
in the HAZID.

4.4.1 MAIN POWER SUPPLY
Main power to the Site will be supplied via a high voltage supply from the local utility grid. The supply
voltage and specific utility substation that will be used to supply power is still being evaluated by the utility
company and will be confirmed in the next study phase. Supply voltage options include 13.2 kV, 69 kV,
138 kV and 230 kV. The design presented here is aligned with the one of the two most likely outcomes of
138kV. The other most likely voltage is 69 kV. The impact of changes in supply voltage would be addressed
in subsequent project phases.
The current base case design includes high voltage switchgear that will receive the incoming power supply
and distribute it to two main power transformers in a parallel configuration (A and B) for 2 x 50%
operation. The transformers will step the utility supply voltage down to 13.8 kV for further distribution
throughout the site, with their dual configuration allowing the facility to continue operation at reduced
capacity should one transformer be out of service. The neutral point of the transformer secondary
windings will be resistance grounded through a neutral grounding resistor that is rated to limit fault
current to 20 Amps during a ground fault event. The transformers are intended to be liquid filled with an
environmentally friendly, natural or synthetic based ester.
The voltage rating of the outdoor switchgear and the primary voltage rating of the two main power
transformers will be dependent on the selected supply voltage to the site; however, final selection of this
voltage will not have a significant impact on the overall system design since the required switchgear and
transformers are available in the various supply voltage options and will not affect the proposed
equipment configuration.
The main switchgear, power transformers, and neutral grounding resistors for the main incoming power
supply are located at the outdoor substation discussed in Section 4.5.

4.4.2 HIGH VOLTAGE POWER DISTRIBUTION
The two main incoming power transformers discussed in in the previous section are connected to the
13.8kV switchgear located in the electrical room of the main site building, where it is further distributed
throughout the site.
The switchgear is configured in a bus A and bus B arrangement, with a main incoming circuit breaker for
each bus section and an interconnecting bus tie circuit breaker between the two bus sections. This
configuration will allow the facility to continue operation at reduced capacity should one section of the
switchgear or one of the upstream transformers be out of service.
The 13.8 kV switchgear will supply power directly to high voltage and downstream low voltage (LV)
consumers, including:

                                                Page 62 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  CO2 Compressors

  CO2 Offloading Pumps

  Vaporizer Heater
  480 V power distribution (transformers and switchboards) for low voltage process and utilities
 consumers

4.4.3 LOW VOLTAGE POWER DISTRIBUTION
The low voltage 480 V switchgear is configured in a bus A and bus B arrangement, with a main incoming
circuit breaker for each bus section and an interconnecting bus tie circuit breaker between the two bus
sections. Each main circuit breaker receives power from a dedicated upstream 13.8/0.48 kV power
distribution transformer. The low voltage switchgear and associated power transformers located in the
electrical room of the main site building.
The power distribution transformers supplying the 480 V switchgear are rated to supply power to both
bus to allow the facility to continue operation should the alternate transformer be out of service. The
neutral point of the transformer secondary windings will be resistance grounded through a neutral
grounding resistor that is rated to limit fault current to 100 Amps during a ground fault event. The
transformers are intended to be dry-type, with natural and forced air cooling to achieve the required
power rating.
The 480 V switchgear will supply power directly to 480 V consumers, primarily motor driven loads, and
supply power to two main 208/120 V small power distribution panel boards via small power distribution
transformers. The main small power distribution boards will in turn supply small power consumers
throughout the facility. The neutral point of the transformer secondary windings will be solidly grounded,
with no neutral grounding resistor used. This small power design will be further evaluated in the next
study phase to determine if remotely located sub-distribution panel boards outside the main electrical
room will allow the small power design to be optimized (e.g., reduced power lengths) or whether the
current design of centrally located panels only is preferred.
The 480 V switchgear will supply power directly to low voltage (LV) consumers, including:

  CO2 transfer pumps
  CO2 loading pumps

  Loading Arm HPUs
  Pumps and fans for the cooling systems associated with CO2 compressors

  Air compressors and air dryers
  HVAC equipment
  208/120 V small power distribution (transformers and panel boards) for small power consumers

                                             Page 63 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The small power distribution system will supply lighting, small power receptacles, system control panels,
UPS and other utilities and process related small power consumers.

4.4.4 UPS POWER DISTRIBUTION
Battery backed UPS will supply power to consumers that require an uninterruptible power supply. The
preliminary UPS design comprises two redundant UPS (A and B) located in the electrical room of the main
site building. The preliminary UPS and battery rack information currently included in the MEL is based on
a per-unit rating of 50 kVA; however, this will be evaluated further in the next study phase as the design
matures and UPS load demand is better defined. The specific battery technology will also be evaluated
further in the next study phase to determine whether more traditional valve-regulated lead acid batteries
or lithium-ion batteries are preferred.
Each UPS will supply a main distribution panel board, which will in turn supply the various electrical
consumers that require UPS power. The typical consumers connected to the UPS system are process
control cabinets, telecoms control cabinets, public address, general alarm equipment, fire detection
system cabinets, aviation obstruction lights, and variable frequency drive control power. A detailed UPS
load list will be developed in a future study phase, which will be used for final dimensioning of the UPS
and battery banks based on the selected battery technology, calculated operating load, required runtime
and applicable regulations.

4.4.5 ELECTRICALLY DRIVEN MOTOR LOADS
Most of the electrical consumers for the facility will be motor driven. This includes the main process
equipment (e.g., CO2 compressors) and associated auxiliary equipment and the various utilities systems.
Several of those consumers require speed control, which will be achieved using variable frequency motor
drives. The remainder of the motor driven loads will be supplied direct-on-line (i.e., full voltage) by the
supplying switchgear.
The rating, location, and supply voltage of each VFD will be determined by the rating of the motor load
that it is supplying. The preliminary design is based on VFDs for loads less than 30 kW located integrally
within the supplying switchgear / motor control center, while VFDs for larger loads will be standalone
(freestanding) units located in the electrical room. The largest VFDs are for the CO2 Compressors and will
be powered from the main 13.8 kV switchgear via dedicated VFD input transformers, which are in the
outdoor substation. The VFD input transformers are intended to be liquid filled with an environmentally
friendly, natural, or synthetic based ester.
The specific variable frequency drive technology for the CO2 Compressor motors will be further evaluated
in the next study phase. The current design is based on a voltage source inverter (VSI) controller, while a
load commutated inverter (LCI) controller is also a technically feasible option. The final selection of the
drive technology will consider power quality, electrical efficiency, CAPEX cost, OPEX cost including
maintenance requirements, etc.
The CO2 Compressor motors are intended to be synchronous due to their higher efficiency versus
asynchronous motors, which will reduce electrical losses and operating cost.

                                             Page 64 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.5 GENERAL SITE ARRANGEMENT

Pre-FEED layout activities initially focused on gathering inputs to design basis and obtaining lessons
learned through review of similar projects carried out by Entr. As the PFD and MEL developed, the site
layout was defined accordingly and included in a 3D model utilizing Aveva E3D version 3.1 with overviews
provided in Navisworks 2018 along with overlays within Google Earth. The following sections will provide
some additional details to each area of the site and parameters or logic used to help develop the layout.

4.5.1 OVERVIEW
The site location for the Tampa Regional Intermodal CO2 Hub (T-RICH) is on Pendola Point within the
boundaries of Port Tampa Bay as previously shown in Figs. 3.1, 3.2 and 3.3. Preliminary PFD's and MEL
were developed to help determine process and equipment size requirements. This information along with
other similar historical projects were utilized to start the development of a site layout while investigating
multiple potential layout options suited for the site.
Based on extensive discussions and feedback from process, mechanical, safety, electrical, construction,
and client updates, it has been recommended to design a facility that minimizes the use of available real
estate while providing ample provisions for safety, efficient process operations, maintenance, and
construction requirements. The recommended option for this project phase is a facility incorporating 11
large horizontal pressure vessels (10 meters in diameter and 32.3 meters in length) with two 50%
compression / liquefaction trains supplied with CO2 via pipeline, rail, and truck. An overview of the site
layout can be seen in Fig. 4.12, with closeups of certain areas in later figures. The remainder of Section
4.5 will provide further details around the options and logic used to help develop the current site
arrangement and identify additional work to be carried out during the FEED phase.
There are many factors which have to be taken into consideration when laying out a CO2 receiving,
compression, liquefaction, storage, and export facility. Safety considerations for construction and
operating personnel, adjacent populated areas and facilities are of top priority. Finding a balance to satisfy
not only safety, but construction and operational activities while being economically viable for the client
and with minimal environmental impact is of great importance and is the end goal to have a successful
project. It has been determined during the Pre-FEED phase that such a layout can be achieved based on
available data received to date. Subsequent phases of the project (FEED, Detail Design) will help further
refine the design and data to ensure viability of the project.

                                               Page 65 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.12: T-RICH Plot Plan

4.5.2 CO 2 STORAGE TANKS
During the Pre-FEED phase, it was determined that selecting 11 horizontal pressure vessels reduces the
risk of potential CO2 leak paths, minimizes connecting pipework, and simplifies installation from a
construction perspective. The initial plan of using 36 vertical tanks posed higher risks of leak paths and
greater complexity in piping and constructability. Therefore, the 11 horizontal units have been chosen and
are located on the southern side of the site facility.

                                             Page 66 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The CO2 storage units are arranged parallel to each other, simplifying piping arrangements by maintaining
symmetry in the piping to and from each pressure vessel. This layout also allows for flexibility in piping to
reduce stress within pipe routes and at vessel nozzle locations. Common pipe racks or sleeper supports
can be used for two or more adjacent units. Multiple pressure relief systems will be located at the upper
level of the tanks, and this arrangement will simplify the design of elevated access platforms, providing
common access points for multiple adjacent tanks. Fig. 4.13 shows the CO2 storage units with pipe racks
between adjacent tanks.

 Figure 4.13: T-RICH Storage Tank Arrangement

4.5.3 COMPRESSION AND LIQUEFACTION
There are two 50% trains of compression and liquefaction located north of the CO2 storage tank area.
Each train includes a three-stage compressor with 1st, 2nd, and 3rd stage inlet scrubbers and discharge
fan coolers. To support modularization it has been assumed that the three stages of compression will be
performed in a single unit with multiple compression stages driven from a single motor. Subsequent
phases of the project will investigate integrally geared compressors and more traditional back to back
stages.
The Cold Recovery Heat Exchanger, LP, MP, and HP Flash Separators, along with the Stripper, Reflux Drum,
Reboiler, and Condenser for each train, are situated just north of the compressors and scrubbers to ensure
a sequential flow of the process, minimizing the need to reverse the process direction. The Liquid CO2
transfer pumps to the storage tanks are located just west of the LP Flash Separator, beneath the Cooler
Fan pipe rack area.

                                              Page 67 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

A minimum spacing of 3 meters between equipment has been maintained to allow for piping, cable ways,
instrumentation, maintenance access, and mobile equipment access if necessary. Some equipment, such
as scrubbers, will have larger spacing to accommodate the required minimum inlet or outlet piping
straight lengths for proper function. Certain equipment, like compressors, will require greater clearance
for maintenance access, which has been accounted for in the layout. Fig. 4.14 provides a view and
identification of the various pieces of equipment associated with the compression and liquefaction train
area.
During the FEED phase, optimization of this area should consider the possibility of mirroring trains A and
B and potentially utilizing a common pipe rack with fan cooling area. This will require consultation with
potential operators to ensure they are comfortable with mirrored facilities from an operational
perspective.

 Figure 4.14: Compression and Liquefaction Layout

4.5.4 METERING ANALYZER PACKAGES
Metering will be provided for the incoming CO2 pipeline, incoming rail and truck deliveries, export to ship,
and return vapor from the ship. The metering skids have been strategically placed near the CO2 supply
sources or outgoing CO2 products. The underground CO2 pipeline supply to the site will have a metering
package located near where the pipeline surfaces, just west of the CO2 storage tanks and east of the utility
area (see Section 4.5.8 Utilities). The CO2 supply from rail or truck will have their metering packages
situated just east of the loading arms area. The CO2 export and vapor return to and from the ship will have

                                              Page 68 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

metering packages positioned just west of liquefaction train 'A', near the north boundary area. Associated
transfer pumps will also be located close to the metering skids. Fig. 4.15 provides a view of the metering
packages and transfer pumps.

 Figure 4.15: Metering and Analyzer Package Locations

4.5.5 CO 2 EXPORT SYSTEM
This facility features an export system consisting of 2x100% LCO2 Ship Loading Pumps, LCO2 metering
package, a CO2 gas return metering package, a vaporizer heater, and three loading arms on the ship berth
for the following services:

  Offloading LCO2

                                             Page 69 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  CO2 vapor return

  Spare arm capable of either service
Based on similar projects, the loading arms will require platform access and room for maintenance access
with the aid of mobile equipment (i.e. scissor lift, cranes, etc.) (see Fig. 4.17). The development and
operational requirements of the area around the berth loading arms will be further refined during the
FEED phase.
There will be a pipe rack network traveling from the CO2 storage tanks to the Berth offloading arms. There
are areas of the pipe rack system which will have to be elevated to allow passage of roadways below. The
pipe rack system will typically be 5 meters above any roadway or access point to provide ample clearance
for larger vehicular traffic. Once the pipe rack reaches the northern boundary of the site it will travel due
west towards the berth just south of and parallel to an existing LPG line.

4.5.6 CO 2 TRAIN & TRUCK RECEIVING STATIONS
There are three CO2 unloading stations available which can service both rail and truck transport. Each
station is equipped with a loading arm package that includes an LCO2 unloading arm, a CO2 vapor return
arm, and one spare arm capable of either service. The loading arms have a 20-meter reach radius which
can access up to four rail cars when parked on site. Additionally, there are four onloading pumps, of which
three are operational and one serves as a spare. There are two metering packages equipped with an
analyzer (ref. 4.5.4 Metering Analyzer Packages).
The loading arm packages will have to be elevated to maximize the reach over top of the rail car areas.
Elevation of the loading arms will be reviewed further during FEED to assess whether site grading and/or
elevated structure support is required or preferred. The four onloading pumps are positioned close to the
loading arm area to maximize available suction head and reduce liquid drop out. The metering packages
will be located just east of the loading pump area prior to entering the pipe rack system. See Fig. 4.18
which highlights the various CO2 train and truck receiving equipment components.

4.5.7 CO 2 VENT STACK
A 50-meter-high vent stack has been positioned on the north side of the facility, just east of the
liquefaction train area. Currently, a 30-meter radius exclusion zone surrounds the vent stack, ensuring no
plant equipment is within this area to prevent personnel from entering. It is anticipated that the height
and exclusion zone will likely decrease once additional dispersion analysis is completed during the FEED
phase. For now, the height and restriction zone are considered a conservative worst-case requirement
(see Fig. 4.19).
The vent stack at its current height may pose a challenge to Peter O Knight Airport, which is 1.5 miles
away, and MacDill Air Force Base, about 5 miles away. Port Tampa Bay engineers believe it will eventually
be approved. Nonetheless, efforts will be made to reduce its height in the FEED stage.

A site utilities area has been designated just north of the main administration building and west of the

packages, two air receivers, and a nitrogen bottle rack. Additionally, a reserved area to the west is
provided for potential future needs, such as handling dehydration and removing O2/amines and their
degradation products. This reserved space is intended for the possible future treatment of incoming CO2
gas if necessary.
Other utility items not located in the main utility area include the Closed Drains Vessel and Vent KO drum,
which have been positioned closer to the process area and vent stack, respectively. Fig. 4.20 shows the
various locations for utility equipment.

4.5.9 SITE ROADWAYS
Roads and access ways should facilitate easy movement for mobile equipment during construction,
operation, maintenance, firefighting, and emergency evacuation. Additionally, roadways should be at
least 3 meters away from processing equipment and road edges to provide a buffer against potential
vehicle collisions. Typically, primary access roads to and from the site will be 6 meters wide, while
secondary roads will be 4 meters wide. All these requirements have been met with the current site
arrangement. Certain areas will require a larger turn radius to accommodate semi-trucks or 25 m³ CO2
trucks (e.g., main access to the site, adjacent to train/truck offloading arms). Port Tampa Bay’s civil
engineering subcontractor has performed a swept path analysis for appropriately sized tanker trucks
which will be incorporated into the roadway design in the FEED stage.
The plans and figures provided herein depict a few options for secondary roadways or evacuation paths
out of the site in the event of an emergency. Some possibilities have been incorporated into the site

                                            Page 74 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

layout, with the primary access located on the southwest side and the secondary access on the southeast
side. Fig. 4.21 shows a plan view of the site highlighting the potential roadway access points and on-foot
egress points. Future considerations for alternative road and foot access and egress points will be
reviewed during subsequent project phases as the design and layout mature. Coordination with offsite
utility providers and adjacent industrial site operators will become increasingly important during the FEED
phase to optimize site road access points if necessary. The northeast corner of the site is a potential
secondary road access point as it will tie into a north/south “future access road” on the east side of the
site shown on existing PTB site plans.

 Figure 4.21: T-RICH Site Roads and Access Points

4.5.10 RAIL ACCESS
The feasibility of delivering CO2 to the site via 100 cubic meter rail tanks has been assessed during this
Pre-FEED phase. An existing rail line south of the site can be used to provide rail access just west of the
primary roadway. The site can accommodate twelve DOT class-105 pressure tank rail cars on two parallel
tracks, accessible via three offloading arms for either rail tanks or trucks.
The on-site rail tracks will be perpendicular to the existing rail line south of the site, necessitating a 90-
degree turn for access. The standard rail gauge in the US is 4 feet 8.5 inches, with a minimum turn radius
of 88 meters for non-general service. This minimum turn radius has been adopted to maximize the
number of rail cars on site. The turn radius is primarily determined by the operating speed and the train's
ability to handle curves. Preliminary indication indicates that 25,800 gal class-105 tank cars can
accommodate curves tighter than the 88m base estimate. As a result an additional six rail tanks could
potentially be accommodated on a third parallel track, which can be further explored during the FEED

                                               Page 75 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

phase in conjunction with the minimum turn radius verification with the rail line operator (CSX) to ensure
it is adequate for the size of rail cars and the speed of loading and unloading.
On the north side of the rail car area there will be a pipe rack that travels to and from the Berth. This rack
will be protected from collisions with the end rail cars by track rail stops.
Fig. 4.22 provides a plan view of the site, highlighting the rail access area.

 Figure 4.22: T-RICH Rail Line Configuration

4.5.11 SITE BUILDINGS
The current site layout includes two main buildings. The security building is situated at the primary site
entrance, providing gated access via two roadways. The second building is the main site building, which
will serve as an administrative, electrical, and workshop area. The workshop area, located on the lower
level, will feature a two-bay garage to accommodate larger vehicles and maintenance equipment, with
overhead crane capabilities. The electrical area will primarily house large VFDs and transformers on the
lower level, with switchgear and power distribution boards on the second level. Variable Frequency Drives
for compressor motors of this size are quite substantial. Additional space has been allocated for future
expansion of electrical equipment. The administrative area, located on the second level, will include 8'x8'
cubicles, a boardroom, kitchen, and washroom facilities. The upper level of the administrative building
will also function as a temporary safe refuge in the event of an incident, such as a CO2 leak.
South of the administrative building is a sub-station area. This area will accommodate the main utility
incomer switchgear, input transformer for VFD compressors, main incoming power transformers, and
neutral ground resistors. These components do not need to be housed indoors and are therefore located
outside the main administration/electrical building.

 Figure 4.23: T-RICH Site Buildings

4.5.12 MARINE BERTH
As stated elsewhere in this report, one of the main reasons the proposed site was originally chosen for T-
RICH was its access to existing Berth 30 in Port Tampa Bay. Technically, PTB has designated the shoreline
immediately north of the site as Berth 29, indicating a potential future physical installation of a berth
there. But there is no physical berth structure located there presently (though there could be in the event
of a T-RICH expansion – see Section 3.9). Berth 30, which is just to the west of the proposed site but which
the site has legal access to, is of relatively new design and in excellent condition. It is used infrequently to
load and/or discharge 1) liquefied petroleum gas from the LPG operation on the site immediately to the
east of T-RICH, and 2) bulk materials (e.g. cement) from the site immediately to the west. LPG is
transferred between the plant and the berth via pipelines along the north edge of the T-RICH site. These
pipelines are noted in Section 4.10 as being an obstacle to site construction that must be considered and
protected during construction. Once T-RICH is in operation, these LPG pipelines should not be a concern
as they are just outside the property boundary.
Since Aptamus is already quite far along in the design of the CO2 ship intended to serve the T-RICH hub, it
was possible to lay the CO2 ship alongside the existing berth to evaluate its suitability to accommodate
the CO2 ship. There are several considerations, including:
 1. The ship’s cargo loading manifold piping lines up with the CO2 loading arms on shore.
 2. The flat side of the ship can make contact with at least 3 fenders near the fore, mid and aft ends
 of the ship.
 3. Mooring bitts on shore or on dolphins are in suitable locations to receive the following 12 total
 mooring lines from the ship:
 a. Two bow lines
 b. Two breast lines forward
 c. Two breast lines aft
 d. Two spring lines forward
 e. Two spring lines aft
 f. Two stern lines
When Aptamus’ CO2 ship design was laid alongside the existing berth in CAD, it was found that it was a
good match for the existing berth. The overall length of the berth is more than adequate for Aptamus’
ship. However, it was also determined that there is not enough room on the main dolphin, which houses
the LPG hose tower and hose handling crane, to add the three required CO2 loading arms. The decision
was made to add an additional dolphin which will be dedicated to the CO2 loading arms. See Figure 4.24,
which depicts a bird’s eye view of the berth as it exists today. Figure 4.25 depicts the new CO2 Loading
Arm platform and CO2 pipeline. Figure 4.26 depicts the mooring line arrangement of the CO2 ship at Berth
30, ensuring that the ship can be securely moored during CO2 transfer operations.

                                                Page 78 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Figure 4.24: Existing Berth 30 Before Modifications

 Figure 4.25: Berth 30 After Modifications

                  Page 79 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.26: CO2 Ship Mooring Arrangement at Berth 30

The allowable ship draft at Berth 30 is another important consideration when evaluating it for
compatibility with the CO2 ship design. The CO2 ship with a capacity of 20,000 tons of CO2 is expected to
have a fully-laden draft of less than 30 feet. By comparison, the Tampa Bay Pilots Association states an
allowable draft at Berth 30 of 43’-6”16. This allows for an under keel clearance (UKC) of 13’-6” which is
substantially more than the typical minimum required 2’-0” UKC. Should expansion of T-RICH ever justify
the construction of a second marine berth to accommodate more CO2 ships which would be required for
a greater overall transportation system throughput, such a berth could be constructed at the open
shoreline along the north side of the parcel designated as “Berth 29”. The Tampa Bay Pilots Association
designates the maximum vessel draft in this area as 34’-07”, still comfortably deeper than the maximum
expected draft of the CO2 ship of less than 30’-0”.
The “capacity” of a marine berth is dictated by the number of ships that can be handled there per week,
which is primarily limited by the speed at which the ship can be loaded or discharged given the type of
cargo transfer that occurs there. With container terminals or bulk aggregate terminals, shoreside cranes
will typically be one of the primary limiting factors to how fast the ship can get in and out and vacate the
berth for the next ship. But for berths where fluids are transferred, such as liquefied CO2, it is primarily
the sizes of pumps and piping which dictate how fast the fluid can be transferred on or off the vessel. T-
RICH has been designed with ship loading piping and pumps so that the ship can be loaded in less than 18
hours. When the required time is added to that to tie up the vessel, hook up cargo hoses, and make all
preparations for the fluid transfer, and the reverse of that process at the conclusion of cargo transfer, the
CO2 ship should have to spend no more than 24 hours at T-RICH’s berth. This means, hypothetically, that
Berth 30 could handle up to 7 CO2 ships per week. Since only 2 ships per week are required to meet the
hub’s annual throughput goal of 2 million tons per annum (mtpa), Berth 30 has more than enough capacity
to meet that goal and even accommodate future expansion to the hub with associated increases in annual
throughput. If two ships per week are adequate to meet an annual hub throughput of 2 mtpa, and the
berth is capable of handling up to 7 ships per week, the berth should be capable of supporting an increased
hub throughput of over 6 mtpa.

16
 https://www.tampabaypilots.com/channels-berths

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.6 OTHER REQUIRED UTILITIES

4.6.1 INSTRUMENT AIR
The instrument air system consists of 2x2,000 m3/h air compressor packages, 2x2,000 m3/h air dryer
packages, and 2x100 m3 instrument air receivers. The air receiver is designed to provide a 15-minute
supply of instrument air in the event of a compressor shutdown. The spare air compressor, air dryer, and
air receiver will be utilized for purging purposes.

4.6.2 NITROGEN
There are no specific nitrogen consumers identified in this study phase. However, a small nitrogen flow
may be required by the CO2 analyzer package(s). When needed, nitrogen will be supplied to consumers
from nitrogen bottles.

4.6.3 POTABLE WATER
It is expected that a potable water supply will be sourced from a connection to the local water main.
Identified potable water users include safety showers, eye wash stations and utility stations, and building
sprinkler systems.
It is likely that some fire hydrants will be required at key locations on the site (refer to Section 8.3.4, Fire
Fighting and Water Supply). PTB’s subcontractor GHD has made provisions for a single fire hydrant in a
central area between the liquefaction trains and storage tanks, fed by a dedicated new 6” pipe tapped
into the street main line, as shown in their plans in Appendix A.

                                                Page 81 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.6.4 CLOSED DRAINS
Specific drainage requirements for the liquefaction plant will be identified during future engineering
stages, however to ensure liquids within the process and storage systems can be displaced safely,
provision for a Closed Drains Drum is included within the liquefaction plant design.

4.7 MODES OF OPERATION

The T-RICH plant will be capable of operating under a range of conditions as outlined in the following
sections.

4.7.1 NORMAL OPERATION
In this mode the plant operates as outlined in Sections 4.1 and 4.2 with CO2 continuously supplied through
a pipeline, or in liquid form by road / rail cars. The CO2 is then compressed, cooled, and liquefied before
being transferred to insulated pressure vessels for later loading onto marine tankers.
Displaced CO2 gas from the storage units is recompressed and mixed with the incoming CO2 gas from the
pipeline and liquefied. Inert components that are separated from the CO2 in the stripper are vented into
the atmosphere through a vent stack, along with a small amount of CO2.
No other continuous venting of CO2 is anticipated during normal operations.
During later project phases, control of the CO2 gathering network will be integrated with the T-RICH plant
design to ensure that the appropriate communication and control protocols are incorporated into the
design of both systems.

4.7.2 NORMAL OPERATION WITH LOADING OF CO 2 TO MARINE TANKER
In this mode the liquefaction plant operates as outlined above.
Twice a week, CO2 is transferred from the storage units to the CO2 carrier ship for shipment across the
Gulf of Mexico to Louisiana or Texas.
During this operation, liquid and vapor loading arms are connected to the liquid and vapor manifolds on
the carrier ship. As necessary, the vapor lines are used to pressurize the ship’s systems before receipt of
the liquid cargo. Liquid CO2 is pumped from the storage vessels through the metering package and
analyzers, to the liquid loading pipeline, liquid loading arm, and onto the ship. As the Liquid CO2 enters
the ship, displaced gas from the ship’s storage tanks is routed back to the onshore storage vessels via the
vapor return loading arm, vapor return piping/pipeline, and vapor return metering package.
Any CO2 vapor present in the loading system—such as that generated by heat entering the system during
operation without loading, or vapor produced when LCO2 contacts warm steel in the loading arm—is
separated from the LCO2 in the ship's storage tanks and is routed to the plant as outlined above.

                                             Page 82 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The onsite pressure control facilities continually manage any excess CO2 gas from the ship, mixing it with
the incoming CO2 gas from the pipeline. Upon disconnection from the ship, a small amount of CO2 may be
released from the loading arms, but most of it is captured in the drain header.

4.7.3 TURNDOWN / REDUCED PIPELINE CO 2 SUPPLY
The liquefaction plant is designed to function with reduced CO2 inlet flow rates, allowing operation
between approximately 10% to 100% of capacity. At throughputs below 50% of plant rated capacity one
liquefaction train is shutdown or put into recycle (depending on anticipated duration) and single train
operation continues. Compressor speed control enables a compression train to operate efficiently at flow
rates as low as 50% to 70% of its design flow. Below this throughput the compressors will operate in a
recycle mode, reducing the plant efficiency. In this mode safety and product quality are not compromised,
although the power consumption per ton of CO2 liquefied will increase compared to 'normal' operations.
This mode of operation will support tanker loading operations, although at lower throughputs. Therefore,
it will be necessary to reduce the frequency of shipments to ensure sufficient LCO2 product is in storage
ready for shipping.

4.7.4 NO PIPELINE CO 2 SUPPLY
This mode of operation would occur if the local CO2 gathering network is shutdown either through
maintenance or failure of a component.
In this instance the liquefaction plant would be shut down with compressors, air cooler fans, and transfer
pumps shut down. For unscheduled or short duration shutdowns the plant will remain filled with liquid
and pressurized while relying on insulation to slow down heat ingress. If the situation persists, heat ingress
will cause the CO2 to vaporize. The resulting pressure increase will cause the PCVs to open under the
control of the plant’s Integrated Control and Safety System (ICSS), allowing CO2 gas to vent to the vent
stack.
Provided power is still available from the external power grid, the instrument air system will continue to
operate, providing control / actuating air to valves through the process. This will allow the plant operators
an opportunity to manage the boil off through the control system. Eventually, the plant will be liquid-free
and still under pressure.
During this time it will be possible to receive cargos of Liquid CO2 via truck or rail car and load marine
tankers. However, it would not be possible to recover any displaced CO2 gas and this would be routed to
the vent stack and lost to the environment.

4.7.5 LOSS OF POWER
This is an upset mode of operation, and would be one of the cases considered when defining the control
and safety system and mechanical relief devices. Loss of power would immediately shut down all electric
motors on the site including compressors, air coolers, and transfer / loading pumps. All operations would
cease until power was restored. The Control and Safety system itself would remain on-line, powered by

                                               Page 83 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

an Uninterruptible Power System (UPS) allowing the operators an opportunity to monitor the plant and
initiate any control activities.
As pressure is lost from the air system actuated valves would fail to a pre-determined safe state (open,
closed, or as is) and pressure increases due to CO2 product boil-off managed through a network of
mechanical relief devices. Should the power be lost during loading or unloading operations these
operations would cease at that time.

4.7.6 PRESSURIZED / UN-PRESSURIZED RESTART
Following a short shutdown, the liquefaction trains would retain liquid content and chilled CO2 vapor. A
restart from this condition is straightforward and will involve starting individual compressor stages in
recycle mode and gradually bringing the incoming gas pipeline on-line once pressures are established and
ramping up production.
Should the majority of the inventory be lost due to evaporation, it will be necessary to re-establish
inventory, pressures, and chill the plant in a controlled manner. This procedure will largely reflect the
original plant start up sequence which will be developed along with other operating processes later in
design.

4.7.7 LOADING OF EMPTY, AIR FILLED TANKS
Periodically, tanks will require inspection resulting in the scenario of having to introduce cold liquid CO2
into a storage tank filled with warm and humid ambient air. The main concern is humidity in the ambient
air introducing water into the tanks and resulting in ice and / or corrosion when the LCO2 is ultimately
introduced. The steps below address the issue.
Step 1. Dry Air Flush
The first step in the process involves introducing dry instrument air, with a dew point of less than -40 °C,
into the tank(s). This effectively replaces the humid ambient air, allowing it to be vented to the
atmosphere. Assuming a purging rate that is three times the tank volume, it will take approximately 4.6
hours to dry air purge a single storage tank with a diameter of 10 meters and a length of 32.3 meters. In
total, it will take about 2.5 days to dry air purge all 11 storage tanks. This air flush will require the standby
air compressor and dryer to be in operation.
Step 2. CO2 Gas Flush
The second step involves routing dry CO2 into the tanks to displace the instrument air. This CO2 could
come from storage or the incoming pipeline (depending on which tanks are being purged and the quality
of the pipeline gas). Initially the dry air-rich mixture would be vented, but as the CO2 displaces the air, the
concentration will increase and return gas from tankage is routed back to the vapor header and
compression system where the plant stripper is designed to remove non-condensable gases and recover
CO2. This step will displace dry air with CO2 (leaving the ship in a similar status to when an empty ship
returns to Tampa).

                                                Page 84 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The purging time is approximately the same as in step 1 for the dry CO2 gas rate of 2000 m3/h. If the
incoming CO2 gas from the pipeline has greater than expected water content, it should be directed to a
dehydration package first before being used for purging the storage / ship tanks or entering the
compression system.
Step 3. Introduction of LCO2
To prevent thermal shock, liquid CO2 is introduced at a low rate through the spray nozzles at the top of
the tank, and the rate is gradually increased. Later in the design process (detailed design), procedures will
be developed outlining the detailed filling procedure with particular attention on how the loading rate is
ramped up during the process to avoid shock loads.
A more detailed philosophy for purging specific systems will be developed in the next phase.

4.8 OPERATING PHILOSOPHY OVERVIEW

4.8.1 OVERPRESSURE PROTECTION
All pressurized equipment will be protected in line with the expectations of ANSI, ASME standards, and
API recommended practices. A remote vent will be provided to dispose of larger inventories in a location
safe for personnel. Smaller local inventories will be relieved to atmosphere local to the equipment.
Lessons from other terminals and storage facilities must be gathered and employed in later project phases
to properly define venting configurations that are safe for field personnel and not subject to blockage due
to dry ice formation.

4.8.2 METERING PHILOSOPHY
As the plant will export LCO2 to another party, a custody transfer metering system is required. This
metering system will be installed on the liquid CO2 product line leading to the ship. Additionally, there will
be a connection to an analyzer to verify the product's quality. A similar system will be required for the
vapor return from the ship. This system will account for the CO2 vapor displaced from the ship's tanks and
routed back to the liquefaction plant during the LCO2 loading process.
Furthermore, some form of allocation metering and compositional analysis will be necessary upstream of
the liquefaction facility and at rail and truck offloading stations to ensure that the CO2 quality meets
specifications before entering the liquefaction process and storage tanks.
As part of normal operations, the inert removal system is expected to vent an inert stream to the
atmosphere. This stream will also contain CO2, which will need to be measured and analyzed for its CO2
content. During idle periods, where boil-off occurs, pressure control will result in CO2 emissions to the
atmosphere. These controlled discharge points will require flow measurement to accurately account for
CO2 emissions released into the atmosphere.

                                               Page 85 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.8.3 DRAIN PHILOSOPHY
It is important to drain any LCO2 before depressurizing a gas system. If a system is partially filled with
liquid, depressurization can cause the formation of dry ice, which poses potential hazards. For
maintenance on larger segments, it is advisable to direct as much LCO2 as possible into the downstream
system before starting the draining procedure. This approach minimizes the time needed to empty the
segment and reduces CO2 emissions into the atmosphere. Personnel responsible for handling LCO2 should
follow established procedures and be well-informed about the associated hazards of these operations. A
more detailed philosophy regarding the drainage of specific systems will be developed in the next phase.

4.8.4 REDUNDANCY AND SPARING PHILOSOPHY
As stated elsewhere in this report, the liquefaction plant will be comprised by two trains of 50% target
throughput each. In other words, each liquefaction train will be able to liquefy 1 million tons of CO2 per
year, requiring both to reach the target of 2 million tons per year. At this early stage, this philosophy of
redundancy seems a reasonable compromise. With 0% redundancy, or a single 100% liquefaction train,
overall CAPEX could be reduced substantially since the liquefaction trains, and specifically the 3-stage
compressors, represent the largest single component of plant CAPEX. However, in that scenario the plant
could risk losing substantial revenue, and risk disappointing its customer(s), since it would have to
completely shut down in the event of a failure of a critical component of the single train. On the other
hand, providing for 100% redundancy, or two trains each capable of the entire hub’s target throughput,
would likely be prohibitively expensive. This is because the liquefaction plant cost is among the single
highest line items in the CAPEX estimate, second only to the cost of the storage tanks. Doubling the
capacity of the liquefaction system to achieve full 100% redundancy would add tens of millions of dollars
to the CAPEX and seriously degrade the hub’s economic feasibility. With two 50% trains, a critical failure
in one train would still allow the hub to receive CO2 from its customer(s) and keep the CO2 ships running,
albeit at a reduced rate.
Additional plant redundancy comes in the form of the eleven storage tanks, each of which will have its
own piping connections and isolation valving off the CO2 header coming from the liquefaction plant. The
loss of one tank will only reduce the hub’s storage capacity by 9%. That will reduce the storage margin
versus the CO2 ship slightly but not enough to affect the hub’s or the ships’ throughput. Choosing to design
T-RICH with fewer, larger tanks, such as four spheres, would result in a significant 25% loss of storage
capacity if one tank goes offline.
To minimize hub downtime due to unplanned failure of critical parts, the plant’s sparing philosophy would
be aggressive in making sure that ample critical spare parts are always on hand, and the plant’s operating
staff is well-trained to replace parts themselves as quickly as possible. Aptamus’ affiliate company OSG is
well versed in keeping critical spare parts on hand for its fleet of petroleum tankers where each day out
of service can result in over $100,000 in lost revenue and harm the company’s reputation. The T-RICH hub
would follow OSG’s aggressive sparing philosophy in order to minimize downtime. Spares will be
categorized into the following types:

                                              Page 86 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  Capital Spares

  Construction Spares

  Startup and Commissioning Spares
  Operating Spares

4.9 ALTERNATIVES AND SENSITIVITIES CONSIDERED

Due to a fairly well-defined range of options presented during the initial project kick-off, the number of
alternatives considered during this phase was minimal. Plant capacity was defined and well aligned with
the liquid CO2 carrier ship size, and identification by Aptamus that they had identified Medium Pressure
as the selected CO2 transport condition meant that storage conditions were not subject to optimization.
As the selected site was more than suitable for the plant requirements, there was no requirement to look
at alternate locations.
Liquefaction technology has historically been subject to selection. However, previous experience has
indicated that Internal Refrigeration as a proven dispatchable technology generally forms a robust base
case design. Power and Cooling duties of Internal and External refrigeration plants typically have similar
requirements and equipment counts. Ultimately, for the purposes of this study, it was agreed that the
base case of internal refrigeration would be retained for the duration of the work, and if necessary
revisited at later stages.
Specific areas where additional focus was required were:
  Storage Configuration (number, size, and orientation of liquid CO2 storage vessels)
  Cooling Demand and Power Consumption (Designing for the worst case ambient temperature
 exaggerates annual power demand, and work is required to provide a more realistic figure)

  Construction Approach (site specific constraints meant a number of options were examined)

4.9.1 STORAGE ALTERNATIVES
Selecting the number of, type, and configuration of liquid CO2 storage tanks was addressed by
development of a base case and performing a capital cost comparison vs the base case. This comparison
was conditioned through a qualitative assessment of relative safety, operability, and construction
considerations as summarized in Table 4.2.
The base case assumed vertical pressure vessels as has been installed on the Northern Lights project in
Europe. In the T-RICH application, 36 vessels would be provided and grouped into 'storage units'. In each
unit, 6 vessels would be connected to each other via a common liquid inlet header, liquid outlet header,
and common vapor header. Liquid CO2 from the plant would be distributed from the common inlet header
and simultaneously enter all storage vessels within the unit. Once the storage unit is filled, liquid CO2 will
be routed to the next storage unit. A total of 6 storage units were envisioned to be provided.
