During the definition phase of this study this approach was challenged. Treating multiple vessels as a
common storage unit increases the number of potential leak sources and makes construction more
complex. As a result, a coarse evaluation was conducted to determine if this approach is valid, or if fewer
larger units could be considered. Following this optimization a revised pre-FEED case was agreed. Eleven

                                             Page 88 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

horizontal pressure vessels (with a diameter of 10 m and a T/T length of 32.3 m) are provided. With both
liquefaction trains producing at full capacity it would take approximately 10.2 hours to fill one unit to 90%
capacity, and it would take 3 days to produce a full 20,000 m³ cargo of liquid CO2 (the intended capacity
of the dedicated CO2 ship).
The option of storing liquid CO2 in spheres was considered. This would produce the minimum number of
vessels, however they would require significant site fabrication and will likely drive the project duration.
This option was therefore not progressed further in this phase but can be revisited in future phases.
During subsequent project phases heat gain calculations will be conducted to optimize the insulation
thickness. As a benchmark reference European projects indicate that approximately 100mm of insulation
has allowed 5 days holding time with no venting at 20°C ambient. For T-RICH’s tanks, 200mm has been
conservatively assumed until the calculations are complete.
Facilities will be included in the design for recirculating liquid CO2 into and out of the tanks as this might
be necessary for operational reasons (e.g. to avoid stratified layers in the tank.)
Should US fabrication be essential, then Option 2 can be progressed within the existing site by expanding
the storage area to the East. This will limit the ability to expand storage in the future if additional
compression / liquefaction trains are developed to increase throughput.
The cost of transporting the 11 insulated vessels from Spain to Florida has been investigated and included
in the evaluation. Preliminary indications from in-house shipping experts is that these tanks can be
transferred in a single shipment using for example a Rolldock ST class heavy lift Ro-Ro multipurpose dry
cargo ship. The transit would take approximately 3 days at a cost of several million USD. This additional
cost and duration is not sufficient to sway the decision regarding revised base case as the Total Installed
Cost differential is in excess of 41 Million USD.

 Figure 4.27 – Example Heavy Lift Vessel for Shipping Storage Tanks from Spain to Tampa

Spherical tanks were also considered in a separate analysis. Such tanks have several advantages for
holding cold, pressurized liquids like liquified CO2 which is why they are often used to store liquefied
petroleum gas (LPG) on land. First, due to their shape, stress concentrations are minimized and the stress
in the steel shell is uniform over the entire surface of the tank. Second, spheres have the lowest surface
area for a given volume of any other tank shape. This presents a particular advantage with low-

                                               Page 89 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

temperature liquids since it limits the surface area of the tank through which heat can enter the tank,
raise the temperature of the liquid and create boil-off gas which raises the pressure inside the tank and
increases the risk of loss of containment. As a corollary, that minimizes the amount of thermal insulation
that must be installed on the tank surface, minimizing initial and future maintenance costs. Finally,
spheres can be expected to take up significantly less land area for a given storage volume compared to
horizontally oriented bullet tanks. In Figure 4.28, the land area required for 11 bullet tanks of 30,000 m3
combined capacity is overlayed on the land area required for four spheres of equal combined capacity.
The four spheres require approximately half the land area of the bullets. This can be a major advantage
when available land area is a challenge and local height restrictions are not an issue.
The major disadvantage to spherical tanks is that they generally must be assembled on site due to their
size. In this configuration they would be too large to transport from a fabrication shop to the site intact.
See Figure 4.29 which shows a spherical tank under construction, approximately the same size as has been
proposed for the T-RICH site. On site fabrication can be a disadvantage for two reasons. First, it is likely to
be more expensive to build the tanks onsite in Tampa using a crew of workers from the tank
manufacturer’s home base which may be several states away. Such workers will incur increased
temporary housing costs (i.e. hotels) and may incur additional pay due to being away from home. Indeed,
a budgetary quotation received for four spherical tanks, built onsite, was approximately 50% greater than
a quote for tanks built overseas by a tank manufacturer that has already built CO2 tanks for European
projects. Second, quality control of tank fabrication can be expected to be marginally lower onsite
compared to the tank maker’s home facility where a more controlled fabrication environment is possible.

 Figure 4.28: Comparison of Land Area Required by Horizontal Bullet Tanks and Spherical Tanks

                                               Page 90 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.29: Typical Spherical Tank Under Construction (source: American Tank & Vessel)

4.9.2 COOLING OPTIONS
The high ambient temperature in Tampa will make air cooling less effective than in more temperate
climates. This in turn drives liquefaction compressor interstage temperatures upwards and increases total
motor loads.
Seawater (or freshwater) cooling may offer advantages over air cooling by eliminating noisy forced draft
air exchangers and potentially allowing for closer temperature approaches. However, the EPA has specific
regulations (62-302.520 Thermal Surface Water Criteria) regarding the use of seawater in this location.
These regulations state that the surface temperature of the receiving body of water must not be raised
above 97ºF (36ºC). Utilizing seawater as a cooling medium for the interstage coolers would require large
heat exchangers made from titanium materials, which would add complexity to the plant.
This option was not progressed further during Pre-FEED, however it can be revisited during later design
phases.

                                             Page 91 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

4.10 CONSTRUCTION PLAN

This section outlines the construction, installation and commissioning strategies that have been used to
build up the overall cost estimate of the hub. The hub site is be set within an industrial area already having
major road and rail links close by and is adjacent to an inlet waterway area of Port Tampa Bay. Thus the
site is already well positioned to make use of existing industrial transportation infrastructure in order to
minimize construction costs and time.
This overview is designed to give an understanding of and a general insight into how the project can be
implemented, with its execution based on the following key guiding principles.
 Safety
 All works are to be carried out safely, in compliance with all regulations and procedures. Worksite
 activities should be thoroughly planned for and executed using proven guidelines, with known
 techniques.
 Schedule
 In developing a construction schedule the aim will be to deliver the build works within the agreed
 timescale while meeting the required safety, quality and cost targets. Starting with the end in
 mind approach, this is key to construction development, sequencing and optimization.
 Quality
 A defined quality strategy should be in place at the outset, ensuring required standards are
 achieved throughout the construction project. Works carried out off-site at remote fabrication or
 manufacturing facilities are considered to already have controlled environments, however
 inspections and quality checks should still be made available and enable monitoring by the project
 team. This can be reinforced by prioritizing equipment suppliers who have established ISO 9001
 and ISO 14001 policies in place.

4.10.1 CONSTRUCTION APPROACH OPTIONS
Two main construction / assembly options were identified.

  Marine Transportation of Large Pre-Assembled Units
  Road / Rail Transportation of smaller components for site integration and erection.

The site is well positioned for road and rail transport, being in a major port with well-established road and
rail infrastructure. However, the size of many major pieces of equipment, primarily the insulated storage
tanks, precludes them from road and rail movements as pre-assembled units. As a result, it is
recommended to make use of the excellent marine transportation links offered by the site and bring in
the majority of equipment by sea. There is currently no berth immediately adjacent to the site; the marine
berth intended to be used for the CO2 ship is shifted too far to the west to be used in site construction.
And an infrequently used LPG pipeline borders the northern edge of the site close to the water’s edge,
creating a critical obstacle. Overcoming these two challenges is addressed in the following sections where
a construction sequence is provided outlining what additional facilities would be required to integrate
marine loading facilities into the overall construction plan. Installation will be sequential and aligned with
a procurement plan to ensure the plant to be installed efficiently and as per schedule.
Based upon the assessment conducted, the base case of marine transportation should be retained.
Bringing in materials / equipment as pre-built multiple units along with specific large scale vessel builds
straight to and traveling into the site has considerable advantages. As an alternative to large on-site
fabrications, this utilization of a remote build concept allow works to be undertaken within controlled

                                               Page 93 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

factory / workshop environments, in steady state conditions, enabling good productivity levels, quality
work control and safety hazard reductions.
To facilitate this, the engineering design should be developed to include an extensive element of
modularization, thus simplifying the on-site installations, minimizing a number of construction high-risk
activities such as extensive working at height and large welding scopes. Modularization of the packages
by size and weight, with ease of interconnections, will determine and optimize the transportation as well
as construction methods, notwithstanding any road/rail transport constraints. Having equipment /
packages assembled in modular type prefabrication builds at subcontractors offsite yards provides for a
number of advantages e.g. reductions on multiple transport, as well as the sequential build schedule
opportunities, further simplifying site installations and reducing on-site specialist trades resources.
Pre-build modularization aids the overall schedule by allowing more time for civil ground works since
module foundations would need to be fully completed prior to construction commencement. In planning
terms, the early phase works such as the metering and utility packages can be completed while pre-
fabrications are being built off-site. That can proceed in parallel, rather than sequentially if these large-
scale fabrications were to be assembled on-site. Where this approach may not be technically achievable
in some situations, e.g. due to transport limitations, then on-site stick-build installations can still be
utilized.

4.10.2 CIVIL WORKS
Civil works including ground work to support equipment receipt will be the first step in the development
of the site. Key activities will include:

  Preparation of a construction barge berthing location to the north of the site,
  Installation of a temporary construction bridge to cross the existing LPG pipeline safely. Elevated
 civil ground works on either side of the pipeline will allow a Bailey type bridge to be laid out and
 positioned across the pipe.
  Paving the roads on site as per the final site plan, to be utilized during construction. Including the
 main site access from Pendola Point Road.
  Preparation of area in the future expansion area on the east side of the site to lay out procurement
 materials / equipment on arrival.
The parcel currently ranges in elevation from 8.1 feet at the southwest corner to 17.6 feet in the eastern
half. Elevations refer to the North American Datum of 1988 (NAVD88). Therefore, it must be filled as
necessary and graded to make it level for the installation of equipment and buildings, but with adequate
sloping towards planned and installed drainage points (between 0.5% and 1.0% slope). Any fill used will
be free of any deleterious material, well-drained and compacted to a minimum of 85% density in
accordance with ASTM 01557-02. After it is filled and graded for use as the T-RICH facility, it is projected
to be 13.5 feet throughout the center drainage basin to 14.5 feet near the perimeter.

                                               Page 94 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

The only known structure on the site are three wood poles supporting power lines to a floodlight. These
are planned to be removed prior to grading. Any potential underground structures will be identified during
thorough site surveys in future phases of the project.
During construction a silt fence will be installed around the perimeter of the site to prevent sediment from
being carried beyond the perimeter with storm water or other means until permanent surfaces (e.g.
asphalt, gravel) and drainage systems are installed. The silt fence will be a minimum of 16 inches above
ground and 8 inches below ground. It will be supported by wood posts driven into the ground no more
than 6 feet apart. Additionally, as the site’s drainage system is constructed before the road is, AGIP (at
grade inlet protection) is used around/over the FDOT Type C & D catch basins to ensure that debris from
construction does not enter the drainage system. It consists of galvanized hardware cloth and nonwoven
geotextile being secured over the steel grates of the catch basins, with 6 inches of stone being placed over
that to allow stormwater to flow through while filtering out sediment. Once construction is complete AGIP
is removed from all catch basins. These controls are temporary and will be removed as construction
concludes.
A Stabilized Construction Entrance at the site’s main road entrance at Pendola Point Road will be used as
additional temporary protection while construction occurs on site. This prevents the entrance to the site
from deteriorating into an unsafe and inaccessible area due to consistent heavy loads passing over
unstable ground. This will be removed once construction of the road is nearing completion.
A retention pond will be created to which all draining infrastructure on the site leads. Such a retention
pond is required by Hillsborough County and SWFWMD (Southwest Florida Water Management District)
to manage stormwater runoff and improve water quality by capturing and treating pollutants. These
systems help prevent flooding by storing water, allowing sediment to settle out, and providing filtration
before water eventually discharges into natural water bodies.
Once the main site civil works are completed the focus will shift to the plant, equipment and module
foundations.

4.10.3 CONSTRUCTION BERTH PREPARATION
This new berth to be used only for equipment delivery during hub construction could be made ready at
the same time as, or possibly even earlier than, the site civil works. It would then be available to be used
for the material, equipment, and fabrication arrivals, allowing them to be transferred directly onto the
site. With all materials being laid out at the storage / laydown areas, construction can commence and
then follow a planned sequence of procurement deliveries and onto installation builds. This negates the
requirement of allocating the ‘future’ northeast site areas as a materials warehousing area.
Marine Off-Loading by Crane Lifts
With civil ground works completed two heavy-lift (700tn) crawler cranes can be brought in by barge and
offloaded. The cranes will be positioned on the site-edge area north of the LPG pipeline, one to the east
with the other to the west and made ready for material arrivals on cargo barges which will berth alongside

                                              Page 95 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

the pontoon. A third may be added further west to enable direct crane lifts over to the actual worksites
without having to relocate one of the offloading cranes.
Marine Off-Loading to Site Transfers (Option 1)
The fabrications / equipment packages will be offloaded to the storage areas by SMPT’s or lifted from the
vessels directly by the cranes into the prepared embankment laydown areas to be stored while awaiting
a scheduled transfer across the pipeline and onto the site. When there is an agreed period that sees the
gas pipeline not being utilized the materials, modules and equipment can be lifted by crane across and
into the site. Subject to the adjacent site owner's standard procedures or requirements, crossing this
pipeline should be agreed and developed during an early project stage. Risk assessments for the crane-
lifts will most likely indicate that ‘dropped object protection’ (DOP) over the pipeline will be necessary.
Marine Off-Loading to Site Transfers (Option 2)
Options for DOP could be developed with just a temporary type of steelwork reinforced arch structure as
a protection barrier specifically for the crane works, or a more robust purpose-built heavy-duty bridge
crossover. By forming elevated civil ground works and building a concrete barrier wall on either side of
the pipeline a Bailey type bridge can be laid out and positioned across. With this bridge in place, in addition
to providing for more substantial pipeline protection at all times, it allows for the capacity of the safe
working load crossings to be increased, with loaded SPMT’s transferring directly onto site.
Marine Off-Loading by Crane or SPMT
Material load transfers could remain flexible but would depend on what lift-plan options become
acceptable. One option would involve heavy crane lifts from the cargo barge directly into site area, then
transferred for positioning to foundations by mobile cranes. Another would involve lifting material already
preloaded on SMPT’s off the cargo barge, transferring it across onto the embankment area, over a Bailey
bridge directly adjacent to the foundations (single transfer operation), and then finally positioned by local
mobile cranes.
Marine Off-Loading Summary
The crossover bridge facility could remain and be utilized for the site’s next construction phase of ‘future’
development, as well as for any possible maintenance tasks. However, the pontoon access could be
removed and brought back in as and when further construction works were required.

4.10.4 DELIVERY AND INSTALLATION OF EQUIPMENT AND FACILITIES
Materials, fabricated modules and packaged equipment will be shipped by barge (where too large to be
trucked on public roads) or by truck to the site in a logical sequence to support fabrication. As large, heavy
items such as the storage tanks and large pre-assembled units (PAUs) are delivered by barge they can be
transferred directly onto site via SPMTs crossing the pipeline as necessary.

                                               Page 96 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.30: Tank Transportation by SPMT

Figure 4.31: Roll On / Roll Off Pontoon Barge

               Page 97 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.32: Waterside Delivery, Offloading & Laydown Overview

Care will need to be taken not to block the navigational channel with the site’s pontoon barge and ocean-
going cargo deck barge in place during movement of heavy equipment onto the site. Initial indications
are that such an arrangement will not block the navigation channel as the overall channel is more than
500 feet wide from the northern bank of the T-RICH site to the marine berths on the northern side of the
channel. If the cargo deck barge is an industry-standard 72 feet wide, and the pontoon barge is
approximately 50 feet wide, that leaves 378 feet of clearance to the berths on the other side of the
channel for ships to pass through. The largest ships that would be expected to transit this particular
shipping channel would be “Panamax” ships with a beam not exceeding 105.6 feet. This is believed to be
an acceptable arrangement.
Fabrication will be conducted generally from West to East and South to North such that the first
equipment sited does not interfere with subsequent deliveries. It is therefore essential that the
procurement priorities are aligned with the fabrication sequence.
First to be constructed will be the administration building / workshop. This can be used initially as support
offices and workforce facilities, before being changed over to offices and control buildings providing for a
'one' build option in the schedule. Storage tanks can then be installed on their foundations in sequence
and progressively tied into the piping and control networks.
Liquefaction trains and metering facilities can be installed as they are delivered with the westerly train
installed first. It is recommended that as far as practical large pre-assembled units (PAUs) be used to
minimize the need for site fabrication and integration. The vent stack is likely to be pre-fabricated in
multiple sections and erected on site using mobile cranes.

                                              Page 98 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 4.33: Construction Sequence

This phase of construction will require a number of heavy crawler cranes often operating in tandem to lift
larger equipment items. Sequencing and planning of the individual operations is a key part of subsequent
design phases. The equipment will be grouped into systems and sub systems to allow construction
activities to be monitored and progress in a structured fashion. As sub systems are completed they can
be inspected and tested in preparation for handover to commissioning. A proven mechanical completion
system must be in place to track and document system completion and handover.
Bringing large PAUs onto site will speed handover to commissioning as key system checks can be done
prior to shipping in the more controlled environment of the construction yard.
This final phase prior to handover to the operations team will ensure the plant's safety, and demonstrate
that all items function reliably. With commissioning tests complete and system acceptance documented
it will be possible to receive cargoes of CO2 and start up the pipeline.

4.10.5 COMMISSIONING
With the philosophy of building equipment at the manufacturers facilities, the use of modular packages,
and equipment outfitting prior to shipping maximized, there will be extensive use of Factory Acceptance
Testing (FAT). This approach aims to ensure PAUs, and PFAs, are pre-commissioned and function-tested
far as practicable prior to transport to site. This minimizes site work and provides an opportunity to

                                             Page 99 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

identify / rectify issues at manufacturer sites by factory workers and the engineers who designed the
equipment.
Mechanical completion, testing, and commissioning will be carried out during a phased program to finalize
construction activities, integration and completion of all new installations.
Commissioning is generally phased as follows:
 1. Pre-commissioning / tests of packaged equipment at suppliers / vendors
 2. Pre-commission / integration, testing at site of newly installed plant and built assemblies
 3. Commission / start-up of all new facilities and integration of all systems
A detailed commissioning procedure will be produced in future stages and aligned to the overall
construction strategy. In general, the goal will be to maximize pre-commissioning and testing at suppliers’
facilities and to minimize carry-over onto the construction site.

                                             Page 100 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

5 REGULATORY PLAN

5.1 REGULATORY PLAN ANALYSIS

5.1.1 BACKGROUND
Port Tampa Bay engineering staff has identified six major governmental regulators that would be involved
in the approval of the proposed T-RICH CO2 hub, based on their experience in developing similar industrial
marine terminals within port jurisdiction.
 1. Hillsborough County – The proposed project site is in Hillsborough County and would require
 Hillsborough County permit approval for the site development as well as the building
 construction. Port Tampa Bay has communicated with the agency via telephone to discuss the
 potential project. Due to the proximity to the nearest airport, the Peter O. Knight airport which is
 approximately 1.3 miles away, a Hillsborough County Aviation Authority (HCAA) permit may be
 required. However, due to the airport being privately owned and operated, a Federal Aviation
 Authority (FAA) permit is not required.
 2. Environmental Protection Commission of Hillsborough County (EPC) – The proposed project site
 is located in Hillsborough County and would require EPC approval of any environmental wetland
 impacts. Port Tampa Bay has communicated with the agency via telephone to discuss the
 potential project. After further discussions with EPC, an air emissions permit will be required due
 to CO2 being processed as a byproduct. A noise permit will not be needed as the project site is in
 an industrial area.
 3. Florida Department of Environmental Protection (FDEP) – The proposed project site is located in
 the State of Florida and thus would require approval for storm water, environmental wetland
 impacts, and marine construction. Port Tampa Bay has communicated with the agency via
 telephone to discuss the potential project.
 4. Army Corps of Engineers (ACOE) – The proposed project site has work in the “Waters of the
 United States” and therefore would require ACOE approval for marine environmental impacts,
 pipelines, and marine construction. Port Tampa Bay has communicated with the agency via
 telephone to discuss the potential project.
 5. CSX - The proposed project site calls for connections to an existing CSX railroad track line. A Utility
 and/or Longitudinal Occupancy Permit will be required and can be submitted through the CSX
 Property Portal.
 6. Port Tampa Bay (PTB) – The proposed project site involves work in the sovereign submerged lands
 owned by PTB and therefore would require PTB approval for marine environmental impacts and
 marine construction. Port Tampa Bay has discussed the potential project internally and sees no
 issue with approving it.

                                                Page 101 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Port Tampa Bay will continue to regularly communicate with these agencies about the Project. Should the
project continue into the FEED phase, prior to concluding the FEED study PTB will further engage each
agency with in-person meetings. If the project were to progress to the construction phase, then PTB would
have Pre-Application meetings with all of the agencies prior to submitting permit applications to the
agencies. The estimated timeline for permitting with each agency varies from 3 months to 9 months
depending on the agency.

5.1.2 ADVERSE PARTIES
This project does not involve any significant impacts to the marine or upland environment and adds a
significant benefit to the local and regional community by introducing a network of CO2 capture, storage,
transportation and sequestration activities that reduces the overall CO2 output to the environment and
results in improved human health conditions, along with the contribution towards mitigating the impacts
of climate change. In addition, the proposed site is well within an industrial port zone with existing
operations surrounding it that present an even greater threat to the environment and public safety than
this proposed operation does. Thus the project is not anticipated to generate any significant opposition
from third parties.

5.1.3 REGULATORY PLAN ANALYSIS SUMMARY
The regulatory requirements were identified via both historical experience in building similar upland site
developments and marine projects in the same jurisdictions as well as phone conversations with each of
the agencies detailing the proposed project and study.
In the past, Port Tampa Bay has had multiple meetings with the community, environmental organizations
and with various agencies about projects that could benefit environmental sustainability. This project
would provide a great benefit to the environment and to human health.
Should the project proceed to the FEED stage, further community outreach could be performed through
various meetings and workshops, and pursuant to our community benefit plan, as described in Section
10, to gain further support and identify any adverse parties. This would occur far enough in advance of a
Financial Investment Decision (FID) to ensure that any negative feedback was considered in the decision
whether to move forward with detailed design and construction.

5.1.4 ENGAGEMENT METHODS AND TIMELINE
Should the project proceed to the FEED stage, meetings would be held with each of the following agencies:
Hillsborough County, Environmental Protection Commission of Hillsborough County, Florida Department
of Environmental Protection, Army Corps of Engineers, and Port Tampa Bay, to provide more detailed
information about the project to the agencies but also to get more detailed feedback from each of the
agencies about the proposed project. Any information gained from these meetings would be used in the
potential future detailed design and construction phases and would of course be considered in the FID.

                                            Page 102 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

If the FEED were completed successfully and the project moved into detailed design, Aptamus and/or PTB
personnel would set up pre-application meetings with each of the agencies to ensure all requirements are
met and included in the construction documents prior to submittal of the permit applications.
Conversations have been had with each of the agencies listed above. Further meetings and presentations
with each of the agencies are planned to take place within the FEED period of performance, and pre
applications would take place at the beginning of the detailed design phase.

5.1.5 CONSENT-BASED SITING STATEMENT
The proposed upland site and submerged lands, as well as adjacent rail and road, are all owned by Port
Tampa Bay, a Team Member of this Grant. Invoking eminent domain would not be required for this
project, though The Tampa Port Authority dba Port Tampa Bay does have the legal authority to assert
eminent domain as a means of land rights acquisition.

5.1.6 HIERARCHY OF COMPLIANCE
There are many complementary (and overlapping) Federal, State, and Regional Regulations, Standards,
and Guidelines that are relevant to consider for the design of the T-RICH Site. The hierarchy of compliance
in descending order shall be as follows:
 1. Federal Acts and Regulations
 2. State Acts and Regulations
 3. Regional (County/Parish/Port Authority) and Municipal Acts and Regulations
 4. National Codes and Standards
 5. Industry Codes and Standards
 6. Company Requirements, Industry, Guidelines and Recommended Best Practices

certification programs, and educational resources. ASME plays a critical role in regulating pressure vessels,

American Petroleum Institute (API): For CO₂ storage tanks operating at pressures above 25 bar (~363 psi),
typical API tank standards (such as API 620 or API 650) become less directly applicable, as these are
intended for lower-pressure, atmospheric, or moderately pressurized storage. At pressures exceeding 25
bar, CO₂ storage tanks move into the domain of pressure vessels, regulated primarily under the ASME
Boiler and Pressure Vessel Code (BPVC). However, API standards are widely referenced for designing and
operating pressure relief systems, pipelines, and equipment in facilities handling hazardous or pressurized
fluids, including liquefied or compressed CO₂.
National Fire Protection Association (NFPA): These are applicable NFPA Standards that are referenced in
either the Florida Building Code (FBC) or the Florida Fire Protection Code (FFPC). NFPA standards govern
fire safety, hazardous materials handling, and emergency response.

prevention and fire protection systems.

6 BUSINESS CASE ANALYSIS

6.1 TECHNICAL OVERVIEW

A description of the technology used at the T-RICH intermodal hub is described in detail elsewhere in this
report and will not be repeated here. But to summarize, the technology and equipment for the storage
and transport of captured CO2 at the T-RICH intermodal hub is compatible with any currently available
carbon capture technology that can be installed at a power plant. Therefore, the T-RICH hub can receive
CO2 captured from any power plant or similar industrial emitter in the region. This is because it doesn’t
matter how the CO2 was captured as long as the result is gaseous CO2 which is reasonably free of
impurities and delivered via pipeline, rail car, or tank truck to the T-RICH hub. The T-RICH hub is therefore
compatible with any of the following carbon capture technologies that may be in use at an industrial
emitter:
 1. Amine Absorption
 2. Membrane
 3. Soda Ash
 4. Direct Air Capture
Furthermore, the T-RICH hub can accept CO2 from multiple sources simultaneously, each using a different
capture technology, as long as they are producing gaseous CO2 of reasonable quality to T-RICH.

6.2 MARKET ANALYSIS

The Tampa Bay region within which the T-RICH hub will be located is host to many large-volume stranded
emitters of CO2, including power generation plants serving ratepayers across the State of Florida. The
immediate vicinity also hosts a large phosphate mining and fertilizer industry with numerous point sources
of significant reported CO2 emissions. According to the U.S. EPA, just 8 stranded emitters in Hillsborough
County alone emitted a combined 6 million tons of CO2 emissions in 2023. Another 11 million tons of CO2
emissions were emitted by just 17 emitters in adjacent Polk County, Florida.17 It is important to note that
Florida lacks any significant pipeline infrastructure and is far removed from the existing national and
interstate pipeline network which traverses the central Gulf of Mexico region. Consequently, most large
emitters in Florida are considered “stranded” and captured CO2 from these various sources must be
transported to a hub site such as T- RICH where it will be accumulated, stored, and then loaded onto
dedicated CO2 marine vessels for transport to ultimate sequestration sites along the onshore and offshore
Gulf of Mexico region.
Initially, Aptamus set the initial throughput of the T-RICH intermodal hub at two million tons per annum
(2mtpa). The intent was to determine if that throughput represented a reasonable goal given the amount

17
 www.epa.gov/ghgreporting/data-sets

                                              Page 108 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

of land available in the vacant terminal parcel that Port of Tampa Bay identified as available for liquid bulk
cargo development. One conclusion of the pre-FEED engineering effort is that a hub terminal site capable
of liquefying and storing 2mtpa of CO2 would require just a portion of that available parcel area. With
some rearrangement and consolidation of equipment, the throughput could safely expand to 8mtpa on
the parcel. Aptamus calls the initial 2mtpa throughput the “Phase 1” throughput, which is the focus of this
study. The potential additional 6mtpa of CO2 throughput on top of the initial 2mtpa is labeled the “Phase
2” throughput which is outside the scope of this study, but is considered to be highly feasible.
Six of the seven electricity power generation plants in the following table which are all within 35 miles of
the T-RICH hub each have enough emissions to satisfy the T-RICH Phase 1 throughput capacity. And just
four of these plants satisfy the maximum potential T-RICH Phase 2 capacity of 8mtpa. This shows the
ample market potential for such an intermodal hub in the Tampa Bay area, and it does not even include
the long list of emitters other than power plants in the area. Moreover, projected power demand in
Florida over the next 20 years is expected to rise substantially due to development of data centers, AI
systems, and continued projections of population growth. As these plants will need to generate
significantly more power supply, these current reported emission levels will also increase.

Phase 2 throughputs of the T-RICH intermodal hub (2mtpa and 6mtpa, respectively). In other words,
Phase 1 of T-RICH could handle nearly all of the CO2 emissions of one power plant, or a significant portion
of two power plants. With the addition of 6mtpa in Phase 2, the total emissions of three power plants
could be handled.

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 Figure 6.1: Area Power Plant CO2 Emissions Shares

The “technology” in use at the T-RICH hub will be applicable to the CO2 captured and transported to the
hub by any of these major electricity generation plants. This is because these stranded emitters will be
contractually required to process and purify the CO2 they have captured to meet the broad purity
specifications set for the T-RICH hub.

6.2.1 PROJECTED 50-YEAR USE
T-RICH could operate for 50 years or more in the configuration presented in this report. In fact, it is likely
to grow and expand steadily over those 50 years as more industrial emitters choose to, or are forced to,
install carbon capture plants and require a method for transporting their CO2 out of state to permanent
sequestration sites. The phased approach described above is just one of many ways this long-term growth
could be structured. Likely it will happen in many phases as each regional emitter installs a carbon capture
plant to capture a portion of the CO2 initially, and then later expands their CC plant to capture more of
their CO2 and transport it to T-RICH via pipeline or rail. And it would not be difficult to control the quality
specifications of each of those emitters’ CO2. Likely, each emitter would tailor their carbon capture system
to produce CO2 within the specifications established by T-RICH.

6.3 FUTURE DEPLOYMENT PROJECTION

6.3.1 POTENTIAL DEPLOYMENT SCALE
The potential deployment scale of the technology across the current and future NGCC point source sector
has no limitations in terms of technology. The basic technology employed by the T-RICH hub is of such a
generic nature that it can be used to liquefy and store CO2 emitted from a variety of point sources with

                                              Page 110 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

proper treatment on the emitter’s side. The primary limitations to deployment are purely geographical
and financial. Because the hub is designed to load CO2 onto marine vessels for transport to other states it
must be located on or near a navigational ship channel with sufficient water depths to accommodate the
envisioned CO2 marine vessel. Luckily, there are numerous power plants throughout the southeast United
States that are close enough to such ship channels that pipelines between the emitter and the marine hub
should be relatively short and practical in terms of permitting, land ownership, rights of way, and cost.
Potentially a half dozen identical hubs like T-RICH could be built in Florida and up the Lower East Coast,
each within practical proximity to existing power plants and other major emitters, and each capable of
processing and storing the CO2 from multiple emitters.
It is estimated that the maximum future expanded throughput of CO2 from each hub identical to T-RICH
is 8mtpa. A half dozen identical hubs throughout Florida and the Lower East Coast could therefore handle
a combined total of 48mtpa from a few dozen power plants and other major emitters.

6.3.2 COMPETING TECHNOLOGY OPTIONS
The primary competing technology of an intermodal carbon hub like T-RICH is a pipeline from the emitter
directly to a sequestration site. Utilization of a pipeline to transport captured CO2 rather than a marine
transport solution which the T-RICH hub is intended to serve could have some advantages, particularly
where shorter distances between the emitter and sequestration site are possible. But it is highly unlikely
that pipelines will have any appreciable advantages over the proposed marine transport solution over the
very long distances from the stranded emitters in the southeastern United States to the proven
sequestration wells or for EOR in the northern Gulf Coast of Texas and Louisiana. Any CO2 captured in the
state of Florida would require a long transport distance to the nearest proven sequestration pore spaces
in the states of Louisiana and Texas. Florida, and its immediate neighbors, are currently not known to have
geology favorable to sequestration of CO2, have a long history of political and social opposition to oil and
gas drilling of any kind, and prioritize the state’s interest in the tourism industry which requires protection
of the fragile ecosystem in Florida. Therefore, it is likely that marine transport of CO2 is the only cost-
effective and viable method for transporting CO2 captured in Florida to Louisiana and Texas.
The primary and significant disadvantages of a new pipeline from Florida to Louisiana are CAPEX,
regulatory complexity, and the likelihood of extensive delays due to litigation over land acquisition and
environmental concerns. A new pipeline to transport the captured CO2 from the Tampa Bay region to
known sequestration sites in Louisiana and Texas would have to pass through four states and at least 28
individual counties to get to the destination proposed by this project. See Figure 6.2 below.
Recently, the Oil & Gas Journal reported that land pipeline construction hit a record $10.7 million per mile
in 202318. A new CO2 pipeline from the Tampa Bay region to Louisiana would be approximately 700 miles
long. Therefore, such a new CO2 pipeline can be expected to cost upwards of $7.5 billion. The cost
estimating performed within the scope of this study for the T-RICH intermodal hub reveals the hub is

18
 https://www.ogj.com/pipelines-transportation/pipelines/article/14299952/land-pipeline-construction-costs-hit-
record-107-millionmile

                                               Page 111 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

expected to cost approximately one-tenth of a pipeline cost, or approximately $779 million. Preliminary
cost estimating for the remaining components of the marine transport system, namely two CO2 ships and
a CO2 discharge terminal in Sunshine, Louisiana, reveals that a rough-order-of-magnitude cost for the
complete marine transportation system, equivalent in length and purpose to a pipeline, will be on the
order of $1.5 billion. Therefore, it is believed the marine transport system will cost approximately 20% of
an equivalent pipeline to build. Moreover, the marine transportation system, of which the T-RICH hub is
a critical component, could be commercially operational far sooner than a new pipeline due to eliminating
the complex permitting processes and environmental and land acquisition issues within the jurisdictions
of multiple local, state and federal regulatory regimes. It was just these various obstacles which caused
the termination of several long multistate CO2 pipelines in the US over the last two years. Finally, rather
than the multiple overlapping jurisdictions with regulatory oversight that pipeline projects must contend
with, the primary regulator for a CO2 ship is the U.S. Coast Guard, an agency OSG has successfully worked
with for more than 50 years.

 FIGURE 6.2: Comparison of Marine Transport Route to Pipeline Route

6.3.3 BARRIERS TO LARGE SCALE DEPLOYMENT
The primary barrier to large scale deployment of intermodal carbon hubs is likely to be high capital and
operating expenses. Large values of CAPEX and OPEX will increase the cost to transport each ton of CO2
over a marine network and could make it challenging to keep the total cost of such transport within the
current 45Q amount of $85 per ton. It is expected that the more the total cost to transport and sequester
CO2 exceeds the available government incentives, the less likely major emitters like NGCC emitters will
choose that method to abate their greenhouse gas emissions. It is therefore imperative that technology
continues to evolve to reduce these costs in order to encourage widespread adoption of intermodal

                                             Page 112 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

carbon hubs serving marine transportation networks. Of course, there are other methods major emitters
can employ to reduce the cost of adopting carbon capture systems which would increase the supply of
CO2 for the T-RICH hub. This includes private funding such as debt financing, traditional loan programs,
and seeking rate increases from the Florida Public Utility Commission to subsidize the cost of capture
systems from their ratepaying customers. Such ratepayer electricity cost increases have been approved
in other states to help pay for the cost of infrastructure construction, modernization, and other purposes.
It is expected that at some point Florida electric utilities will similarly seek rate increase approval from the
state regulator.
Another potential barrier is public resistance to intermodal carbon hubs being built near their
communities. This takes the form of the fear of widespread releases of CO2, or, more generally, the Not-
In-My-Back-Yard “NIMBY” syndrome. Several CO2 pipeline leaks in recent years, such as one in Louisiana
related to the Exxon pipeline, have received relatively little widespread media attention in the U.S. but
that is likely because the CO2 transportation industry is still relatively small and located primarily in
industrial or rural areas. The more that CO2 transport infrastructure is placed near urban areas, the higher
the risk of accidents affecting a larger number of people. The public may assign the risks of pipeline
accidents in rural areas to a new carbon hub in a seaport, making its acceptance more challenging.
However, a hub like T-RICH, and the maritime transportation system it serves, has several important
advantages over these pipelines which have experienced accidents and garnered some adverse public
attention in recent years:
 1) The hub will be located in an existing heavily industrial seaport which already provides
 terminals for gas and other hazardous liquid bulk cargo, and is next to deep water shipping
 lanes, which are likely to already be in heavily commercial areas and not near residential areas.
 2) Pipelines bringing captured CO2 to the hub will likely be installed on existing rights of way for
 pipelines carrying natural gas and other hazardous gases or liquids. These are likely to be
 already located a safe distance away from residential areas.
 3) The CO2 carrying vessels taking CO2 away from the hub will traverse established shipping lanes
 which in almost all cases will be far away from residential areas. There are few deep water
 shipping lanes in the U.S. that are close enough to residential areas such that accidents on the
 ships present an immediate danger to those residential areas.
Additional economic and social risks that may negatively impact the deployment of this project include
the following:

6.4 BENEFITS OF THIS TECHNOLOGY

The benefits of the technology embodied by the T-RICH intermodal CO2 hub include the following:

 It is estimated it will take five years of planning, engineering, and construction to complete and
 commission the T-RICH hub. This will undoubtedly create hundreds of high paying jobs, many of
 which will be filled by members of the local community, and other jobs at the many equipment
 manufacturers, delivery service providers, and third-party contractors at or near the T-RICH project
 site.
 It is estimated it will take approximately 15 full time positions to run and maintain the plant over
 the life of the T-RICH facility. Those positions will likely be filled by members of the local
 community.
 If the plant is expanded in the future as additional emitters commission carbon capture plants and
 send their CO2 to the T-RICH hub, additional construction and operations jobs will be created and
 filled by locals workers.
 The primary benefit of this technology is the critical link it fills in an overall CCS logistics supply
 chain being centrally located in the State of Florida which itself is a stranded CCS location for
 emissions. The particular logistics train that the T-RICH intermodal hub will be a part of will allow
 the prevention of more than 50 million tons of CO2 from entering the atmosphere over a 25-year
 period. With the potential ability to expand the hub to a throughput of 8mtpa, that amount could
 increase to 200 million tons. The prevention of this CO2 from entering the atmosphere will serve a
 critical role in mitigating the effects of climate change such as sea level rise and extreme weather
 events.

6.5 POTENTIAL FINANCING STRUCTURES AND PARTNERSHIPS

6.5.1 LOAN PROGRAMS
There are many existing traditional and emerging programs and instruments which can support the
construction of the T-RICH terminal. Among the most intriguing for eligible companies and projects is
the U.S. Department of Energy’s own Loan Program Office (LPO) which assists infrastructure
development projects like the subject one with favorable discounted loan rates from the U.S. Treasury
Department. Aptamus has met twice with representatives of the LPO and understands the process for
such financial assistance and the favorable terms available for large projects, generally supportive of
loan amounts larger than $200 million, of which T-RICH would certainly qualify. Of course, in the current
political environment the availability of funds from LPO may be restricted in the future, but we currently
view the LPO as a very favorable option. Additionally, there are traditional loan vehicles to consider,
depending on the domestic economic conditions which impact interest rate levels, and which cannot be
anticipated at this time.

                                               Page 115 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

6.5.2 PARTNERSHIPS
Meeting the substantial CAPEX required for a proposed CO2 terminal can also include entering into
partnership arrangements with other firms, particularly those which can provide essential operating
services such as managing the terminal itself, handling and maintaining the equipment, and providing
the workforce. The T-RICH terminal could be an attractive business for an existing terminal operator
familiar with liquid bulk cargo operations who may want to expand into the Florida market and would
be willing to pay for some of the cost of the construction.

6.5.3 EQUITY PARTNERS
Selling minority equity interests in the operating company or company that would be the terminal service
provider would also provide substantial funding of the construction cost. Such an investor could be a
passive or active investor.

6.5.4 VENTURE CAPITAL
The new energy technology projects are of keen interest to venture capital (VC) firms. These firms often
acquire a minority ownership in the company and can include terms which allow it to become majority
owner under certain conditions. VC interest is often high in emerging technologies where traditional
lenders are less inclined to accept the risks involved in new programs.

6.5.5 VOLUNTARY CARBON CREDITS
Voluntary carbon credits are emerging as a means to raise substantial funds to complete climate change
and emission mitigation projects. Among the firms investing in voluntary carbon credits in 2024 are
Google, Microsoft, and Bank of America, each in project amounts greater than $100 million. This
industry is maturing as a viable financing option and we are looking at how to structure the project, from
emitter to sequestration provider, to qualify for carbon credit investment funds.
Carbon credits can play a meaningful—and growing—role in financing carbon capture and storage
projects in the U.S., particularly those that deliver clear, verifiable, and permanent removals of CO₂. As
a funding option for the T-RICH project, which is one part of Aptamus’s larger and related Carbon Ocean
and Storage Transport 20 (COAST20) full FEED level study of the supply chain connecting Florida emitters
to a discharge terminal and sequestration site in Louisiana, carbon credits can provide partial funding
for capture, transport and storage infrastructure project development costs.
How Voluntary Carbon Credits Support CCS in the U.S.:
 1. Providing Financial Support for Emerging Technologies
 Voluntary markets have become a vital revenue source for nascent CCS solutions that aren’t yet fully
 viable on economics alone.
  Direct Air Capture (DAC): This technology is expensive and often not fully supported by policy
 tools like the U.S. 45Q tax credit. Voluntary carbon credit prepurchase agreements offer upfront

                                             Page 116 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 revenue to make projects runway ready. For example, Microsoft has agreed to purchase 500,000
 tons of removal credits from Occidental’s 1PointFive Stratos DAC facility, slated to become the
 world’s largest when it opens in 2025.

  CO₂ Derived Concrete: Some companies permanently sequester CO₂ in building materials. They
 generate and sell carbon credits through the voluntary market to make the economics work—
 combining revenue from product sales, waste handling, and credit monetization.

  CO2 capture at power plants and other industrial facilities that process and transport carbon in
 liquified form to sequestration sites for permanent underground storage. These projects should
 qualify for carbon credits for removing CO2 from being released into the atmosphere, and the
 verification and measurement by independent third parties will provide confidence to investors.
 2. Bioenergy with Carbon Capture and Storage (BECCS)
 BECCS projects that capture carbon from bioenergy processes and store it deep underground can
 qualify for voluntary credits—especially when traditional compliance mechanisms don’t apply.
 One notable example: Red Trail Energy in North Dakota, an ethanol plant, issued 157,000 carbon
 credits via the Puro.earth registry—marking the largest technology-based carbon removal credit
 issuance to date.
 3. Building Trust and Integrity in the Market
 For voluntary carbon credits to be effective, they need to be credible, additional, and permanent.
 U.S. policy guidelines released in 2024 outline principles for high-integrity voluntary carbon
 markets. These stress verified measurement, additionality (projects needing credit revenue to
 proceed), uniqueness (no double issuance), permanence, and social co-benefits.
Voluntary Carbon Credits Can Stabilize the Commercialization of CCS Projects particularly in an era of
limited Federal Funding

  Fills Finance Gaps: CCS projects—particularly early-stage or engineered removal methods—
 often lack sufficient incentives. Voluntary market commitments help bridge that gap.

  Accelerates Innovation: Prepurchase contracts and credit revenues allow technically viable
 solutions to scale more rapidly improving costs and effectiveness sooner.
  Encourages High-Quality Projects: With robust standards and verification, voluntary credit
 markets can direct funds toward genuinely impactful CCS projects, avoiding issues like double
 counting or false claims.
Potential Positive Impact on CCS and the T-RICH CO2 Transport and Storage Project
Voluntary carbon credits are helping to catalyze U.S. CCS development—especially in areas where
traditional incentives fall short. By creating financial certainty for emerging technologies like DAC,
BECCS, and CO₂ utilization and sequestration, they’re driving market-driven innovation. Continued
emphasis on transparency, verification, and integrity will be key to sustaining and scaling this support.
Examples of Leading U.S. Companies Use of Carbon Credits

  Credit mechanism: They generate CO₂ Removal Certificates (CORCs) certified by Puro.earth,

on method of CO2 removal, the per ton price ranges from about $125 to $2,000.

6.6 POTENTIAL UTILIZATION OF TAX CREDITS

Sec. 45Q currently provides for $85/ton for permanently sequestering anthropogenic captured CO2. In the
recently passed policy bill H.R.1, the Congress and Administration continued the Sec. 45Q tax credit for
CCS and the $85/ton for sequestration but also raised the credit to $85/ton for use of captured CO2 for
enhanced oil recovery. At the same time, H.R. 1 essentially terminated most other tax credits for other
new energy products including ammonia and hydrogen production. The continuation of the Sec. 45Q
credit should provide market participants with confidence to continue to engage in the CCS industry and
allow projects to continue to develop towards commercialization
As the current 45Q credit continues to exist in its current amount, it is a significant benefit to the overall
CAPEX calculation as the $85/ton will defray some of the financial risk of the business model. Although
the emitter and sequesterer will also be part of the allocation of the credit, it is our belief that the credit
is not intended to subsidize 100% of the entire supply chain costs. Nonetheless, the credit is useful in
helping establish the CCS supply chain in the U.S. Moreover, until a private market-driven price for carbon
is established, which will emerge over time, the 45Q credit helps set a range in which the per-ton value of
CO2 in the U.S. is evaluated. We note also that the carbon price per ton is set under different factors in
other major international CCS markets such as the EU, UK and Asia. These other systems could influence
the price of carbon in the U.S. as the global CCS industry emerges and matures, and could significantly
assist in how carbon price per ton is determined in the U.S. Finally, carbon prices and federal incentives
are highly variable based on national politics and can change dramatically from today to after the current
Administration ends in 2029. With a timeline for these large and complex infrastructure projects spanning
greater than five years until construction could commence, which is beyond this current political
environment, a different political administration could adopt environmental policies and programs which
could include adoption of larger tax credits and other financial incentive programs by the federal
government.

                                              Page 120 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

7 HAZARD IDENTIFICATION STUDY (HAZID)

HAZID (Hazard Identification) is a workshop-based qualitative risk assessment technique commonly used
for the identification of potential hazards and threats in early design concepts. The objective for the HAZID
workshop that was conducted for T-RICH was to ensure that, at a minimum, process safety,
environmental, regulatory, and operability requirements can be achieved and that there are no
fundamental showstoppers. The session goal was to review the design with a view toward ensuring that
it is sound in principle and accounts for credible hazards and associated project risks. A secondary
objective was to identify design elements that need to be further evaluated in FEED.
Pre-FEED HAZIDs are the first HSE study for new Entr projects. The benefit of performing a Pre-FEED HAZID
is proactive identification of any high impact hazards to project safety or feasibility, thereby providing
essential input to project development decisions before the design progresses too far. This enables a safer
and more cost-effective design with a future minimum cost of change penalty.
The Pre-FEED HAZID process applied for T-RICH was consistent with the principles and requirements of
ISO 31000 - Standard on Risk Management.
This document summarizes the scope and methodology applied to conduct the November 7th, 2024 Pre-
FEED HAZID review for the base case design concept for the T-RICH - Intermodal CO2 Storage Hub. In
addition to HAZID core requirements, this early HAZID also incorporated appropriate elements of what
Entr refers to as a System and Layout (Siting) Review, as well as Working Environment Hazard Risk
Assessment (WEHRA), and Environmental HAZID (ENVID).
The session was well attended, with active participation by all members shown in Table 7.1 and achieved
its purpose and objectives for this phase of the project. There were no critical flaws in the concept
identified and no showstoppers in terms of technical feasibility and safety. A list of higher priority
recommendations can be found in Appendix C.

7.1 SCOPE

Safety, environmental, regulatory, operational, and public/reputational concerns related to the Pre-FEED
base case were within the scope of review of the Pre-FEED HAZID. Construction and execution
methodologies will be subject to a future Constructability Assessment(s) and Risk Assessment(s) at future
project stages. The Pre-FEED HAZID was not a design solution workshop. Issues identified in the Pre-FEED
HAZID requiring further design development were captured as actions, but solutions were not developed.
The Pre-FEED HAZID covered the T-RICH facility battery limits within Entr scope as defined in the Contract.
This was agreed in the Pre-FEED HAZID Charter as:
 • Equipment and operational activities sited within the T-RICH property boundaries including the
 offloading pipeline and loading arm connections to the Tanker at the Berth.
 • Interfaces with the site CO2 incomer pipeline, as well as other utility supplies to the site to support
 operations.
 • Offsite impacts resulting from hazards originating from the T-RICH.
 • Onsite impacts to the T-RICH resulting from fire, explosion, toxic hazards originating from 3rd
 party properties, roadways, railway tracks, and waterways.

7.3 METHODOLOGY

HAZID methodology is simply a what-if “brainstorming” session which considers each major design,
layout, and operational element of the proposed concept and relies on the technical knowledge of the
team to identify potential hazards. The Pre-FEED HAZID team captured issues and concerns (hazards) as
a group applying a consistent approach within each project element in the scope of review (see above).
The HAZID process drew on the expertise and active participation of team members from Entr and
Aptamus and their familiarity with design and operational requirements and aspects related to the
proposed location.
HAZID worksheets were recorded within Excel using computer recording and projection equipment,
shared with remote participants via Microsoft Teams, and visible in the local conference room. Full
recording of all identified Pre-FEED HAZID causes, consequences and safeguards was captured even if no
further recommendations arose for that item. A comprehensive HAZID recording session is more robust
than “reporting by exception” for future record keeping purposes. The Pre-FEED HAZID team did not
recommend “obvious” work activities that are to be carried out as part of routine project execution as per
the Entr Project Execution Model (PEM) since this would result in redundant action item assignments.
The Pre-FEED HAZID recommendations are related to additional risk reduction measures that are either
new considerations or are aspects of the project that are not yet well-defined or poorly understood and

                                            Page 125 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

may present risk that requires additional work to be undertaken to properly characterize and mitigate.
The composition of all assigned Pre-FEED HAZID recommendations strived to be S.M.A.R.T. in nature:
 S - Specific
 M - Measurable
 A - Achievable
 R - Relevant
 T – Timely
Refer to APPENDIX C for actions identified and assigned.

                                            Page 126 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

8 ENVIRONMENTAL, SAFETY AND HEALTH (ES&H) ANALYSIS

This section provides a preliminary description of the major chemical types expected to be required during
the normal operation of the T-RICH project so that associated ES&H risks can be identified, confirmed and
evaluated as the design progresses. At the time of writing concept design had been completed for the T-
RICH site and engineering activities were commencing for the next design stage (FEED). Therefore, while
the class and function of expected major chemicals associated with equipment, process and utilities have
been led by the project design basis, specific chemicals and associated Material Safety Data Sheets
(MSDSs) cannot be confirmed until the designs have matured and engagement with package vendors has
been possible.
The objective of this preliminary evaluation is to describe at a high level the types of major chemicals
expected to be required by current stage of design at the T-RICH site associated with CO2 reception
handling treatment, storage and tanker loading.
A comprehensive ES&H evaluation of the project will be undertaken at a later stage as the designs develop
and necessary inputs become available. This later evaluation will identify chemical hazards and
environmental aspects to confirm compliance with all identified project and regulatory requirements and
objectives. It will seek to demonstrate that safe handling, storage, management and disposal of all
chemicals are feasible and compliant with requirements.
Table 8.1 outlines the types of chemicals typically involved in CO₂ liquefaction, storage and transportation
operations. Chemicals of particular interest from an ES&H perspective, due to their nature or inventory
size, have been separately identified.
A more in-depth list of these chemicals, including their specific applications, handling & storage
requirements, and associated hazards for the T-RICH Terminal site will be provided in a subsequent
detailed ES&H evaluation in a future design stage.

8.1 UTILITIES & CHEMICALS

Utility systems for the liquefaction plant include:
CO2 Vent
The liquefaction plant will be designed to minimize CO2 venting and emissions into the atmosphere,
aiming to essentially eliminate the need for venting during normal steady-state operation. The only
continuous vent anticipated is from the inert gas removal unit, emitting approximately 0.27% CO2 per
mass weight of LCO2 produced. As the tankers are dedicated to this service, CO2 remaining in the tanks
after discharge will meet export specification, therefore during the loading of LCO2, the returning CO2
vapor, with a maximum flow rate of 1250 m³/h from the carrier, will be redirected back to the compression
system.
Both the liquefaction plant and the storage units will be equipped with Pressure Control Valves (PCVs) as
the primary protection against overpressure, and Pressure Safety Valves (PSVs) as secondary protection.

                                              Page 127 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

CO2 vapor will be released to the atmosphere when the pressure in the system exceeds normal operating
levels. In an emergency situation, pressure relief will be managed by the PCVs and the PSVs.
A closed CO2 vent system comprising a vent knockout (KO) drum and an elevated vent stack will be
provided to safely dispose of CO2 at a designated safe location.
Closed Drains
Specific drainage requirements for the liquefaction plant will be identified during future engineering
stages, however, to ensure liquids within the process and storage systems can be disposed of safely and
responsibly, provision for a closed drains drum is included within the liquefaction plant design.
Instrument Air
The instrument air system consists of 2 x 2,000 m3/h air compressor packages, 2 x 2,000 m3/h air dryer
packages, and 2 x 100 m3 instrument air receivers. The air receiver will be designed to provide a 15-minute
supply of instrument air in the event of a compressor shutdown. The spare air compressor, air dryer, and
air receiver will be utilized for purging purposes.
Nitrogen
There are no specific nitrogen consumers identified in this study phase. However, a small nitrogen flow
may be required by the CO2 analyzer package(s). When needed, nitrogen will be supplied to consumers
from nitrogen bottles.

This will maximize protection of adjacent waterways, and will minimize requirements for fixed fire
suppression. For example, transformers shall either be dry transformers or filled with synthetic esters (i.e.
MIDEL 7131) that do not require fixed fire suppression. MIDEL fluids are far less combustible for dielectric
purposes and do not require the fire safety measures compared to traditional mineral oils.
