8.2 INHERENT SAFETY

As discussed elsewhere in this report, the proposed T-RICH site is not situated near residential areas and
is located on a small peninsula within a controlled industrialized area of Port Tampa Bay. While this
provides specific Facility Siting benefits, the safety of the facility site itself and the adjacent industrial
properties, roadways, railways, and waterways requires appropriate consideration.

8.2.1      PIPING
The piping design for CO2 and any hazardous utilities shall take advantage of efficient, remotely isolatable
process segments (sectionalization), with minimal welds and fittings (minimized leakage points).
Appropriate materials of construction for the composition of the fluids managed on site will be selected
in accordance with API, ANSI, and ASME requirements. CO2 storage tanks (ASME Pressure Vessels) shall
be grouped, oriented, and sized to optimize both safety in design and efficient operations. This includes
consideration for safe and efficient tank positive isolation, pressure relief, and efficient confined space
entry requirements for internal pressure vessel inspections.
The details of the piping and equipment design will be progressed and subjected to multiple reviews
including HAZOP in future engineering phases. Refer to Section 4.5.

                                              Page 130 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

8.2.2 PRESSURE RELIEF & VENTING
Pressure control and relief arrangements for major equipment packages such as compressors and
significant piping segments will be considered based on design reviews and HAZOP-LOPA studies. It is
anticipated that some local equipment and trapped pressure CO2 segments will be vented locally. Larger
inventories of CO2 that may be vented from the process during an upset or emergency relief scenario may
require special consideration, such as routing to the main vent stack. Displacement venting of transport
tankers at pressures higher than the system can recycle will be directed to the main vent stack.
Evaluation of venting rates and risk will be subject to further Process engineering, potential dispersion
analysis, and risk assessment in FEED. Assessments will include considerations related to dry ice and water
ice formation and the identification of appropriate safeguards to ensure venting capacities remain
available when required.

8.2.3 STORAGE UNIT BLEVE RISK
CO2 will be stored at medium pressure (15 barg) in liquid phase at (-26°C) onsite in multiple insulated
pressure vessels. There have been several CO2 BLEVE incidents globally resulting from rapid pressurization
of large storage units due to a loss of cooling resulting in a rapid phase change to gas combined with
inadequate or blocked pressure relief devices.
It is not feasible to design facilities to withstand the consequences of a BLEVE, since BLEVEs generate
catastrophic explosion pressures and projectiles. BLEVEs are addressed in process safety design by
providing sufficient independent protection layers (IPLs) to reduce the aggregate risk of any BLEVE
occurrence on site to not more than 1.0E-06 / year. This is in accordance with best practices for risk targets
for industrialized areas as established by the CCPS (Center for Chemical Process Safety). This is typically
achieved through the application of some combination of the following IPLs:
  Inherent process and mechanical design (operating envelopes, limiting tank quantities), process
 control systems
  Active Safety Instrumented Systems (SIS) to monitor tank pressure and initiate relief through
 pressure control valve(s)
  Passive Pressure Relief Valve(s)

  Passive Burst Disc(s)

  Relief elements are sized, insulated (potentially heated) to prevent freezing blockage due to CO2
 phase changes and Joule-Thompson effects.
Facility relief system design and IPL reliability targets will be further evaluated and quantified as the design
progresses and subsequent risk analyses.

8.2.4 VENT STACK ARRANGEMENT & PLACEMENT
There are 2 general scenarios for which a tall vent stack is required:

                                               Page 131 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  Displacement venting during tanker loading

  Possible emergency process or pipeline venting scenarios
Emergency venting to the vent stack not considered in the design basis for the base case. Offsite pipeline
venting through the site vent stack is also not currently considered in the concept base case at this time.
Venting due to tanker operations will occur at a low mass flow rate relative to any emergency venting
case.
The vent stack is positioned on the site at a height to avoid levels of CO2 from reaching 5,000ppm onsite
where personnel may be present. This is the ACGIH threshold limit value (TLV) for an 8-hour work day. It
is also the same value applied by NIOSH and OSHA. This TLV shall be reduced to 3,300ppm for a 12-hour
work shift (5,000ppm x 8/12).
For an emergency process vent case, the stack would need to be positioned and at a height to avoid levels
of CO2 from reaching 30,000ppm where people may be present. This is the ACGIH short term exposure
limit (STEL), which is not more than 15 minutes of exposure (evacuate area).
Preliminary work performed in this pre-FEED phase has informed the placement of the vent stack in the
northeast area of the site and at a height of 30m. This will be optimized, and the height likely reduced,
with refined venting process conditions and dispersion work in FEED.
Noise levels for stack venting are not expected to be of concern (Refer to Section 8.3.8 Noise Control).
Any possible emergency venting cases will be short in duration and infrequent. Efforts shall be made to
select a vent design that minimizes noise levels and should not exceed 120 dBA in worker locations. This
will be evaluated as the design progresses.

8.2.5 FIRE & VAPOR PROOF OCCUPIED BUILDINGS
The fire ratings of partitions, divisions, doors and openings, and provision of appropriate air quality for
occupied buildings, shall be in accordance with applicable NFPA and OSHA requirements. See also Section
8.3.6 SIP HVAC Pressurization & Dilution.
Control rooms and offices will need to be separated from other industrial spaces with fire-rated barriers
to prevent fire spread. NFPA 101 - Life Safety Code and IBC (International Building Code) defines the
requirements for fire-rated walls, doors, and HVAC. Doors to control rooms shall be assigned fire ratings
consistent with wall ratings and include self-closing mechanisms. Penetrations for cables, pipes, and
conduits in fire-rated walls will be sealed with fire-stop materials to maintain the integrity of fire barriers.
A Fire Protection Philosophy and Preliminary Fire Division Layouts will be developed as the design
progresses.

8.2.6 SITE SECURITY
Site security requirements for industrial properties in the U.S. are based on various factors, including the
type of industry, level of risk, and applicable regulations. Requirements include physical, digital, and
procedural security measures to protect property, assets, and personnel. OSHA standards do not mandate

                                               Page 132 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

specific security design measures but do state the need for protocols to prevent unauthorized access to
hazardous areas. The Maritime Transportation Security Act and Transportation Worker Identification
Credential (TWIC) define requirements for personnel working in ports. It should be noted that access to
the peninsula is controlled by Port Tampa Bay and is already off limits to the public.
The following physical site and layout aspects shall be considered to develop a Site Security Philosophy in
FEED:

  Perimeter Security: Fencing, walls, and barriers around the property to prevent unauthorized
 access.

  Controlled Access Points: Entry gates and security checkpoints to regulate who enters and exits.

  Lighting: Sufficient outdoor lighting around entrances, parking areas, and perimeters to deter
 intruders and improve camera visibility.
  Surveillance: CCTV cameras positioned throughout the property.
  Signage: Clearly displayed signs around the perimeter, indicating restricted areas and notifying of
 surveillance.
The following control and information systems cybersecurity guidelines and requirements shall be
considered to develop a Cybersecurity Philosophy:

  National Institute of Standards and Technology (NIST) Cybersecurity Framework
  Cybersecurity and Infrastructure Security Agency (CISA) Guidelines
  Transportation Sector (TSA Pipeline Security Guidelines)

8.2.7 MARKINGS & SIGNAGE
OSHA requires that all workplaces display safety signs, floor, stairway, and equipment platform and piping
markings that inform, warn, and guide employees and visitors about potential hazards. Appropriate
Hazard and Life Safety signage, symbols, and markings will be provided onsite as required in accordance
with ANSI Standards. ANSI Z535 establishes best practices for the design, color coding, and formatting of
safety signs and labels, which OSHA incorporates by reference. Additionally, NFPA "diamond" labels will
be applied as necessary onsite to show flammability, health, instability, and specific hazards on any
chemical containers and areas where chemicals are stored or handled.
This will be further evaluated in subsequent design phases.

8.2.8 ACCESS & EGRESS
Appropriate site access and diverse means of egress offsite and to/from work areas to shelters onsite will
be provided in the facility siting layout. Placement will be based on worker distribution on site,
requirements for emergency response from offsite assets, and traffic "pinch points," for example the
interaction of trains, trucks, and roadways intended for dual-emergency use. Requirements will be
adhered to as outlined in OSHA and NFPA 101 Standards.

                                             Page 133 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

This will be further evaluated and a Preliminary Layout developed as the design progresses.

8.2.9 LIGHTNING PROTECTION
Lightning and surge protection requirements for the facility will be developed in detail in a later study
phase in compliance with NFPA 70 and NFPA 780. Lightning protection requirements are governed by a
combination of national standards and codes designed to ensure safe and effective protection of facilities
from lightning strikes. Primary standards and guidelines shall include:

  NFPA 780 - Standard for the Installation of Lightning Protection Systems

  UL 96A - Standard for Installation Requirements for Lightning Protection Systems

  NFPA 70 - National Electrical Code (NEC)

8.3 ACTIVE SAFETY (ENGINEERING CONTROLS)

8.3.1 FIRE AND GAS DETECTION, ALARM, CONTROL - OUTDOOR LOCATIONS
Fixed CO2 detection, alarm and control will be logically distributed on site as appropriate. Placement
locations and alarm and trip setpoints will be assigned consistent with ACGIH TLVs and STELs. Toxic gas
detection devices shall be integrated into an Instrumented Safety System that is independent of the
Process Control System and shall be in accordance with ANSI 61511 and ANSI 61508. If there is a need to
provide hydrocarbon gas detection near the east and south boundaries of the site due to LPG hazards,
that shall be considered in FEED and integrated into the same system.
Fire detection, alarm, and control actions for outdoor equipment packages and outdoor areas (if classified
as a requirement by NFPA) shall be provided. Fire (flame, heat, smoke) detection will be distributed on
site as appropriate in accordance with NFPA requirements and shall be integrated into the same
Instrumented Safety System as CO2 and other gas detection.
A Safety Requirements Specification, Fire and Gas Philosophy, Systems Topology, and Detector Layouts
will be developed as the design progresses.

8.3.2 FIRE AND GAS DETECTION, ALARM, CONTROL - OCCUPIED BUILDINGS
OSHA and NFPA 72 - National Fire Alarm and Signaling Code requires control rooms, offices, and occupied
premises to have a reliable fire detection and alarm system and define design requirements. Suppression
systems will meet NFPA requirements and may be a combination of sprinkler, non-water-based
suppression, or early smoke detection and active smoke control.
Occupied buildings' detection, alarm, and suppression system(s) can be self-contained, and independent
of any systems provided for the site exterior process areas and are therefore not required to be ANSI
61511/61508 compliant. However, such systems must be FM or UL rated. See also Section 8.3.4 Fire
Fighting and Water Supply.

                                               Page 134 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

A fire detection, alarm, and control philosophy, and preliminary detector layout, will be developed as the
design progresses.

8.3.3 PROCESS, FIRE AND GAS, EMERGENCY SHUTDOWN SYSTEM
The process emergency shutdown system (ESD) will be the primary means of shutting down the plant
processes during an emergency to a safe (neutral) state. All safety functions in this automated,
instrumented safety system will achieve a SIL2 reliability level (Probability of Failure on Demand < 0.01)
in accordance with ANSI 61511 requirements.
The fire and gas system (F&G) will be the primary means of detecting toxic gas leaks, any offsite flammable
gas migration onto site (LPG site and train loading areas), or any fires, and initiating actions including
sending inputs to the ESD system to perform critical safety output functions. The combined reliability of
F&G functions and ESD functions shall achieve a SIL2 reliability level in accordance with ANSI 61511
requirements.
There will be a separate process control system (PCS) including a process shutdown system (PSD) for the
T-RICH facility. While the PCS and PSD perform safety related actions, it is primarily an operability control
system, and is not required to meet ANSI 61508 or ANSI 61511 SIL requirements.
Shutdown initiators and actions for each level (and related system) of shutdown will differ from plant to
plant. However, the following can be taken as a general guideline and will be defined further for T-RICH
in FEED:
  Level 0 Shutdown - ESD (F&G) - Full Plant Shutdown
  Level 1 Shutdown - ESD (F&G) - Full Process
  Level 2 Shutdown - ESD (F&G) - Process Unit(s) within the Process
  Level 3 Shutdown - PSD - Total Process Shutdown
  Level 4 Shutdown - PSD - Shutdown for a Process Unit(s) within the Process

  Level 5 Shutdown - PSD - Shutdown of Individual Equipment and Utilities
The Fire & Gas System, Emergency Shutdown System, and PCS/PSD systems shall be independent of each
other, with the ESD and F&G powered by emergency UPS power for at least 30 minutes after the loss of
facility main power to ensure sufficient system operability time to effect and verify a safe plant condition.
Field elements for safety functions (i.e. sensors and valves required to make the process safe) may be
shared between these systems, but only if they meet ANSI 61508/61511 requirements and are
independently hardwired to each system and UPS powered.
A Functional Safety Requirements Specification (SRS), Shutdown Hierarchy, and Philosophy will be
developed as the design progresses.

                                              Page 135 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

8.3.4 FIRE FIGHTING AND WATER SUPPLY
Fire Fighting Systems on site will be minimized through the use of non-combustible fluids and materials
where feasible. It is expected that there will be NFPA 13 compliant fixed sprinkler protection systems in
offices, workshops, and mechanical rooms on site in accordance with all NFC/NFPA 1 occupancy and usage
classifications for buildings.
Electrical Rooms, UPS Rooms, and Control Rooms may require early smoke detection, active smoke
control, and either water mist or non-water suppression as appropriate to meet NFPA 17, 90A, 750, or
2001 requirements.
Fire suppression systems protecting sensitive electrical equipment, where required, will be of a
firefighting medium that avoids electrical shock and equipment damage. Such fire protection systems may
require local reservoir tanks, pumps, and control panels (package skids) to meet system requirements or
may be tied into the local port fire water main supply.
Exterior locations shall be assessed for fire risk from equipment packages as a result of motor lubricants
or electrical fires and assigned protective measures to meet regulatory requirements. Fixed fire
suppression systems are not expected to be required on site in most exterior locations other than some
NFPA 14 water hydrant (or monitor) standpipes and hoses fed from the existing port water main that
feeds the LPG fire protection systems adjacent to the site. Such stations will require supplementation of
foam concentrate eduction tanks or carts where the risk of Class B fires may be present (truck and train
loading stations, berth area).
NFPA 10 requirements for portable (handheld, wheeled) fire extinguishers will inform their distribution
on site for Class A, B, C fires where appropriate.
A firefighting system philosophy, preliminary UFD, and layouts will be developed as the design progresses.

8.3.5 SHELTERS IN PLACE (SIP)
OSHA Process Safety Management (PSM) Standard, 29 CFR 1910.119, outlines requirements for managing
hazards associated with handling highly hazardous chemicals and encourages facilities to establish SIPs as
part of emergency planning. The following regulations and guidelines are referenced:

  EPA Risk Management Plan (RMP) Rule, 40 CFR Part 68 - Requires facilities to consider measures
 like SIP for toxic chemical releases.

  API RP 752 - Guidelines for Management of Hazards Associated with Location of Process Plant
 Buildings - includes SIP as a protective measure for process safety management.

  CCPS Guidelines for Evaluating Process Plant Buildings for External Explosions, Fires, and Toxic
 Releases - provides detailed risk assessment and design recommendations for SIP areas to
 withstand toxic gas exposure.
The control room and potentially a portion of the administration building shall be designated as the
primary SIP in the event of a large CO2 release. It is considered safer to remain sheltered in place (SIP)
rather than go outside in the event of a large CO2 release which is expected to safely disperse within 30-

                                            Page 136 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

60 minutes. Subject to a further evaluation of workforce distribution, smaller secondary SIP(s) may be
required in the northwest (marine berth area) and eastern (remote from primary SIP) property areas.
Designated "safe areas" offsite, subject to dispersion analysis in FEED, may alternatively be assigned for
personnel to reach on foot rather than providing physical secondary SIPs. This will be further evaluated
and risk assessed in FEED.
In the event of a fire on site, fire muster area(s) will be designated in area safety layouts to be developed
as the design progresses.

8.3.6 SIP HVAC PRESSURIZATION & DILUTION
Occupied buildings that serve as SIPs for toxic gas events will be structurally designed with specified
minimum air infiltration rates. Leak rate (vapor tight) criteria will be supplemented by the provision of
mechanical ventilation (HVAC) for the SIP envelopes which shall maintain a positive pressure in the SIP
relative to exterior conditions. SIP envelopes include vestibules and differential pressure monitoring with
alarms upon loss of SIP overpressure. CO2, hydrocarbon gas (if appropriate), and smoke detection in SIP
HVAC intakes shall result in the closure of gastight fire damper(s) and the HVAC units switching to
emergency UPS power in a recycle mode.
Safety related HVAC for SIPs shall be able to function on emergency power (UPS) in a recirculation mode
for the duration of a large CO2 leak case (up to 60 minutes). Fresh air will be taken from a safe location (to
the maximum extent practical) at rates not less than 12 air changes per hour (ACH) for occupied buildings
and SIP(s) to ensure adequate air dilution and pressures. Placement of air intake(s) shall be further
evaluated and optimized based on further dispersion work conducted in FEED.
NFPA 90A - Standard for Installation of Air-Conditioning and Ventilating Systems specifies the design of
HVAC systems in buildings, focusing on fire prevention, smoke control, and fire spread containment
including the installation of fire and smoke dampers within ducts that penetrate fire-rated walls, floors,
or ceilings to prevent fire and smoke from spreading through the HVAC system.
HVAC systems may also function as smoke control systems, using pressurization and exhaust to contain
or redirect smoke in case of fire. However, this may be prohibitive without large quantities of back-up
power onsite.
Specific SIP and muster criteria and layout concepts will be developed as the design progresses.

8.3.7 LIGHTING
ANSI RP-7-20 Recommended Practice for Lighting Industrial Facilities provides comprehensive
recommendations for lighting levels in various industrial settings to enhance safety, efficiency, and
visibility. OSHA lighting requirements for industrial properties adopt ANSI and NFPA 101 - Life Safety Code
(Refer to Table 8.3).

8.3.8 NOISE CONTROL
Noise levels onsite shall be minimized. Noise levels above 85 decibels in outdoor areas require "noise
mitigation measures" (i.e. PPE and acoustic insulation). See Fig. 8.1 for maximum allowable noise levels.
For interior workspaces such as control rooms and offices, ANSI S12.60 specifies acceptable noise levels
for environments where clear communication is critical - 45-50 dBA for control rooms. Alarms in rooms
shall be clearly audible but not excessively loud, typically designed to be 10–15 dBA above the ambient
noise level in order to be noticeable without disturbance or discomfort.
ANSI/ASHRAE Standard 62.1 provides recommendations on ventilation and noise levels for acceptable
indoor air quality and acoustic comfort, also suggesting a maximum of 45 dBA for offices in noisy
environments.
Hillsborough County has a noise ordinance for offsite noise levels, i.e. adjacent industrial sites that receive
noise from T-RICH. The noise standards are determined by the type of land receiving the noise. The
continuous decibel limit for industrial receiving properties is 70 dBA, measured as an equivalent
continuous noise level, Leq, over a 10-minute period.

                                                Page 138 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Initial predictive noise levels will be evaluated in FEED applying good practices with generic noise values
for standard equipment and activities. This will be further evaluated as the project progresses in future
engineering phases with site specific equipment data specifications, when available.

 Figure 8.1: OSHA Permissible Noise Levels

8.4 ACTIVE SAFETY (ADMINISTRATIVE & PPE CONTROLS)

8.4.1 PPE PLACEMENT
Portable Fire Fighting units, Self Contained Breathing Apparatuses, and miscellaneous Life Safety and
Emergency Response equipment will be staged in protected rooms or onsite cabinets and marked as
appropriate as per OSHA, ANSI, and NFPA requirements. Placement will consider the expected worksite
personnel distribution relative to hazards. Safety Showers with Eyebath stations may be required in
workshops, warehouses, or any work area where the risk of exposure to hazardous chemicals on skin,
eyes, and mouth may exist. Such systems shall in accordance with ANSI Z358.1.
A PPE (fire and life safety) philosophy and preliminary layouts will be developed as the design progresses.

8.4.2 EMERGENCY RESPONSE
Emergency response is ordinarily the responsibility of the Operator. However, alarm systems for process
safety events are critical for alerting neighboring properties, emergency response teams, and facility
personnel to hazardous situations that could impact areas beyond the immediate facility.

  The EPA’s Risk Management Plan Rule (40 CFR Part 68) requires facilities that handle hazardous
 chemicals above threshold quantities must include the provision to notify nearby properties of
 potential offsite impacts.

                                              Page 139 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

  OSHA's Process Safety Management Standard (29 CFR 1910.119) requires appropriate means to
 coordinate with local emergency responders, which often includes systems to alert offsite
 stakeholders.

  The Emergency Planning and Community Right-to-Know Act requires facilities to report chemical
 hazards to local emergency planning committees and includes provisions for offsite alarm
 coordination.
Refer to the next Section, Types of Offsite Alarms.

8.4.3 TYPES OF OFFSITE ALARMS
There will be a need for an offsite notification system in the event of a major CO2 release. At a minimum
this will likely include:
 Sirens to warn nearby properties and facility employees of a CO2 release. Sirens shall be designed to
 be heard at considerable distance. Tone, pattern, and duration shall be distinct from other area alarms
 to avoid confusion.
 Visual Signals such as colored (i.e. blue for toxic gases, red for fires or flammable gases) beacons
 installed along the facility perimeter and adjacent road/rail locations will provide visual warning
 where sirens may be ineffective.
 Integration of Siren/Beacon alarms with real-time monitoring (detection) and process control systems
 allowing automatic activation if sensors detect a sufficiently hazardous condition for offsite receptors.
An alarm philosophy for offsite properties and transportation corridors will be further developed as the
design progresses. See also Section 3.7.

8.5 CONCLUSIONS

This preliminary ES&H analysis has been prepared to indicate the types and classes of chemicals normally
expected to be used at the T-RICH hub to highlight any significant concerns for future detailed evaluation.
At this stage no ES&H concerns have been identified that might affect project feasibility and compliance
based on the information available. No novel or untested chemicals are currently included within the
design. Detailed ES&H evaluation of associated hazards and confirmation of regulatory compliance will be
undertaken at later stages of the ongoing design as further design detail is developed and following risk
identification workshops.
Design and process alternatives have been considered. The design bases under development for the next
stage take these into account to reduce the risk to the external environment and reduce the requirement
for on-site chemical inventories. Environmentally friendly alternatives for required chemicals have been
preliminarily identified and will be considered for use where feasible (e.g. transformer oil, switchgear
insulation, refrigerant gases). The process design requires no consumable chemicals, thus minimizing
inventories held on-site and associated ES&H risks.

                                               Page 140 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

9 CO 2 MANAGEMENT PLAN

This plan outlines procedures for the intake, monitoring, and quality assurance of CO₂ via pipelines, roads,
rails, and marine vessels under T-RICH ownership or control, incorporating the best practices from
international CCUS initiatives documented in the References section. The philosophy of CO₂ management
at T-RICH is grounded in proactively identifying, quantifying, and mitigating risks across the entire
transport and storage chain to ensure operational safety, regulatory compliance, and public confidence
in a high-integrity, low-probability failure environment.

9.1 CO₂ ORIGIN AND SPECIFICATIONS

The captured CO₂ is mainly derived from the combustion of natural gas using amine-based separation
techniques. The incoming CO₂ must satisfy the following composition standards:
     ≥ 99.81 mol% CO₂
     H₂O ≤ 30 ppm
 H₂S ≤ 9 ppm
     NOₓ ≤ 1.5 ppm
 SOₓ, hydrocarbons, NH₃, and amines ≤ 10 ppm each
Compliance is ensured by:

  Gas Chromatography (GC) for major and trace components
  Fourier Transform Infrared (FTIR) spectroscopy for acid gases and VOCs
  Chilled mirror hygrometers for moisture
  Electrochemical sensors for H₂S and CO
  ISO 6145-aligned sample validation

9.2 CO₂ TRANSPORT PARAMETERS

9.2.1 PIPELINES
CO₂ is transported at ambient temperature and ~15 barg pressure, with delivery rates of 5,600
tonnes/day. The SCADA systems provide continuous monitoring. Pipeline risk probability is defined as 0.7–
6.1 × 10⁻⁴ km⁻¹·yr⁻¹ (Mahgerefteh et al., 2012).

9.2.2 ROAD AND RAIL
Insulated pressurized tanks (15–17 barg, -25 to -30°C) will be monitored using onboard sensors. The road
and rail risk probability are defined as 2x10-5 per journey (IPCC, 2005).

                                                Page 141 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

9.2.3      MARINE
Marine transport operations utilize 20,000 m³ LCO₂ carriers on a biweekly basis, with a loading capacity
of 1,250 m³ per hour. The dual-pump and vapor-return systems adhere to International Maritime
Organization (IMO) compliance standards. The risk of loss during marine transport is quantified as ≤ 10⁻⁴
per shipment (Brownsort et al. 2014).

9.3 STORAGE INFRASTRUCTURE

CO₂ will be stored in 11 insulated vessels, with a total capacity of 30,000 m³, under cryogenic conditions
(-26.2°C, 15 barg). The worst-case CO₂ dispersion scenario at the T-RICH site involves the rupture of a 6-
inch line from a pressurized liquid CO₂ tank, releasing 640 kg/s of CO₂ in a dense, ground-hugging plume
under calm wind conditions. This release could affect much of the site and nearby infrastructure, including
Pendola Point Road and adjacent industrial facilities, with concentrations exceeding fatality thresholds.
To mitigate this risk, the design includes three-sided containment walls around the storage area and will
undergo detailed CFD modeling and natural ventilation studies in FEED to refine response strategies and
minimize offsite risk.

9.4 MONITORING AND ENVIRONMENTAL CONTROLS

Fiber-optic cables and chemical sensors are employed to detect leakages at critical junctures. The system
is equipped with automated emergency isolation capabilities, responding in less than two minutes. To
maintain infrastructure safety and enable swift corrective action, the facility will incorporate a continuous
leak detection system at key locations, including CO₂ pipelines, compressors, storage tanks, and injection
interfaces. Fixed sensors calibrated for CO₂ will be complemented by portable detection units for
maintenance personnel. These systems provide real-time alerts when ambient CO₂ concentrations exceed
safety thresholds. These sensors are part of the safety and environmental critical elements (SECEs) and
will be integrated into the Emergency Shutdown System (ESD) for automatic containment isolation when
needed. Safety drills and calibrated dispersion models are utilized to ensure adherence to NFPA 55, EPA
RMP, and PHMSA regulations.
Under future consideration is the deployment of on-site sensors to monitor minor seismic activities and
subterranean movements in real time. The data collected from these sensors could be integrated into a
risk model that utilizes geological data, historical operations, and performance thresholds to predict and
manage risks. This system would be designed to provide early warnings regarding ground stability issues,
thereby contributing to the safety of infrastructure through automated alerts and maintenance strategies.
Cradle-to-gate life cycle assessments (LCAs) could also be performed in accordance with ISO 14040/14044
standards. The impact metrics evaluated could include Global Warming Potential (GWP), energy intensity,
and water usage. These assessments could be conducted annually utilizing life-cycle assessment software,
such as OpenLCA or SimaPro.

                                              Page 142 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

9.5 CONTRACTUAL ASSURANCE AND SYSTEM RELIABILITY

T-RICH will enforce receiving contracts with defined CO2 specifications, denies receiving, and manages
data-reporting gaps. If incoming CO₂ fails to meet specification, it will be diverted to a bypass line for
venting, temporary holding, or controlled rejection back to the supplier in accordance with pre-agreed
interconnection protocols. Off-spec incidents are logged and reviewed as part of performance audits.
Dual-train liquefaction enables a 95% up-time. These systems are designed to withstand tropical storm
events. Reliability targets: ≥ 98% availability, MTBF > 3,000 hr.

9.6 IMPLEMENTATION PHASES AND FAULT MONITORING

The execution follows five phases: (1) mobilization and risk registry, (2) contractor onboarding, (3) detailed
engineering, (4) Commissioning with FAT/SAT, and (5) operational monitoring and feedback loops. Seismic
sensors can track geo-mechanical changes near critical infrastructure.

9.7 SUMMARY

This plan represents the best-in-class strategy for managing industrial CO₂ with an emphasis on safety,
performance, regulatory alignment, and environmental stewardship.

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

10 COMMUNITY BENEFITS

This Community Benefit Plan will establish a baseline understanding of the community in which T-RICH is
intended to operate. This is a desktop study that provides information about the people that live, work,
learn, and gather in the region. Additionally, this report identifies other regions’ practices and programs
that successfully incorporated communities in commercial projects.
Through this desktop study, Aptamus aims to better understand its fellow community members and
identify how to best collaborate with Tampeños to establish a terminal that can facilitate the protection
of property, people, and businesses. This report identifies active members of the Tampa community
including organizations, agencies, and departments that are embedded within the Project Region and
have proven that they represent and support the community.

10.1 COMMUNITY ENGAGEMENT FRAMEWORK

Successful Community Benefit Plans employ a four-part framework19 to engage with various stakeholders.
Given that T-RICH is a desktop study, this report will address the Design portion. Subsequent aspects of
the next phase full FEED COAST20 project will delve into the Plan, Implement, and Learn portions.

10.2 BACKGROUND

Aptamus was established in 2024 as a subsidiary of Overseas Shipholding Group, Inc. (“OSG”), a firm that
has been present and working in the Tampa community for over 25 years. Moreover, OSG’s commercial
tank vessels call on Port Tampa Bay regularly for liquid commodity cargo deliveries such as refined crude
oil products for distribution throughout the State. The company’s employees, coworkers, and working
environment are experiencing firsthand the impacts of disruptive incidents such as increased hurricanes,
extreme destructive weather events, high-tide flooding, and impacts of sea level rise. The Aptamus and
OSG teams recognize their ability to positively impact the Tampa region through CO2 emission reduction
and workforce-centered community education, job and economic opportunity support, and project
collaboration.
As noted in a May 2025 interview, Jeffrey Williams, Aptamus President, highlighted “[…] carbon emission
reduction helps improve public health, grow local economies through job creation and innovation, and is
good for businesses too, all these benefits are in addition to mitigating the destructive consequences of
climate change.”20
An Aptamus community benefits initiative is foundational to the T-RICH project’s long-term success.
Aptamus and OSG will benefit financially and operationally if it best understands the community in which
it operates and if the community understands the companies’ goals and operations. This understanding
will be established through the T-RICH research and further explored through follow-on projects such as
COAST20, the FEED level study that includes the design of the CO2 transport vessel and the intended
discharge port on the Mississippi River near Baton Rouge, LA. It will translate into meaningful
collaboration between the stakeholders in the Tampa community, Aptamus employees, and Aptamus
projects. Early and frequent community engagement leads to more successful and timely industrial
project completions21.
The long-term goal of this Desktop Community Benefit Plan is to prioritize and encourage socioeconomic
conditions for those in the Project Region and to develop the local, skilled workforce needed for T-RICH
to succeed. The T-RICH report supports future endeavors with the COAST20 CO2 vessel which has the
potential to facilitate a technological transition throughout the entire U.S. maritime sector. It will
introduce a new type of cargo, new ship design for that cargo, and new skilled labor and professional jobs
that create new opportunities for the workforce within Tampa. This will provide a pillar of support for
realizing economic and energy dominance goals within those communities. These jobs are likely to include
higher wages because of the enhanced and differentiated skill sets. This includes shipboard jobs as well
as shore-side jobs needed to manage ship operations, crewing, provisioning, and vessel management
activities.

10.3 REGION OF SCOPE

The Tampa Regional Intermodal Carbon Hub (T-RICH) at Port Tampa Bay is intended to be the principal
gateway for intermediate storage of approximately 2 million tons of captured CO2 annually from emitters
across the state of Florida. Tampa is in Hillsborough County on the west coast of Florida. For the purposes
of this Desktop Community Benefit Plan, Hillsborough County is considered the Region of Scope.

10.4 DEMOGRAPHIC OVERVIEW

Tampa has a population of approximately 415,000 people, 47% of which are Black, Hispanic, or Latino22.
4% of the city’s population are U.S. veterans. 90% of people living in Tampa have a high school degree or
higher.
The Centers for Disease Control and Prevention and Agency for Toxic Substances and Disease Registry
Social Vulnerability Index is a place-based index, database, and mapping application designed to identify
and quantify communities experiencing social vulnerability. It measures and monitors regions’ social
vulnerabilities which are demographic and socioeconomic factors (such as poverty, lack of access to
transportation, and crowded housing) that adversely affect communities that encounter hazards and
other community-level stressors. Based on 2020 data and on a score from 0 (least vulnerable) – 1 (most
vulnerable), Tampa is rated 0.738 (high)23. The Center for Disease Control defines Social Vulnerability as
“the potential negative effects on communities caused by external stresses on human health.”
Aptamus aims to address these challenges in its community through innovative technologies and
processes that have the potential to generate inclusive workforce development opportunities, economic
improvements, and reduction of local environmental pollution. Aptamus aims to establish itself as an
industry leader that achieves commercial success while generating community wealth. The Aptamus team
envisions community wealth as improved public health, consistent and well-paying jobs, and hard and
soft skills training that can be applied throughout an individual’s lifelong career.

10.5 LANGUAGES SPOKEN

28% of Tampa’s population speaks a language other than English in their home (primarily Spanish). Tampa
is located within Hillsborough County which has over 80,000 K-12 students that identify as Hispanic24 and
may face communication barriers. 12 public schools within the county offer dual immersion programs

developed by and comprised of local residents. These organizations represent the Region of Scope’s
population and have established deep-rooted relationships with the people that may be impacted by T-
RICH. Certain of these organizations are local subsets of national organizations which provide additional
resources to the Tampa-focused branch.

categories:
 ▫ Community Outreach
 ▫ Education
 ▫ Healthcare
 ▫ Local Government
These groups consist of institutional and grassroot organizations, for-profit and non-profit companies,
publicly funded, privately financed, and member-driven associations. The common thread among these
seemingly dissimilar entities is that they exist to serve and support the individuals that live, work, learn,
and gather in T-RICH’s Region of Scope. Certain of the below listed organizations aim to serve a particular
subset of the Tampa population, but, in aggregate, these Active Community Members represent a fuller
picture of the people of Hillsborough County.
Aptamus’ community engagement plan is strategically diversified to encompass different entities. It is
critical that Aptamus connect with and establish relationships with as many organization types as possible.
This will support T-RICH’s timely success. This intentionally expanded engagement will benefit a larger
portion of the Tampa community because more local folks will have access to CO2 education and T-RICH
information.

community. The Tampa Bay chapter's goal is to empower Hispanic-owned businesses by providing
essential resources, invaluable networking opportunities, and robust advocacy and to foster connection,
collaboration, and success across the diverse Hispanic community.

seeks to drive advancements in renewable energy, pollution control, hazard mitigation, and ecosystem
services while providing a “continuum” of support for hundreds of BlueTech startups.

 ▫ BayCare

 ▫ BayCare - St. Joseph's Hospital

rankings. Tampa General Hospital is a research and academic medical center. Located on Davis Island,
Tampa General Hospital is approximately two miles from Port Tampa Bay.

10.12 PRELIMINARY PROGRAM RESEARCH

The Aptamus team aims to implement programs that can achieve the most positive impact to the people
of Tampa while maintaining alignment with OSG’s mission, values, and financial budget. Other programs
throughout the transportation industry have succeeded and some have stumbled in this pursuit. The T-
RICH desktop study identified those plans and analyzed their accomplishments and challenges. Through
these lessons learned from these peer initiatives, Aptamus aims to avoid previously identified missteps
and seek to leverage proven successes. This early-stage assessment will support a more timely and
impactful result for all stakeholders in this T-RICH and at later stages of the COAST20 project.
As noted above, it is critical to research, understand, and establish working collaborations with the Region
of Scope’s stakeholders before a company undertakes a community benefit program. The Aptamus team
is committed to developing relationships and co-designing initiatives. Based on the preliminary
understanding of the Tampa community, Aptamus may consider proposing the below-listed programs
with Active Community Members. If appropriate, the Aptamus team and ACMs may co-design custom
versions of these programs to best fit the project’s stakeholders’ needs.

                                               Page 154 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

Preliminary Program Ideas:

Develop and Execute Hands-on Training for Tampa First Responders
Program Goal: Properly prepare the professionals that could be called upon to protect
 people and property during a liquid CO2-related emergency at the T-RICH
 terminal or on the COAST20 vessel.

Overview: OSG is a proven expert in safe transportation of liquid cargo.28 As noted in
 the firm’s Health and Safety Policy, Environmental Protection and Social
 Responsibility Policy,29 safety is top priority across all OSG activities. This is
 critical given the quantity of liquid cargo OSG transports over waterways, and
 liquid cargo’s potential ability to cause an emergency situation such as a fire
 or blowout.
 T-RICH could be the U.S.’ first captured CO2 storage and transportation hub.30
 This puts OSG in a distinct position; one that simultaneously introduces the
 Region of Scope to new economic opportunities, and to a new source of
 potentially hazardous scenarios.
 The OSG and Aptamus teams are committed to handling and transporting the
 material in as safe a manner as possible. They are already collaborating with
 The American Bureau of Shipping (ABS) to determine the best vessel design
 and standards of practice. In April 2025 ABS, after extensive review of the
 vessel design drawings and related documents, issued an Approval in
 Principle to Aptamus, the first required step towards construction ready
 drawings for a new maritime vessel.31
 To realize the firms’ commitments to excellence and safety, they recognize
 despite all best efforts, a mistake may occur, and they aim to be prepared for
 any potential emergency.
 To ensure preparedness, OSG and Aptamus teams must consider all potential
 emergency causes, their many potential outcomes, and how to safely
 manage these situations whether it occur in a terminal, a port, or in transit
 on the waterway.
 But Aptamus cannot generate a successful training preparation plan alone; it
 must co-design with the appropriate Active Community Members to ensure
 all possibilities are explored and all potentially impacted stakeholders are
 incorporated into the response plans.

 Together, Aptamus and the appropriate ACMs will utilize the ExxonMobil
 itinerary as a starting point to develop the Tampa-specific training program.
 They will learn from instructors in the Texas A&M Engineering Extension
 Service. Aptamus and ACMs will incorporate local knowledge from regional
 experts and professional input from National Association of State Fire
 Marshalls into the training program and hold hands-on lessons.

Sample Initiatives: ▫ Develop a hands-on training course to prepare for and respond to potential
 CO2 emergencies
 ▫ Leverage ExxonMobil’s course itinerary and Mobile Training Trailer
 ▫ Provide training to first responders that operate near the OSG terminal and
 vessel’s pathway
 ▫ Trainees could include:
 ▫ Emergency medical technicians
 ▫ Emergency medicine doctors
 ▫ Firefighters
 ▫ Port emergency personnel
Tampa ACMs: ▫ Port Tampa Bay – Security & Safety Compliance Department
 ▫ St. Joseph's Hospital – BayCare
 ▫ Tampa Fire Rescue

Peer Program Example: HazMat Liquid & CO2 Pipeline and Industrial Fire Emergencies Training
Peer Organization: ExxonMobil Pipeline Co., Texas A&M Engineering Extension Service, National
 Association of State Fire Marshalls
Additional Info: exxonmobilpipeline.com/en/community/community-programs/first-
 responders

Share Maritime & Alternative Fuels Knowledge with Tomorrow’s Workforce
Program Goal: To generate awareness within the appropriate Tampa communities about
 employment opportunities with OSG/Aptamus and to provide clear
 information on the necessary skillsets and pathways to attain and maintain
 long-term employment.

Overview: TRICH and COAST20 will generate reliable, long-term employment in the
 Tampa region. But even before Aptamus breaks ground on the hub or begins
 vessel construction, members of the community can seek employment today
 at OSG and Port Tampa Bay. Unfortunately, this is not an option for many
 because they are either unaware of the opportunities, or they don’t know
 how to build the skillsets necessary for the jobs.

 Many US residents living near ports and waterways are unaware of the
 maritime industry’s impact on their daily lives and the variety of potential

                                         Page 156 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 career pathways in maritime. This knowledge disparity will be further
 exacerbated when companies implement alternative fuels and new
 technologies at the terminal, but local Tampa academic and training
 institutions haven’t included them in curricula. The roles may draw informed,
 skilled applicants from other regions and diminish locals’ employment
 prospects.
 OSG is proud to operate in Tampa and aims to share opportunities with as
 many of its fellow community members as possible. To achieve this, Aptamus
 will implement a plan to communicate to Tampa’s youth and mid-career
 workforce about the maritime industry, alternative fuels, and the many
 related career possibilities.
 Simply communicating the myriad potential jobs is insufficient. For those that
 have no knowledge of the industry nor local role models, these jobs may
 remain intangible if they don’t know how to attain the job. To be impactful,
 Aptamus must explain the methods and pathways to get from where one is
 today, to where one may want to be in the future. The presentations will
 address necessary education or certifications, available support networks
 such as labor unions, and physical or legal requirements.
 It is critical that the career pathway information is presented to the
 appropriate, eligible (or soon-to-be eligible) communities within Tampa. This
 program will seek to share knowledge with as many potential future
 employees. It will make best efforts to present to audiences that diverge from
 the typical maritime workforce profile (Caucasian male). Aptamus will
 collaborate with the ACMs to connect with as many and as diverse audiences
 as possible.
 The Aptamus teammates are not educators. They must collaborate with
 seasoned professionals that are experienced in effective instruction.
 Conversely, Aptamus may provide schools advice or guidance on high
 demand skills that should be incorporated into educational programs.
 To ensure that these efforts are successful, the information must be
 presented in an inclusive, accessible manner. Aptamus will coordinate with
 ACMs to customize messaging that resonates with the target audience. This
 may take shape across a variety of forms. For example, depending on the
 audience’s primary language, a presentation may need to be conveyed in a
 language other than English (Aptamus employees’ primary language).
 Perhaps presentations will need to be held outside of regular Aptamus office
 hours to accommodate the target audience’s current personal work
 schedules or home life responsibilities.
 Together, Aptamus and the appropriate ACMs will seek to expand the Tampa
 community’s awareness and understanding of maritime and to provide clear,
 attainable guidance to long-term employment at OSG.

Sample Initiatives: OSG, Aptamus employees regularly visit post-secondary schools to present:
 ▫ Alternative fuels

                                      Page 157 of 158

 T-RICH Pre-FEED Report – Public Version

 ▫ Skillsets needed to work with CO2
 ▫ Various employment opportunities at OSG Port Tampa Bay terminal
Tampa ACMs: Erwin Technical College
 University of South Florida
 - College of Marine Science’s Continuum
 - Institute on Black Life

 CONSTRUCTION SPECIFICATIONS INLET GRATE

 1. PLACE STABILIZED CONSTRUCTION ENTRANCE IN ACCORDANCE WITH THE APPROVED PLAN. VEHICLES MUST TRAVEL OVER THE ENTIRE
 LENGTH OF THE SCE. USE MINIMUM LENGTH OF 50 FEET (*30 FEET FOR SINGLE RESIDENCE LOT). USE MINIMUM WIDTH OF 10 FEET. FLARE
 SCE 10 FEET MINIMUM AT THE EXISTING ROAD TO PROVIDE A TURNING RADIUS.

 2. PIPE ALL SURFACE WATER FLOWING TO OR DIVERTED TOWARD THE SCE UNDER THE ENTRANCE, MAINTAINING POSITIVE DRAINAGE. WIRE TIES
 PROTECT PIPE INSTALLED THROUGH THE SCE WITH A MOUNTABLE BERM WITH 5:1 SLOPES AND A MINIMUM OF 12 INCHES OF STONE OVER
 THE PIPE. PROVIDE PIPE AS SPECIFIED ON APPROVED PLAN. WHEN THE SCE IS LOCATED AT A HIGH SPOT AND HAS NO DRAINAGE TO

 B A CONVEY, A PIPE IS NOT NECESSARY. A MOUNTABLE BERM IS REQUIRED WHEN SCE IS NOT LOCATED AT A HIGH SPOT.
 CROSS SECTION
 A B 3. PREPARE SUBGRADE AND PLACE NONWOVEN GEOTEXTILE, AS SPECIFIED IN SECTION H-1 MATERIALS.
 CONSTRUCTION SPECIFICATIONS
 4. PLACE CRUSHED AGGREGATE (2 TO 3 INCHES IN SIZE) OR EQUIVALENT RECYCLED CONCRETE (WITHOUT REBAR) AT LEAST 6 INCHES DEEP
 OVER THE LENGTH AND WIDTH OF THE SCE. 1. USE NONWOVEN GEOTEXTILE AS SPECIFIED IN SECTION H-1 MATERIALS.
 5. MAINTAIN ENTRANCE IN A CONDITION THAT MINIMIZES TRACKING OF SEDIMENT. ADD STONE OR MAKE OTHER REPAIRS AS CONDITIONS 2. LIFT GRATE AND WRAP WITH NONWOVEN GEOTEXTILE TO COMPLETELY COVER ALL OPENINGS. SECURE WITH WIRE TIES AND SET GRATE
 DEMAND TO MAINTAIN CLEAN SURFACE, MOUNTABLE BERM, AND SPECIFIED DIMENSIONS. IMMEDIATELY REMOVE STONE AND/OR SEDIMENT BACK IN PLACE.
 SPILLED, DROPPED, OR TRACKED ONTO ADJACENT ROADWAY BY VACUUMING, SCRAPING, AND/OR SWEEPING. WASHING ROADWAY TO
 B A

 REMOVE MUD TRACKED ONTO PAVEMENT IS NOT ACCEPTABLE UNLESS WASH WATER IS DIRECTED TO AN APPROVED SEDIMENT CONTROL
 B A 3. PLACE CLEAN 3 4 TO 11 2 INCH STONE OR EQUIVALENT RECYCLED CONCRETE 6 INCHES THICK ON THE GRATE.
 PRACTICE.
 4. STORM DRAIN INLET PROTECTION REQUIRES FREQUENT MAINTENANCE. REMOVE ACCUMULATED SEDIMENT AFTER EACH RAIN EVENT TO
 MAINTAIN FUNCTION AND AVOID PREMATURE CLOGGING. IF INLET PROTECTION DOES NOT COMPLETELY DRAIN WITHIN 24 HOURS AFTER A
 STORM EVENT, IT IS CLOGGED. WHEN THIS OCCURS, REMOVE ACCUMULATED SEDIMENT AND CLEAN, OR REPLACE GEOTEXTILE AND STONE.

APPENDIX C: HAZID RECOMMENDATIONS AND WORKSHEETS

Below (Table 6.1)is a summary of action items to be followed up on either before the end of Pre-FEED or in FEED based the assigned target completion dates. All
details for all actions are included in the HAZID Worksheet (8 Appendix A - HAZID Worksheet). All actions from all project risk workshops, assessments, and
analysis will be deposited into a SharePoint based Risk Register for all stakeholders to collaboratively review and develop their closure plans and commitments to
close-out. Risk Study Action Register.

 Hazard statements : Contains gas under pressure; may explode if heated.

SECTION 4. FIRST AID MEASURES

 Inhalation : Remove to fresh air. If breathing is irregular or stopped,
 administer artificial respiration. Use oxygen as required,
 provided a qualified operator is present. Call a physician.

 Skin contact : Rapid evaporation of the liquid may cause frostbite. If there is
 evidence of frostbite, bathe (do not rub) with lukewarm (not
 hot) water. If water is not available, cover with a clean, soft
 cloth or similar covering. Call a physician if irritation develops
 or persists.

 Eye contact : Rinse immediately with plenty of water, also under the eyelids,
 for at least 15 minutes. In case of frostbite water should be
 lukewarm, not hot. If symptoms persist, call a physician.

 Ingestion : Unlikely route of exposure. As this product is a gas, refer to the
 inhalation section. Do not induce vomiting without medical
 advice. Call a physician immediately.

 Notes to physician

 Indication of immediate : Treat frost-bitten areas as needed.
 medical attention and
 special treatment needed, if
 necessary

SECTION 5. FIREFIGHTING MEASURES

 Suitable extinguishing media : Use extinguishing measures that are appropriate to local
 circumstances and the surrounding environment.
 Water mist
 Dry powder
 Foam
 Carbon dioxide (CO2)

 Specific hazards during : Contents under pressure.
 firefighting Heating will cause pressure rise with risk of bursting
 Cool closed containers exposed to fire with water spray.
 Product is not combustible under normal conditions.
 Page 3 / 15

Version 1.8                               Revision Date 03/05/2019                      Print Date 11/10/2021

 However, this material can ignite when mixed with air under
 pressure and exposed to strong ignition sources.
 Do not allow run-off from fire fighting to enter drains or water
 courses.
 Vapours are heavier than air and can cause suffocation by
 reducing oxygen available for breathing.
 Some risk may be expected of corrosive and toxic
 decomposition products.
 Fire may cause evolution of:
 Hydrogen fluoride
 Carbon oxides
 Carbonyl halides
 Halogenated compounds

 Special protective equipment : In the event of fire and/or explosion do not breathe fumes.
 for firefighters Wear self-contained breathing apparatus and protective suit.
 No unprotected exposed skin areas.
 Exposure to decomposition products may be a hazard to
 health.

SECTION 6. ACCIDENTAL RELEASE MEASURES

 Personal precautions, : Immediately evacuate personnel to safe areas.
 protective equipment and Keep people away from and upwind of spill/leak.
 emergency procedures Wear personal protective equipment. Unprotected persons
 must be kept away.
 Remove all sources of ignition.
 Avoid skin contact with leaking liquid (danger of frostbite).
 Ventilate the area.
 After release, disperses into the air.
 Vapours are heavier than air and can cause suffocation by
 reducing oxygen available for breathing.
 Avoid accumulation of vapours in low areas.
 Unprotected personnel should not return until air has been
 tested and determined safe.

 Environmental precautions : Prevent further leakage or spillage if safe to do so.
 The product evapourates readily.
 Prevent spreading over a wide area (e.g. by containment or oil
 barriers).
 Page 4 / 15

Version 1.8                             Revision Date 03/05/2019                      Print Date 11/10/2021

 Methods and materials for : Do not direct water spray at the point of leakage.
 containment and cleaning Allow to evaporate.
 up

SECTION 7. HANDLING AND STORAGE
 Handling

 Precautions for safe : Handle with care.
 handling Avoid inhalation of vapour or mist.
 Do not get in eyes, on skin, or on clothing.
 Wear personal protective equipment.
 Pressurized container. Protect from sunlight and do not expose
 to temperatures exceeding 50 °C.
 Follow all standard safety precautions for handling and use of
 compressed gas cylinders.
 Use authorized cylinders only.
 Protect cylinders from physical damage.
 Do not puncture or drop cylinders, expose them to open flame
 or excessive heat.
 Do not pierce or burn, even after use. Do not spray on a naked
 flame or any incandescent material.
 Do not remove screw cap until immediately ready for use.
 Always replace cap after use.

 Advice on protection : Do not spray on a naked flame or any incandescent material.
 against fire and explosion Keep away from direct sunlight.
 Fire or intense heat may cause violent rupture of packages.
 Vapours may form explosive mixtures with air.
 The product is not easily combustible.

 Storage
 Conditions for safe storage, : Keep containers tightly closed in a cool, well-ventilated place.
 including any Keep away from direct sunlight.
 incompatibilities Protect cylinders from physical damage.
 Store away from incompatible substances.

 Further information on : Keep only in the original container at temperature not
 storage conditions exceeding 50°C

 Respiratory protection : In case of insufficient ventilation wear suitable respiratory
 equipment.
 Wear a positive-pressure supplied-air respirator.

MIDEL 7131 Synthetic Ester Transformer Fluid
 Fire safe and Biodegradable

Customer service: +44 (0)161 864 5422 or midelsales@mimaterials.com

 MIDEL 7131 – Synthetic Ester
 Transformer Fluid
 MIDEL protects life, property and the environment.
 It saves money while enabling innovation. It’s MIDEL.
 It’s safety inside.
 MIDEL is the acknowledged leading brand of ester transformer fluid. Since
 the 1970s MIDEL has been used in hundreds of thousands of installations
 worldwide, providing unrivalled fire safety, environmental protection, increased
 asset performance and real cost savings.

 MIDEL 7131 is a synthetic ester dielectric fluid designed to provide an
 alternative to mineral oil, silicone fluid and dry-type transformers.

 MIDEL 7131 has a high fire point, significantly increasing the fire safety of your
 transformers and reducing the need for fire protection equipment.

 MIDEL 7131 is readily biodegradable, avoiding environmental damage should
 leakage occur and enabling reductions in containment measures.

 MIDEL 7131, with its extremely low pour point of -56˚C, is a highly
 effective solution for colder climate installations.

 MIDEL 7131 has exceptionally high moisture tolerance. This characteristic
 enables the extension of cellulose insulation life.

 MIDEL 7131 is currently used in a wide range of transformer applications up to
 433kV. Perfectly suited for non free-breathing and free-breathing transformers
 (due to its excellent oxidation stability) located indoors or outside.

 MIDEL 7131 offers the ability to safely increase transformer loading or reduce
 transformer size.

MIDEL 7131 Transformer fires are a frequent occurrence in the world’s power networks. Such
IS PROVEN UP fires are notoriously unforgiving, spreading very quickly and causing extensive
 damage, sometimes involving loss of life. MIDEL 7131 offers a proven solution
TO 433kV in terms of fire risk mitigation. Specified and used across the globe, MIDEL 7131
 has a 100% fire safety record since its introduction in the 1970s.

 MIDEL’s engineers and chemists have built close working relationships with end
 users and the major transformer manufacturers. They are also active in IEEE,
 CIGRÉ and IEC working groups and regularly undertake extensive projects with
 industrial associations and academic bodies. Such depth of experience allows
 the MIDEL team to provide an unrivalled level of expert technical guidance.

 SECTION 4. First Aid Measures 4.1 Description of first aid measures
 General advice: Have the product container, label or Safety Data Sheet with you when
 calling the emergency number, or a physician.
 Inhalation: None envisaged due to the low vapour pressure of the substance.
 Skin: Wash with soap and water. Obtain medical attention if irritation develops.
 Eyes: Irrigate with copious amounts of water. Obtain medical attention if irritation
 develops.
 Ingestion: Do not induce vomiting, obtain medical attention.

 4.2 Most important symptoms and effects, both acute and delayed
 No adverse effects expected.

 4.3 Indication of any immediate medical attention and special treatment needed
 No special treatment required. Treat symptomatically. Do not induce vomiting.

 SECTION 5. Fire Fighting Measures 5.1 Extinguishing media
 Suitable extinguishing media: Carbon dioxide, dry powder, ABF foam or water fog.
 Unsuitable extinguishing media: Do not use water jets.

 5.2 Special hazards arising from the substance or mixture
 Specific hazards during firefighting: None.

 5.3 Advice for fire fighters
 Special protective equipment for firefighters: Wear full protective clothing and self-
 contained breathing apparatus.
 Further information: Cool closed containers exposed to fire with water spray.

 SECTION 6. Accidental Release 6.1 Personal precautions, protective equipment and emergency procedures
 Measures Personal precautions: Refer to protective measures listed in sections 7 and 8.
 Spilt product constitutes a slip hazard. Avoid contact with skin and eyes.

 6.2 Environmental precautions
 Environmental precautions: Do not contaminate any lakes, streams, ponds,
 groundwater, or soil. Avoid flushing into drains. In the event of a large spillage, contain
 the product as thoroughly as possible, and dispose of it in accordance with local
 regulations.

 6.3 Methods and material for containment and cleaning up
 Methods for cleaning up: Soak up spilt material with non-combustible
 absorbent material, (e.g. sand, earth, diatomaceous earth, vermiculite) and place in a
 container for disposal according to local / national regulations (see section 13). Clean
 contaminated surface thoroughly. Clean with detergents. Avoid solvents. Retain and
 dispose of contaminated water.

 6.4 Reference to other sections
 For disposal considerations see section 13.
 Refer to protective measures listed in sections 7 and 8.

 SECTION 7. Handling and Storage 7.1 Precautions for safe handling
 Advice on safe handling: Avoid eye and prolonged skin contact.
 For personal protection see section 8.

 7.2 Conditions for safe storage, including any incompatibilities
 Requirements for storage areas and containers: Keep containers tightly closed in a
 dry, cool and well-ventilated place. Keep out of the reach of children. Keep away from
 food, drink and animal feed.

 7.3 Specific end use(s)
 Exposure to air should be minimised. Opened containers should be properly resealed.
 For proper and safe use of this product, please refer to the label.

 Personal protective equipment:

 Respiratory protection: Not required for normal use.
 Skin protection: Wear coveralls.
 Hand protection: Wash hands after use. For prolonged or repeated skin contact gloves
 are recommended.
 Recommended gloves:
 Material: Nitrile rubber.
 Break through time: > 480 min.
 Glove thickness: 0.5 mm.
 Remarks: Gloves should be discarded and replaced if there is any indication of
 degradation or chemical breakthrough.
 Eye protection: If splashes are likely to occur wear safety glasses. Use equipment for
 eye protection tested and approved under appropriate government standards such as
 NIOSH (US) or EN 166(EU). Eye washes should be available for emergency use.

 SECTION 9. Physical and Chemical 9.1 Information on basic physical and chemical properties
 Properties Appearance: Pale amber liquid.
 Odour: Faintly sweet.
 pH: Not applicable.
 Freezing point: -56°C.
 Initial boiling point and boiling range: >300°C.
 Flash point: 260°C (closed cup).
 Flammability (solid, gas): Non-flammable.
 Upper/lower flammability or explosive limits: Data not available.
 Vapour pressure: <0.001Pa at 20°C.
 Vapour density: Not applicable.
 Relative density: 0.97 at 20°C.
 Water solubility: <1mg/l.
 Solubility: Not applicable.
 Partition coefficient: log Pow: >10.
 Auto-ignition temperature: No auto-ignition expected.
 Decomposition temperature: Data not available.
 Kinematic viscosity: 29mm2/s at 40°C.
 Explosive properties: Non-explosive.
 Oxidising properties: Non-oxidising.

 9.2 Other information
 Surface tension: No data available.
 Particle size: No data available.

 SECTION 11. Toxicological Information 11.1 Information on toxicological effects
 Likely routes of exposure: Skin and eyes are the most likely routes for exposure.
 Accidental ingestion may occur. Inhalation is not expected to be a relevant route of
 exposure.
 Product:
 Acute oral toxicity: Low toxicity: LD50 (Rat, female): >2000mg/kg, OECD 401.
 Acute dermal toxicity: Expected to be of low toxicity: LD50 (Rat, female):
                                                                                      >2000mg/kg, OECD 402.
 Acute inhalation toxicity: Low volatility makes inhalation unlikely. LD50 (Rat, female):
                                                                                      > 5.1 mg/L air.
 Skin corrosion/irritation: Not irritating, skin (Rabbit), OECD 404.
 Eye corrosion/irritation: Not irritating, eye (Rabbit), OECD 405.
 Respiratory or skin sensitisation: Not sensitising, skin (Guinea pig), OECD 406.
 Aspiration hazard: Not considered an aspiration hazard.
 Carcinogenicity/mutagenicity: Not considered a mutagenic hazard or carcinogen.
 This product is not considered to be a carcinogen by IARC, ACGIH, NTP or OSHA.

 SECTION 12. Ecological Information When used and/or disposed of as indicated, no adverse environmental effects are
 foreseen. Ecotoxicological effects based on knowledge of similar substances.

 12.1 Toxicity
 Product: Substances of this type do not pose a hazard to aquatic organisms.
 Toxicity to fish: Oncorhynchus mykiss (rainbow trout) LC50(96h), OECD 203: low
 acute toxicity, expected to be >1000 mg/L.
 Toxicity to aquatic invertebrates: Daphnia magna (Water flea) EC50: (48 h) >1000
 mg/L.
 Toxicity to aquatic plants: Desmodesmus subspicatus (green algae) EL50 >100 mg/L.

 12.2 Persistence and degradability
 Product: Readily biodegradable.

 12.3 Bioaccumulative potential
 No potential for bioaccumulation.

 12.4 Mobility in soil
 Product has low mobility in soil.

 The substance/mixture does not contain components considered to have endocrine

 regulation (EU) 2017/2100 or Commission Regulation (EU) 2018/605 at levels of 0.1%
 or higher.

 12.7 Other adverse effects
 No other adverse effects envisaged.

 SECTION 13. Disposal Considerations 13.1 Waste treatment methods
 Product: Do not contaminate any lakes, streams, ponds, groundwater or soil. Avoid
 flushing into drains.
 Product and packaging must be disposed of in accordance with local and national
 regulations.
 May be incinerated.
 Unused product may be returned for reclamation.
 Contaminated packaging: Empty remaining contents. Triple rinse containers. Empty
 containers should be taken to an approved waste handling site for recycling or disposal.
 Waste code: Uncleaned packaging code 15-01-02 - Empty packaging contaminated
 with residues of non-hazardous substances.
